#152. Climate loss and damage fund exists, but the money doesn't

Climate-vulnerable countries fought for years to create a loss and damage fund. Four years on, demand far outstrips funding, pledges are scarce, and disaster-hit nations are still waiting for support.

On the sandbars and river islands of Bangladesh's Brahmaputra and its branches, families have little to fall back on when the water rises.

"The floodwater swallows everything," said 16-year-old Rasel Babu, who had traveled to Antalya, Turkey, to speak to delegates at the UN climate conference.

Created after years of pressure from vulnerable nations, the loss and damage fund was designed to help countries recover from climate disasters and irreversible climate impacts. This includes extreme weather events such as floods, heat waves and wildfires as well as slow-onset impacts such as sea level rise, desertification and damage to infrastructure and crops.

But the fund is already struggling to meet demand. It received $2.8 billion (€2.56 billion) in urgent requests in a first call for funding, with only $342 million available at the time.

Even the money promised has not fully arrived. Only about half of the money currently pledged had been received by this summer.

While the European Commission signed a €25 million ($27 million) contribution agreement to the fund on the margins of COP31, formalizing an earlier pledge made at COP28, and Spain pledged an additional €15 million, other contributions to loss and damage remained scarce.

The fund's first emergency response is going to be paid out to Nepal. The board of the Fund for Responding to Loss and Damage had provisionally set aside $21.6 million following the catastrophic glacier collapse that triggered flash floods in August, killing more than 1,500 people with 5,600 more missing.

But the money has not moved yet. Nepal must first submit a funding request setting out how it will be spent.

"It's asking for a country that is dealing with the climate emergency to work on a proposal," Nepali climate finance expert Bibek Raj Kandel said, adding a layer of bureaucracy inefficient to address the urgency.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Nepal is already waiting on another request, filed after floods in Kathmandu and landslides in the eastern hills. "There's already one pending proposal sitting on the table," Kandel said. Unless the fund speeds up, he warned, it's "going to disappoint many, many countries."

Pakistan which led the push for the fund in 2022 after floods affected 33 million people and killed more than 1,730, also hasn't seen any of the money yet.

"Pakistan was one of the countries which really spoke up and managed to get the loss and damage fund on the climate agenda. But it takes a long time," Pakistan's Minister of State for Climate Change, Shezra Mansab Ali Khan Kharal, told DW.

"We have still not really got anything. We haven't seen anything on the ground at all as yet," Kharal said. Pakistan has submitted two projects and was told the cap is $200 million.

Other forms of aid often come in the form of loans.

"These promises, they are hollow promises," said Abid Qaiyum Suleri, Executive Director of the Sustainable Development Policy Institute (SDPI) in Islamabad, adding that for a heavily indebted country, the form of the money matters as much as the amount.

"We do not want that a climate disaster in Pakistan should translate into further debt for Pakistan," Kharal said, calling for grants rather than loans.

#151. Amazon and Meta reportedly in talks to lift blockade on AI agent Muse

Amazon may be having a change of heart about Meta’s AI agent, Muse. The two tech giants are reportedly discussing a potential deal that would allow Meta’s popular AI agent to connect directly to Amazon’s store.

The talks would mark a significant reversal from just two months ago, when Muse users discovered they were unable to purchase products on Amazon and instead received an error message when attempting to check out.

According to a Wednesday report by Fortune, citing people familiar with the discussions, Amazon and Meta have held multiple rounds of talks about the matter. Two key issues remain under negotiation: how search results would be presented and how advertising would be handled under a potential agreement.

One of Amazon’s biggest concerns is how third-party shopping agents like Muse could affect its advertising business. In October, Amazon CEO Andy Jassy argued that few customers were making purchases through outside shopping agents.

Amazon may also have been waiting for Muse to mature before agreeing to integrate it with its marketplace. If Muse places the wrong order or creates a problem with a purchase, Amazon could ultimately be left dealing with frustrated customers and unhappy sellers.

Fortune reported that executives are concerned that third-party shopping agents could weaken Amazon’s direct relationship with consumers, according to people familiar with the matter.

Any potential agreement between Amazon and Meta would reportedly require approval from both companies’ CEOs, Fortune also reported.

Meta and Amazon have not yet responded to TechCrunch’s requests for comment.

#149. Todoist confirms 6% of customers’ details were stolen in data breach

Task management app Todoist said on Tuesday that a hacker gained access to its production systems and stole customer data, including email IDs, password hashes, and account creation dates. The company said around 6% of its customers were affected and blamed stolen credentials for the breach.

Todoist said the attacker used stolen credentials to access the company’s version control system and source code between November 22 and 23. The hacker then changed employee access controls and reached the app’s cloud infrastructure via an employee’s work account.

The hacker downloaded credentials of early Todoist users whose passwords were stored using an older hashing method, but didn’t change any source code, the company said. Todoist said it has blocked the attack path and reset passwords as a mitigation step, as well as disabled the Todoist for Outlook email add-on.

For a small number of people, about 0.001% of its userbase, the hacker also searched through tasks, comments, and attachment names to look for credentials for other services, like API keys and access tokens.

The hacker also pulled Todoist API tokens and Slack integration tokens, and looked for emails, addresses, and attachment file names through the Todoist for Outlook extension if it was connected to accounts and had permission to read emails.

The company said it has sent a notice to those affected and asked them to reset their credentials and change their login details in other places with the same password. The company is asking users to also update API tokens, reconnect integrations, and revoke or replace any access keys, recovery phrases, or credentials they had stored in Todoist comments.

Todoist said it has also reset its internal access structure and how remote command execution works in its cloud environment to prevent such incidents in the future.

Todoist’s founder and CEO Amir Salihefendić apologized to users about the incident on X, and said the company will take a closer look at why it happened.

The to-do app’s data breach is the latest in a burgeoning list of data breaches in which hackers use stolen credentials to access data and systems at enterprises. So far this year, we’ve seen several companies fall victim to this form of attack, including Uber and Klue.

#148. Israeli military veterans, a backbone of protest movement, vow to keep demonstrating

In a sea of Israeli flags, Yiftach Golov holds one that looks a little different.

Among the hundreds of thousands of protesters who took to the streets for the 13th week in a row on Saturday, Golov hoists a brown flag that represents a group called “Brother and Sisters in Arms.”

They are veterans – many, like Golov, from elite forces – who now feel they are fighting on a new battlefield: To save Israeli democracy.

“We believe this is our responsibility to go once again called to the flag of the nation to stop this madness to defend Israel,” Golov said, as he weaved his way through the protesters on Tel Aviv’s Kaplan street, between the high-rises that house many of Israel’s high tech companies.

During the second intifada, in the early 2000s, Golov served in a special forces reconnaissance unit. He was never before particularly political, focusing more on getting his PhD in biophysics from Tel Aviv University.

But when the protest movement against the Israeli government’s judicial overhaul plan began in January, Golov attended one a demonstration and soon became one of thousands of veterans, and now military reservists, who have taken up the cause as their new mission.

Some, including elite Air Force reservists, have taken it a step further, threatening not to heed the call to train or even serve in protest of the government’s plans planned judicial changes, which would give the governing parties more control over Israel’s judiciary.

Others have taken to becoming some of the most active organizers and demonstrators. Last week, a group from Brothers and Sisters in Arms protested by carrying a figure wrapped in the Israeli flag on a stretcher, the way they would carry a wounded comrade off the field.

While Golov says he has not taken the drastic step to refuse service, he understands the motivation.

“We’re fighting for justice and liberty, just like the American story, that’s the values that that are being represented symbolized back when we look at our flag, that’s something that was lacking lacking for the last few decades. So basically, we reclaim the flag,” he said.

Palestinian taxi driver recounts surviving attack by right-wing protesters

Fellow members of the group, all wearing brown shirts with the organization’s logo, come up and say hello. They’re sprinkled all throughout the protests. One is even leading the “Pink Front,” a group of coordinated drummers who look like they are dressed for a rave, and often lead the chants at the protests.

They’re using skills they learned in the military – how to organize, how to mobilize – now for the protests. But more importantly, they say they have the same type of motivation.

“The very deep feeling that you are part of something bigger than yourself, that (you’re) allowed to sacrifice anything that is needed, whether it’s your career, health, seriously mental health,” Golov said. “We all have a mission, you’re willing to do it at any cost. You’re very determined, you know that you are on the right side, you’re carrying the torch of light. That keeps us being highly motivated despite the fact that we’re not sleeping for days.”

Israel’s protest movement is made up of many disparate groups, but the pressure from Israel’s much vaunted veterans has been seen as a key to moving the needle.

Last Monday, after weeks of sustained protests and the largest general strike in Israeli history, Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu announced a pause to the legislation, to allow time for negotiations with the opposition.

But despite the announcements, protesters are still out in the streets in large numbers. CNN affiliate Channel 12 in Israel estimated the size of Saturday’s demonstration in Tel Aviv at about 150,000 people. Organizers claimed it was 230,000.

Last week’s mass protests and widespread strike action came after Netanyahu said he had decided to fire Defense Minister Yoav Gallant for advocating a delay in passing the legislation – a move that Netanyahu has since delayed, sources told CNN, due to “the present security situation.”

In his televised speech calling for a delay, Gallant had said the pause in the legislation was needed “for the security of Israel,” citing the refusal of some Israel Defense Forces reservists to train in protest of the government plans. He said pressing ahead with the proposals could threaten Israel’s security.

Under pressure at home and from allies abroad, Netanyahu said he would delay votes on the remaining legislation until after the Knesset’s Passover recess in April “to give time for a real chance for a real debate.”

“Out of the responsibility to the nation, I decided to delay … the vote, in order to give time for discussion,” he added.

But Netanyahu indicated that the delay was only temporary. He insisted that the overhaul was necessary, and reiterated criticism of refusal to train or serve in the military in protest at the planned changes. “Refusing is the end of our country,” he said.

Many protesters don’t believe that the pause is real, or say it’s simply a stalling tactic to give Netanyahu some breathing room and get the protesters to go home before he plows on with the reforms.

“We will start doing deactivation only when we will know 100% that Israel state will stay a functional democratic country. Whatever needs to be done for that,” Golov said.

#146. Allstate suffers multi-day tech outage ahead of Thanksgiving

U.S. insurer Allstate has been experiencing a tech outage since Monday morning, leaving customers nationwide unable to access their accounts, review their policy information, file claims, pay their bills, and more, just ahead of the U.S. Thanksgiving holidays.

The outage has been impacting both the company’s website and mobile app, TechCrunch confirmed.

The breakdown follows a report from earlier this month that Allstate was planning to lay off up to 10% of its staff as part of an AI-driven efficiency push. (The company had not confirmed the report, which was attributed to unnamed sources.)

Allstate’s outage is the latest in a series of disruptions to services such as Microsoft Outlook and Teams, the airlines, Apple Pay, and several banks. Trump administration officials have said the outages are not being caused by cyberattacks, but are rather being caused by a recent set of upgrades to systems, many of which run on older technology. Those systems are now in need of more frequent security and bug fixes as AI makes it easier and faster to discover vulnerabilities.

At present, a message on Allstate’s website advises customers trying to log in that its services aren’t available, and that the company is “working to restore access as soon as possible.” On X, Allstate is employing what appear to be automated replies to customer complaints, asking them to DM the Allstate account with more information.

Downdetector, a crowdsourced outage-reporting website, shows users began reporting Allstate’s outage from Monday morning. However, the company hasn’t yet issued a formal statement about the disruption, what’s causing it, or when it may be resolved. In the meantime, local insurance agents have been fielding calls and emails from customers who aren’t able to use the company’s online services.

The timing of the outage is particularly bad given that Thanksgiving is a big travel holiday in the U.S., which means there will be car accidents. Customers will be left without a way to get in touch with their auto insurer to file claims and get roadside assistance through Allstate’s app.

Allstate did not immediately return a request for comment.

#144. WhatsApp now lets you schedule messages in group and individual chats

WhatsApp on Wednesday started rolling out a much-sought-after feature: the ability to schedule messages in both individual and group chats.

The feature is rolling out globally to all users across iOS and Android. Once it becomes available, you can type a message and long-press the send button to schedule it. You can choose to send a message anywhere between 10 minutes and 14 days later.

Users will be able to see all their scheduled messages and delete them if needed. It’s not clear if there is a way to edit the schedule of a message.

Notably, scheduled messages will be sent even if your phone is off or outside network coverage. You can schedule messages containing text, photos, and videos.

The feature could prove useful for wishing someone a happy birthday on time or chatting with people across time zones. Businesses may also find it easier to schedule texts and updates to their customers, and people who use the app to coordinate work will likely find themselves using scheduled texts to update their teams.

WhatsApp rivals like Telegram and Signal have had the ability to schedule messages for a while now, so the Meta-owned chat app is late in rolling it out.

#143. Shot in the face at 15: The lives shattered by Cameroon's decade of conflict

Median Bah was shot several times in the face by unknown gunmen

Median Bah was just 15 when her life changed forever.

She remembers the date vividly - 7 April 2018. She was ill and confined to her bed.

Armed men stormed her family's compound in Cameroon's Southwest region, one of the country's two English-speaking areas at the centre of a separatist conflict.

Median, now 23, says she was fast asleep when her brother woke her, panicked by the approach of a group of armed men who were shooting indiscriminately.

He escaped through a window. But for Median, fleeing was not so easy.

"Since I was sick, I had no strength to jump through the window with him, so I had to pass through the door," she tells the BBC.

That decision would have devastating consequences.

She says she came face to face with two of the attackers in the backyard, who opened fire at her.

They first shot her in the thigh before firing several more rounds, which severely damaged her face.

"I kept pleading with them not to kill me, that I wasn't feeling well. However, I found myself on the floor," she recalls.

Everything went dark. She lost consciousness and could neither see nor speak.

Median initially thought she was dreaming.

The attack left her with devastating facial injuries. Her jawbone was damaged, most of her teeth destroyed and much of her mouth severely injured.

The Anglophone crisis began in 2016, when English-speaking Cameroonians took up arms over alleged marginalisation by the French-speaking government (file photo)

Over the past eight years, she has undergone at least four reconstructive surgeries in Cameroon, Ghana and India, with more procedures expected next year.

Parts of her face have been reconstructed using tissue taken from other parts of her body.

She also lost the ability to breathe normally through her nose and mouth. She underwent a tracheostomy, a surgical procedure that creates an opening in the neck to allow air into the windpipe.

For eight years, she relied on a tube to breathe. It was only recently removed, allowing her to breathe normally again.

A teenager left with devastating facial injuries, a mother forced to flee her home and a student whose education was disrupted. Nearly a decade of fighting in Cameroon has left countless lives shattered.

The crisis began in October 2016, when lawyers and teachers in Cameroon's English-speaking regions went on strike over the alleged marginalisation of Anglophone communities by the Francophone-dominated government.

They were concerned about the appointment of French-speaking judges and teachers to courts and schools in the English-speaking Northwest and Southwest regions.

Initially, the protest was peaceful, with thousands demanding greater recognition of the English-speaking minority.

But human rights groups accused security forces of using excessive force to suppress the protests. The crackdown, critics say, helped fuel an armed separatist movement.

By 2017, a dozen armed groups had emerged, according to the International Crisis Group, seeking independence for the two English-speaking regions and the creation of a breakaway state known as Ambazonia.

Since then, separatist fighters have engaged in a prolonged and often brutal conflict with government forces.

Rights groups have documented serious abuses by both sides.

Separatist fighters have been accused of targeted killings, kidnappings for ransom, torture and attacks on schools and other public and private facilities.

Government forces, meanwhile, have faced allegations of arbitrary arrests and detention, torture and unlawful killings.

Both sides have denied the accusations, although in some instances they have acknowledged responsibility for abuses.

The violence has devastated communities and forced nearly half a million people from their homes, according to data published by the UN this year.

At least 6,000 people have been killed since the conflict began, according to Human Rights Watch.

The UN refugee agency, UNHCR, says more than 70,000 Cameroonians have sought refuge in neighbouring Nigeria.

Gloria Akom is among 15,000 people who fled Cameroon for Nigeria's Cross River state

Glory Akom, 44, lives with a disability after surviving severe burns in an incident unrelated to the conflict.

The mother-of-two now lives in a camp in Ogoja, a town in Nigeria's south-eastern Cross River state, near the Cameroonian border.

Ogoja hosts about 15,000 refugees across three settlements, Richard Effiong, from the UNHCR, told the BBC.

The UNHCR has provided refugees with brick houses topped with tin roofs.

For many, however, uncertainty remains over when, or whether, they will ever return home.

Glory says her life in Cameroon was far better. She ran a business and was able to provide for her family.

But everything changed when fighting intensified in 2017.

She said soldiers deployed to her area in the Southwest region pursued residents into the bush, forcing them to abandon their homes and flee into Nigeria.

"We were sleeping in the bush, as we were always running," she said.

"If you slept in your house, they could kill you there."

Initially, she settled in a neighbouring village before moving to Ogoja, where she was eventually allocated a permanent home.

Like many other refugees, Glory depends on $67 (Â£51) monthly cash assistance from UNHCR to support her children, one of who suffers from sickle cell and requires regular medical attention.

The money is barely enough to cover food, medical bills and other necessities, she says.

Her sadness at having to leave home is shared by other Cameroonian refugees in Ogoja who spoke to the BBC.

Maurine Mangha, 21, said she and five other members of her family fled Akwaya in the Southwest region after separatist fighters opened fire during celebrations marking what they described as their independence on 1 October 2017.

"They shot a lot of people, and we had no choice but run into the bush," she tells the BBC.

Education in Cameroon's Southwest region has been severely disrupted by the conflict (file photo)

Maurine had stopped attending school months earlier because of a separatist-imposed education boycott.

The 2017 boycott campaign, which sought to pressure the government, involved threats and attacks against schools and those who defied orders to stay away.

The consequences for children have been severe.

More than 200,000 children are currently out of school in the conflict regions, while nearly 2,500 learning institutions remain non-operational in the two regions, according to the UN's children's agency, Unicef, citing Cameroonian education authorities.

Despite the disruption, Maurine managed to secure a scholarship through a higher education programme for refugees managed by the UNHCR.

She is now studying microbiology at a local polytechnic.

But Maurine says she cannot help thinking about friends who remained in Cameroon.

"I feel very bad for my friends as they did not have the opportunity [to further] their education because of the crisis," she says.

Maurine Mangha has managed to get a scholarship to continue her education

After nearly a decade of fighting, the Cameroonian government wants the separatists to abandon their armed struggle.

In September, the authorities launched a community dialogue initiative aimed at finding grassroots solutions to the conflict.

"It is you, all of us sitting here, who have the capacity today to organise to talk to those boys [separatists], to tell them, the time has come for them to leave," Prime Minister Joseph Dion Ngute said during the launch in Buea, capital of the Southwest region.

He repeated a similar message in Bamenda, the main city in the Northwest region and one of the epicentres of the conflict.

At both events, nearly 60 people described as former separatist fighters were presented as having abandoned the armed struggle and surrendered to the state.

A similar initiative in 2019 failed to end the fighting.

As of November 2026, almost 1,500 former separatist fighters were undergoing rehabilitation at Disarmament, Demobilisation and Reintegration (DDR) centres in the two English-speaking regions, a Cameroonian official from the agency overseeing the programme told the BBC.

"A lot of Amba boys [separatist fighters] have been killed, others have surrendered, and many of them have abandoned the ideology of independence," says Clovis Naseri, a former separatist commander.

President Paul Biya, who has been in power since 1982, has faced criticism over his government's military crackdown on Anglophone separatists

Clovis spent about two years with the White Tigers of Ribbons, an armed group operating in the Southwest region, before abandoning the group in 2019.

He was subsequently admitted to a reintegration centre, where he underwent rehabilitation for several years.

He is now a vocal critic of the separatist movement and advocates peace and national unity.

Despite defections and government efforts to encourage fighters to surrender, separatist groups continue to carry out attacks across the two regions.

Some analysts argue that the conflict has persisted for so long partly because of the government's failure to address the grievances that triggered it.

Roland Fru, a researcher on the Anglophone crisis, says the authorities are yet to demonstrate a clear understanding of the issues driving the conflict.

"I've not heard even once, the government explain things in such a way that helps me understand that they even understand the problem," he says.

The BBC contacted several government officials for comment but did not receive a response.

Cameroonian authorities have frequently rejected criticism of their handling of the crisis.

Last November, President Paul Biya said the reconstruction efforts, supported by international partners, were helping restore normality in the affected regions.

"These efforts will continue steadily," he said during his swearing-in ceremony, while praising security forces for their "effective actions" and "professionalism" in improving the situation.

For survivors such as Median, Glory and Maurine, however, lasting peace may remain elusive until the fighting comes to a permanent end.

As they try to rebuild their lives and hold on to hope, Median, who still lives in Cameroon, says she remains grateful to have survived.

"I have defeated death," she says confidently. "I don't think there's anything that can stop me."

#141. Houthis claim attacks on Saudi refinery, financial district

Iran-backed Yemeni Houthis say they carried out fresh retaliatory attacks in Saudi Arabia. A fire was reported at Ras Tanura oil refinery and Riyadh's financial district was attacked for the second time in a week.

Iran-backed Houthi rebels in Yemen say they are continuing their attacks on Saudi Arabia, claiming to have damaged a key oil refinery as well as the petromonarchy's financial district in the capital Riyadh.

Houthi military spokesman Yahya Saree on Wednesday said that "accurate and direct" attacks targeting Ras Tanura — Saudi Arabia's largest oil refinery and a major export terminal for crude — had "caused fires to break out at the facility."

Saree said an attack on Saudi Arabia's financial district, located in the capital Riyadh, was "carried out in three phases, with strikes hitting their targets accurately."

Saudi defense officials said that air defenses in the kingdom had taken down four Houthi drones at Ras Tanura and that 11 people had been injured when a tower in the financial district was hit.

The building in question was also targeted on November 19, but no one was injured in that attack.

The Houthis and Saudis have been fighting for over a decade as part of a civil war that has become a regional conflict with increased significance as a result of the ongoing conflict in Iran and the Strait of Hormuz.

#139. X’s latest update for creators focuses on vertical video

X announced new creation tools Wednesday, many of them focused on short-form vertical video. The updates are part of the platform’s effort to compete with social networks such as YouTube and Instagram as the format continues to grow in popularity.

The tools are designed to help creators make professional-quality vertical videos, share original content more easily, and earn more. They also give creators more ways to record, edit, and publish content directly on the platform without relying on third-party apps.

A key addition is an upgraded media editor that lets creators produce polished vertical videos without leaving X. Recording features include the ability to capture video in segments and use green-screen effects, the company said.

The editor also offers a range of postproduction tools. Creators can convert horizontal videos to vertical format, automatically generate captions, trim footage, add text overlays, choose custom thumbnails, and adjust photos before publishing.

Thanks to a recently announced update, users can now add up to 20 photos and videos to a single post, which audiences can swipe through horizontally. X says the feature is intended for sharing event recaps, behind-the-scenes moments, and product launches.

Availability of the new features varies by region, X said.

Earlier this month, the company introduced a clipping feature that lets users take snippets from longer videos, add their own commentary, and post the clips without leaving X. Clips credit and link to the original video, plus the person who creates a clip and the original poster can earn from its views.

Another feature, “React with Video,” launched earlier this year. Similar to TikTok’s “stitch” feature, this lets creators respond to posts with video commentary while displaying the original post in the background.

X has also introduced new earning opportunities and updates to its creator monetization programs in recent months. These include the “Video Kickstart Bonus,” an invitation-only pilot that rewards selected creators for publishing original vertical videos under three minutes long and without watermarks.

#137. Fact check: Deepfakes and 'US abandons Taiwan' claims spread ahead of vote

Ahead of local elections, Taiwan is seeing a surge in AI-generated attacks on mayoral candidate Puma Shen, alongside disinformation portraying the US as abandoning the island.

On November 28, Taiwan will hold local elections that could reshape power dynamics between the sovereignty-leaning Democratic Progressive Party (DPP) and the Beijing-friendly Kuomintang (KMT). The outcome could influence relations between Taiwan and China, as well as regional geopolitics and ties between Washington, Beijing and Taipei.

A month before the election, there was a spike in AI-generated content featuring DPP Taipei mayoral candidate Puma Shen. Claims that the US is abandoning Taiwan also increased during the same period. Experts say that fake quotes and misleading clips are being used as part of a campaign to weaken trust in US security commitments.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The V-Dem Institute at the University of Gothenburg ranked Taiwan "the country most severely targeted" by foreign disinformation for eleven consecutive years – from 2014 to 2024.

The Taiwanese Ministry of the Interior has stated that foreign actors are increasingly using diverse methods to interfere in elections. That includes manipulating election betting, inviting Taiwanese citizens to China, using donations from Taiwanese business people to organize events, such as banquets, and leveraging temple and religious activities, to mobilize and win over supporters. Taiwan has functioned with de facto independence since 1949 despite Beijing's decadeslong claim to the island as a province and repeated vows to annex it.

DW Fact Check examined viral claims that have circulated online since September during the run-up to the election.

Claim: Puma Shen takes drug and receives kickbacks through arms purchases from the US.

An audio clip posted on Facebook on October 29, claims to show Puma Shen, the DPP candidate for Taipei mayor, admitting to receiving kickbacks from US arms deals.

The recording is heavy with background noise – a method used to mask synthetic manipulation in AI-generated audio. Multiple deepfake detection platforms, including Is this AI and Undetectable, flagged the audio as likely AI-generated.

The Facebook account that posted the audio was created on October 29, 2026 – the date the audio was also published. The account's followers are mainly profiles using languages, such as English, Arabic and Thai.  That points to possible bot activity.

Another Facebook account posted an image of Puma Shen taking drugs. Apart from the "AI content" label on the post, multiple AI detector tools identified this photo as likely AI-generated.

The account was created in March 2026. Since then, a lot of AI-generated content mocking Taiwan's President Lai Ching-te and his pro-sovereignty party has been posted. It is occasionally interspersed with posts praising the development of Chinese cities.

This account's profile indicates that the primary country location for people who manage this page includes China and Taiwan.

Taiwanese independent media platform Watchout investigated the account's advertising activity and found that it had previously run at least five Facebook ads. The advertisers were identified as Peng Zhiwei and Guo Yang, both based in China. The findings suggest that the account received financial support from individuals in China.

The Taipei mayoral race is a key battleground in Taiwan's upcoming local elections. As of mid-November, polls showed a neck-and-neck contest between Puma Shen and his opponent, Chiang Wan-an, who claims to be a great-grandson of Taiwanese leader Chiang Kai-shek.

Chiang Kai-shek was a leading figure of the Kuomintang and relocated with the party to Taiwan in 1949 after losing the Chinese Civil War to the Chinese Communist Party.

While the Kuomintang was historically staunchly anti-communist, critics argue that it now promotes closer ties with China. For example, they point to a senior Kuomintang figure's proposed 10-year unification framework as evidence that its stance runs counter to mainstream public opinion in Taiwan. Although the Kuomintang subsequently stated that this framework is entirely unrelated to the current party leadership, it has sparked controversy in Taiwan.

Claim: A TikTok video claims to show US president Donald Trump saying the US is abandoning Taiwan.

The original video was from a Fox News interview with Trump on May 16. The interviewer did not ask whether the United States would defend Taiwan, and the US president did not comment on the issue.

Trump said he did not want Taiwan to become independent or for a war to break out, adding that the status quo should be maintained to allow tensions to de-escalate. He also clearly stated that his policy toward Taiwan "has not changed." Washington has long maintained a policy of strategic ambiguity toward Taiwan– neither explicitly guaranteeing the island's defense nor ruling out the possibility of doing so. Correction from November 25, 2026: A previous version of this article (published on November 25, 2025) included an example referring to headlines on search engines claiming that US troops were withdrawing from Taiwan. This has been removed because while they were sensational, they were not entirely wrong because they referred to a report by a thinktank. We apologize for the error.

#136. Clashing political visions reflect a divided Spain ahead of election

Pedro SÃ¡nchez has won praise from the left for standing up to Donald Trump and promoting progressive causes

Speaking in a wrestling ring on the island of Tenerife as he campaigned ahead of Sunday's general election, Spain's Socialist Prime Minister Pedro SÃ¡nchez cautioned those listening about the spread of right-wing governments.

"What good has President [Donald] Trump done the United States and, by extension, the world - was he a change or a step backwards?" he asked supporters, before invoking the administrations of Brazil's former leader, Jair Bolsonaro, and Argentinian President Javier Milei.

"If we don't want that for the world, why would we want it for our own country?" said SÃ¡nchez.

He issued his warning as Spain's conservative People's Party (PP) leads most of the polls and appears poised to form a governing coalition with the hard-right Vox. The election comes amid a deeply polarised political atmosphere in which both left and right have warned about their opponents' ability to undermine democracy.

In a Europe dominated by right-leaning governments, SÃ¡nchez has gained a reputation as the continent's most outspoken and uncompromising left-wing prime minister.

His strident rhetoric defending feminism, immigration and green energy, along with his condemnation of Israel's military action in Gaza and verbal clashes with Trump, has fostered this image.

Having overseen consistent economic growth in recent years, his stock is high among the international left.

Yet in Spain, SÃ¡nchez has become a hate figure for the right, who see him as an autocrat with little regard for the separation of state powers, obsessed with clinging on to power and surrounded by a corrupt cabal.

Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o's right-wing People's Party (PP) is currently ahead in the polls

In a bullring in Valencia, the same day that SÃ¡nchez was staging his rally, PP leader Alberto NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o told supporters that the last few years of Socialist-led government had been "insulting and even humiliating for a lot of people". He added: "We know them, they are capable of anything."

A couple of days earlier, Ignacio Garriga, spokesman for Vox, had gone further, describing SÃ¡nchez as "a very dangerous psychopath".

Both the PP and Vox have cast doubt on the neutrality of postal votes and votes cast by Spaniards abroad ahead of the election, suggesting that government manipulation could sway them in its favour.

"This is a campaign that is overwhelmingly ideological," said Cristina Monge, a political scientist at the Complutense University. "That means that it is not so much about voters rewarding or punishing a government's performance or the proposals being made, but about belonging to one or other ideological bloc."

Much of the hostility to SÃ¡nchez stems from his reliance on hard-left and separatist parties.

Ahead of the 2023 general election, he faced fierce criticism from the right for his reliance on the pro-independence EH Bildu coalition, which had links to the defunct Basque armed group ETA.

After that vote, he was able to form a new administration with the support of another separatist party, Together for Catalonia (Junts), in exchange for an amnesty for all Catalans facing legal action for their role in a failed 2017 bid for independence.

At the time, NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o accused SÃ¡nchez of being "a prime minister who has bought his investiture in exchange for the judicial impunity of his partners".

Guillermo FernÃ¡ndez VÃ¡zquez, a political scientist at Carlos III University, said those parliamentary partnerships "fed the rhetoric of the right and far right, which was that Pedro SÃ¡nchez is an amoral person without scruples â someone whose word can't be trusted and who just wants to remain in power".

This legislation has been one of turmoil for SÃ¡nchez, during which he has been unable to push a single budget through parliament.

Shock of rail tragedy gives way to sorrow and outrage in politically divided Spain

In addition, the prime minister has been corralled by a litany of judicial investigations. He has acknowledged errors of judgement in trusting two close former associates in the Socialist Party, JosÃ© Luis Ãbalos, who has been given a 24-year-jail sentence for corruption-related crimes, and Santos CerdÃ¡n, who is due to go on trial on similar charges.

By contrast, he has defended his former attorney general, Ãlvaro GarcÃ­a Ortiz, who was barred from public office for revealing confidential information, and his brother, David, who was barred from office for nine years after being found guilty of irregularities in taking on a post as a regional arts director.

In the most controversial case, SÃ¡nchez's wife, BegoÃ±a GÃ³mez, is due to go on trial for influence peddling.

She denies wrongdoing and SÃ¡nchez has said the cases involving GarcÃ­a Ortiz, his brother and wife are "nasty politics, not corruption", blaming them on efforts by his political opponents to use the judiciary to destabilise his government.

Politicians on the right draw no such distinction between the various cases, and Vox leader Santiago Abascal describes the government as "a criminal mafia".

MarÃ­a PÃ©rez, a young office worker who has no political allegiance, says she is going to vote for the PP, partly because of her concerns about the country's housing crisis and the rail system, which has seen two major tragedies this year, but above all due to the judicial investigations.

"I think that SÃ¡nchez has lost credibility with all the corruption cases," she says. "It seems that everyone in government is involvedâ¦ so I'm not sure I want them to continue governing. I believe it's time for a change."

Santiago Abascal and his far-right Vox party could enter government for the first time after Sunday's election

Allegations that SÃ¡nchez has co-opted state institutions, such as the CIS national research centre, which publishes opinion polls, and national broadcaster RTVE, have only compounded the right's view of him as a populist who has scant regard for the rule of law.

However, with the far right seemingly set to enter government for the first time in Spain's democratic era, claims of demagoguery cut both ways.

Politicians on the left say that coalition administrations formed by the PP and Vox in four of Spain's 17 regions are a blueprint for socially regressive policies they plan to implement on a national level after the election.

In all four of those regions, Vox has negotiated the introduction of "national priority", which seeks to give Spaniards priority access to public services and subsidies ahead of foreigners.

The Castilla y LeÃ³n region has also seen culture-related controversies driven by Vox. Most recently, a play about left-wing poet and dramatist Federico GarcÃ­a Lorca, who was killed by right-wing rebels during the 1936-39 civil war, was cancelled in the region's capital, Valladolid, apparently at the behest of the Vox-controlled local culture ministry.

Last month the same department published new guidelines governing the awarding of subsidies for films, stating that projects involving what it called "ideological indoctrination" would not be eligible. Spain's culture minister, Ernest Urtasun, described this as "an intolerable attempt at censorship".

Susana Fuertes, a schoolteacher who will vote for the left on Sunday, said such moves are a sinister throwback to the dictatorship of Francisco Franco.

"These political messages take us back to the Franco era by talking about getting rid of basic civil rights regarding women, gender equality and immigration," she said. "All of this has been said in such an aggressive tone, with insults and lack of respect for people who don't share the same ideas."

"These political messages take us back to the Franco era," Susana Fuertes says

Critics of NÃºÃ±ez FeijÃ³o argue that he has already shown signs of taking on Vox's agenda by hardening his rhetoric on immigration and promising to roll back a law allowing euthanasia and eliminate the equality ministry, which manages matters related to gender, sexuality and ethnicity.

Spain's deep left-right schism has also been visible beyond the political arena.

Hooded anti-fascist youths have clashed with police on a number of occasions on the campaign trail, such as when they demonstrated against an appearance by Vox's Abascal in A CoruÃ±a.

An arguably more visible phenomenon has been far-right youths. In one incident, several dozen of them raised their arms in the fascist salute and sang the pro-Franco anthem Cara al Sol (Facing the sun) while facing off against housing protesters in Madrid.

A 2025 CIS poll found that around one in five under-25s had a positive view of the dictatorship.

#134. The rising cost of putting food on the table in India

As food inflation rises, vegetables are becoming an increasingly expensive part of the household budget

India's inflation is rising again. And the biggest worry is what's happening to food prices.

The latest government data, external shows retail inflation climbed to 6.09% in October, up from 5.54% in September.

But look more closely at food inflation. It has surged to 8.94%.

The squeeze was sharper in rural India, where food inflation was 9.09%, compared with 8.68% in urban areas.

In other words, food prices are rising considerably faster than the overall rate of inflation.

Food inflation this year has been driven by a combination of weather disruptions, supply shortages and rising input costs, say economists.

With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, external, India remains acutely vulnerable to the vagaries of the monsoon. This year, the southwest monsoon was 13% below normal and unevenly distributed, while intensifying El NiÃ±o conditions have heightened risks to farm production.

Onion prices have surged, adding to the pressure on household food budgets

A survey by polling agency LocalCircles found that six in 10 consumers say household essentials cost them over 20% more each month than a year ago, prompting calls for government intervention.

Rising prices are also forcing cutbacks: 74% are curbing discretionary spending on eating out, shopping and entertainment, while half are buying smaller quantities of essentials.

Food accounts for a smaller share of Indian household spending than it once did, but remains the biggest driver of inflation, with significant economic, social and political consequences.

In India's latest consumer price index, food and beverages account for 36.75% of the basket of goods and services used to measure inflation.

That is down sharply from 45.86% under the previous series, reflecting changing consumption patterns as Indians spend more on services, transport, healthcare, education and other non-food items.

Yet a sharp increase in food prices can move the overall inflation rate significantly, while disproportionately affecting people whose incomes leave little room for adjustment.

"Adjustability for a poor country like India is significantly lower," says Rajeswari Sengupta, an associate professor of economics at Mumbai-based Indira Gandhi Institute of Development Research, pointing to the fact that a substantial proportion of household income is still spent on food.

Rising food prices hurt the poor the most

According to calculations by Dharmakirti Joshi, chief economist at analytics firm Crisil, the bottom 20% of rural households faced inflation of 6.9% in October, against 6.5% for the richest 20%.

In urban India, the corresponding rates were 6.1% and 5.3%.

The pressure is visible even in the cost of a simple meal.

Crisil's Roti Rice Rate analysis, external found that the cost of a home-cooked vegetarian thali - a meal platter - rose 12% year-on-year in October, driven by higher prices of onions, tomatoes, vegetables, edible oil, rice and cooking gas. A non-vegetarian thali became 9% more expensive.

Rice prices were up about 10% year-on-year, pulses 8%, edible oil 13% and LPG 10%, according to Pushan Sharma, director of intelligence at Crisil. "It's no longer a few commodities that are driving this increase, it's becoming a little more broad-based," he says.

When onion prices spike, households can cut back on their consumption to some extent. But substituting rice, pulses and cooking oil is considerably harder.

And food inflation is not just about what farmers produce. It is also about how efficiently that food is stored, processed and moved to the market. Weaknesses at each stage can turn seasonal shortages into sharp price spikes - and gluts into waste.

Onionomics: Peeling away the layers of India's food economy

Onions, for instance, are particularly susceptible to seasonal price swings.

The rabi crop - winter crops, sown after the monsoon and harvested in spring - which accounts for roughly two-thirds of onion production, is harvested in March and April and stored for several months. As stocks dwindle, prices can rise sharply before the next crop arrives.

Tomatoes, meanwhile, tend to see price spikes during the summer months, when arrivals decline in some markets or heat stress affects production.

Sharma says perishables are often responsible for the sudden spikes in food inflation, while prices of staples such as rice and wheat tend to rise more gradually but can stay higher for longer.

"The perishables are what caused the volatility. The non-perishables, like rice and wheat, are the ones that structurally increase the cost," he says.

Economists say India still lacks adequate infrastructure to sort, grade, store and process much of its perishable produce.

Tomatoes, for example, can become so abundant during a glut that farmers struggle to sell them at remunerative prices, only for consumers to face sharply higher prices months later.

With nearly 60% of its farmland dependent on rainfall, India remains vulnerable to erratic monsoons

Better storage and processing could help turn some of that surplus into products such as puree, smoothing the extremes between abundance and scarcity, according to Sharma

Energy shocks feed into the cost of transport, irrigation and agricultural inputs. Fertiliser prices can rise. Global edible-oil markets matter because India relies heavily on imports.

To be true, India, however, has some protection against the worst effects of food inflation.

Government stocks of cereals are comfortably above prescribed minimum levels, and the free-food programme covering nearly 800 million people offers a cushion against rising prices. Rice stocks, external are particularly ample.

There is a wider economic risk, says Sengupta.

"Food inflation may begin with a supply shock, but its effects can spread. Workers who find that their wages no longer cover basic living costs may seek higher pay. Businesses facing higher labour costs may raise prices. What begins as a rise in the price of food can gradually become a more general inflationary pressure - the familiar wage-price spiral that central banks try to prevent," she says.

For India, this creates a particularly difficult balancing act.

Agriculture is a relatively low-productivity sector, and the government has long sought to protect farmers by setting minimum support prices (MSPs) for certain crops - a price floor at which it promises to buy produce, helping farmers guard against a market crash.

At the same time, food prices are politically sensitive. Sharp increases in the price of staples such as onions and tomatoes can quickly anger consumers, prompting governments to step in with measures such as restricting exports, limiting how much traders can stockpile, or otherwise trying to bring prices down.

The broader story of India's changing food consumption makes the current inflation more interesting.

Households are eating differently from a decade ago. An analysis, external of the 2011-12 and 2023-24 consumption surveys shows that the share of cereals in food spending has fallen sharply, particularly among the poorest households, reflecting the impact of subsidised food programmes.

Meanwhile, consumption of nutrient-rich foods such as dairy, fruit and meat has increased across income groups, with the biggest gains among the poorest, the study revealed.

India's food basket is becoming more diverse. But diversity is meaningful only if ordinary Indians can afford it. Ultimately, food inflation is about what makes it to the plate - and what has to be left out.

#133. Character.AI’s plan to auction off users’ private chats challenged by state AGs

What happens to your private conversations when an AI company goes bankrupt? If it’s Character.ai, apparently, the plan is to auction them off to the highest bidder.

Now, a coalition of 42 state attorneys general is intervening in Character.ai’s bankruptcy proceedings in an effort to stop the chatbot maker from selling off sensitive user data, which includes the private conversations of millions of users.

The attorneys general involved in the matter have put out individual press releases, claiming that Character.ai’s dataset includes chat histories from more than 21 million U.S. accounts, more than 2.1 million of which belong to minors. In addition, the sale would involve other personal data, like IP addresses, the statements said.

Only one anonymous bidder for the chat data has been disclosed so far: Smirks and Pangs Acquisition LLC (Smirks), which was set up as a Delaware company on November 13, 2026. The following day, Character.ai picked it as the base bid for the auction, scheduled for December 1, California Attorney General Rob Bonta noted. It is unclear what the buyer’s plans are for the chats.

In addition, some states, including California, Colorado, New York, and Connecticut, have enacted chatbot safety legislation that will go into effect on January 1, 2027, which could trigger further violations if the sale goes through.

“Character.AI promised consumers that their data would not be sold. This kind of behavior is illegal under federal and California law and bankruptcy should not be used to sidestep these consumer protections,” Bonta said in a statement.

Connecticut Attorney General William Tong also criticized Character.ai’s business model.

“Their chatbots drove vulnerable youth down a rabbit hole of emotionally volatile and sexually inappropriate chats,” Tong said. “Their reckless misconduct and broken business model drove their company into the ground. Now they want to abuse the bankruptcy process to do exactly what they said they wouldn’t do — auction off those private chat records and personal details to a shady buyer.”

Tong added: “We objected to the Disney sale, and we’re calling on the bankruptcy court to reject this sale too as yet another guise to eviscerate legal liability and consumer protection.”

The opposition to the Disney sale was led by a similar but not identical coalition of state AGs. They fought against a proposed $20 million licensing deal that would give Disney access to Character.ai’s intellectual property, but not to users’ chats. The deal has not yet received final court approval. (Disney also agreed to hire most of Character.ai’s remaining staff.)

Character.ai suffered after its co-founders, Noam Shazeer and Daniel De Freitas, returned to Google, when the tech giant signed a non-exclusive license to use the AI startup’s tech. The company also faced lawsuits related to child safety — one involved the suicide of a 14-year-old Florida boy whose mother claimed he had become obsessed with a chatbot on the platform.

California AG Bonta is joined in the effort to oppose the sale of Character.ai user data by attorneys general from Alaska, Arkansas, Colorado, Connecticut, the District of Columbia, Delaware, Florida, Georgia, Hawaii, Idaho, Illinois, Indiana, Kansas, Kentucky, Louisiana, Maine, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Mississippi, Missouri, Nevada, New Hampshire, New Jersey, New York, North Carolina, North Dakota, Ohio, Oklahoma, Oregon, Pennsylvania, Rhode Island, South Carolina, South Dakota, Tennessee, Utah, Virginia, Vermont, Washington, Wisconsin, and West Virginia.

#132. A new approach to a Covid-19 nasal vaccine shows early promise

Scientists in Germany say they’ve been able to make a nasal vaccine that can shut down a Covid-19 infection in the nose and throat, where the virus gets its first foothold in the body.

In experiments in hamsters, two doses of the vaccine – which is made with a live but weakened form of the coronavirus that causes Covid-19 – blocked the virus from copying itself in the animals’ upper airways, achieving “sterilizing immunity” and preventing illness, a long-sought goal of the pandemic.

WHO experts revise Covid-19 vaccine advice, say healthy kids and teens low risk

Although this vaccine has several more hurdles to clear before it gets to a doctor’s office or drug store, other nasal vaccines are in use or are nearing the finish line in clinical trials.

China and India both rolled out vaccines given through the nasal tissues last fall, though it’s not clear how well they may be working. Studies on the effectiveness of these vaccines have yet to be published, leaving much of the world to wonder whether this approach to protection really works in people.

The US has reached something of a stalemate with Covid-19. Even with the darkest days of the pandemic behind us, hundreds of Americans are still dying daily as the infection continues to simmer in the background of our return to normal life.

As long as the virus continues to spread among people and animals, there’s always the potential for it mutate into a more contagious or more damaging version of itself. And while Covid infections have become manageable for most healthy people, they may still pose a danger to vulnerable groups such as the elderly and immunocompromised.

Researchers hope next-generation Covid-19 vaccines, which aim to shut down the virus before it ever gets a chance to make us sick and ultimately prevent the spread of infection, could make our newest resident respiratory infection less of a threat.

China approves its first mRNA Covid-19 vaccine

One way scientists are trying to do that is by boosting mucosal immunity, beefing up immune defenses in the tissues that line the upper airways, right where the virus would land and begin to infect our cells.

It’s a bit like stationing firefighters underneath the smoke alarm in your house, says study author Emanuel Wyler, a scientist at the Max Delbruck Center for Molecular Medicine in the Helmholtz Association in Berlin.

The immunity that’s created by shots works throughout the body, but it resides primarily in the blood. That means it may take longer to mount a response.

“If they are already on site, they can immediately eliminate the fire, but if they’re like 2 miles away, they first need to drive there, and by that time, one-third of the house is already in full flames,” Wyler said.

Mucosal vaccines are also better at priming a different kind of first responder than injections do. They do a better job of summoning IgA antibodies, which have four arms to grab onto invaders instead of the two arms that the y-shaped IgG antibodies have. Some scientists think IgA antibodies may be less picky about their targets than IgG antibodies, which makes them better equipped to deal with new variants.

The new nasal vaccine takes a new approach to a very old idea: weakening a virus so it’s no longer a threat and then giving it to people so their immune systems can learn to recognize and fight it off. The first vaccines using this approach date to the 1870s, against anthrax and rabies. Back then, scientists weakened the agents they were using with heat and chemicals.

The researchers manipulated the genetic material in the virus to make it harder for cells to translate. This technique, called codon pair deoptimization, hobbles the virus so it can be shown to the immune system without making the body sick.

“You could imagine reading a text … and every letter is a different font, or every letter is a different size, then the text is much harder to read. And this is basically what we do in codon pair deoptimization,” Wyler said.

In the hamster studies, which were published Monday in the journal Nature Microbiology, two doses of the live but weakened nasal vaccine created a much stronger immune response than either two doses of an mRNA-based vaccine or one that uses an adenovirus to ferry the vaccine instructions into cells.

The researchers think the live weakened vaccine probably worked better because it closely mimics the process of a natural infection.

The nasal vaccine also previews the entire coronavirus for the body, not just its spike proteins like current Covid-19 vaccines do, so the hamsters were able to make immune weapons against a wider range of targets.

As promising as all this sounds, vaccine experts say caution is warranted. This vaccine still has to pass more tests before it’s ready for use, but they say the results look encouraging.

Health officials consider another round of bivalent boosters for the most vulnerable Americans

“They did a very nice job. This is obviously a competent and thoughtful team that did this work, and impressive in the scope of what they did. Now it just needs to be repeated,” perhaps in primates and certainly in humans before it can be widely used, said Dr. Greg Poland, who designs vaccines at the Mayo Clinic. He was not involved in the new research.

The study began in 2021, before the Omicron variant was around, so the vaccine tested in these experiments was made with the original strain of the coronavirus. In the experiments, when they infected animals with Omicron, the live but weakened nasal vaccine still performed better than the others, but its ability to neutralize the virus was diminished. Researchers think it will need an update.

It also needs to be tested in humans, and Wyler says they’re working on that. The scientists have partnered with a Swiss company called RocketVax to start phase I clinical trials.

Other vaccines are further along, but the progress has been “slow and halting,” Poland said. Groups working on these vaccines are struggling to raise the steep costs of getting a new vaccine to market, and they’re doing it in a setting where people tend to think the vaccine race has been won and done.

In reality, Poland said, we’re far from that. All it would take is another Omicron-level shift in the evolution of the virus, and we could be back at square one, with no effective tools against the coronavirus.

“That’s foolish. We should be developing a pan-coronavirus vaccine that does induce mucosal immunity and that is long-lived,” he said.

At least four nasal vaccines for Covid-19 have reached late-stage testing in people, according to the World Health Organization’s vaccine tracker.

The nasal vaccines in use in China and India rely on harmless adenoviruses to ferry their instructions into cells, although effectiveness data for these has not been published.

Two other nasal vaccines are finishing human studies.

Covid vaccine maker Novavax says it may not survive

One, a recombinant vaccine that can be produced cheaply in chicken eggs, the same way many flu vaccines are, is being put through its paces by researchers at Mount Sinai in New York City.

Another, like the German vaccine, uses a live but weakened version of the virus. It’s being developed by a company called Codagenix. Results of those studies, which were carried out in South America and Africa, may come later this year.

The German team says it’s eagerly watching for the Codagenix data.

“They will be very important in order to know where whether this kind of attempt is basically promising or not,” Wyler said.

They have reason to worry. Respiratory infections have proved to be tough targets for inhaled vaccines.

FluMist, a live but weakened form of the flu virus, works reasonably well in children but doesn’t help adults as much. The reason is thought to be that adults already have immune memory for the flu, and when the virus is injected into the nose, the vaccine mostly boosts what’s already there.

Sign up here to get The Results Are In with Dr. Sanjay Gupta every Tuesday from the CNN Health team.

Still, some of the most potent vaccines such as the vaccine against measles, mumps and rubella use live attenuated viruses, so it’s a promising approach.

Another consideration is that live vaccines can’t be taken by everyone. People with very compromised immunity are often cautioned against using live vaccines because even these very weakened viruses may be risky for them.

“Although it’s strongly attenuated, it’s still a real virus,” Wyler said, so it would have to be used carefully.

#130. Lies, a disguise and surfboard bags: How a jealous Sydney policeman tried to cover up double murder

Watch: The CCTV that helped convict a Sydney policeman of double murder

It was just after 09:00 on a warm February morning when CCTV picked up a man dressed as an UberEats rider hovering on a sidewalk in one of Sydney's wealthiest neighbourhoods.

He dawdled for around 20 minutes, "steeling himself for what he was about to do" prosecutors say, before disappearing out of sight. Minutes later another CCTV camera captured the sound of gunshots ringing out.

Former police officer Beau Lamarre, 31, was on Wednesday found guilty of murdering his former partner Jesse Baird, a TV presenter, and Luke Davies, Baird's new partner, with his work-issued gun.

In February 2024, the bloodied belongings of Baird and Davies were found in a skip in the suburb of Cronulla, sparking a frantic search for the couple. It culminated in the discovery of their bodies on a rural property, partially buried beneath sticks and debris.

Luke Davies (left) and Jesse Baird had been dating only months when they were gunned down

During a two-month trial filled with at-times extraordinary testimony and a wealth of CCTV evidence, the court heard Lamarre had planned his crime over months.

Jurors watched CCTV of the then police officer purchasing a surfboard bag in which to put Baird's body, after casually sizing it up against a friend in the shop, and heard from a stream of former friends and colleagues who testified that Lamarre asked them for advice about everything from mobile phone signals to where he could buy a gun.

One recounted the chilling moment Lamarre told her that if he were ever to murder someone, he would leave their belongings in a dumpster and hide the body in bushland where they would never be found.

"What would you do if I told you I killed somebody? Would you help me hide the body?" he asked, according to her testimony.

Later, Lamarre asked for help in covering up the killings; his friends - none of whom have been implicated - say they began receiving panicked calls and odd requests from him.

One told the court Lamarre persuaded her to go for a drive in the country, during which he tried to dispose of the bodies at the family farm of a man he'd met on a dating app. Over the speakers, he played Taylor Swift's No Body, No Crime.

Baird, 26, and Lamarre - a one-time celebrity blogger who also had his own swimwear line on the side of his police work - met in a nightclub in October 2022. For Lamarre, it quickly became an infatuation, but for Baird, an AFL referee and former Network Ten presenter, the relationship appeared to be casual.

After discovering in April 2023 that Lamarre had gone through messages on his phone, and unfollowed other men on his Instagram account, Baird broke it off.

But Lamarre's obsession only grew. He watched Baird having sex with another man through his bedroom window, broke into his house multiple times, deleting contacts on his phone and turning on location settings so that he could engineer meetings with the TV presenter.

On one occasion Baird woke up to find Lamarre in his bedroom; not realising who it was, he chased him down the street. Lamarre was also convicted of aggravated break and enter over that incident on Wednesday.

Prosecutor Brett Hatfield said Baird was "oblivious to the accused's scheming and manipulations", and the pair briefly rekindled the relationship before Baird ended it definitively in November.

He'd discovered Lamarre had, again, been accessing his phone and had attempted to ward off a love rival by pretending to have received flowers from Baird.

Beau Lamarre pictured at the annual Gay and Lesbian Mardi Gras parade in Sydney in 2020

His pride wounded, Hatfield said Lamarre's thoughts turned to a "darker place": "revenge".

He asked an old friend if her ex-partner could get him a gun: "This was the beginning of the plan for the murder," the prosecutor said.

In the months leading up to the killings, Lamarre bought a burner phone, a motorcycle helmet, and practised shooting in the woods. On holiday with a friend in Bali in January 2024 he tried to buy a silencer.

While his murder plans percolated, he also began lashing out with petty acts of malice, prosecutors said, such as putting water in the fuel tank of Baird's car.

But at the same time as he plotted, Lamarre was still trying to win his ex back.

On one occasion he pressured a friend to send Baird a 1,600-word message he had himself composed, claiming that he had cancer, and may need to have his arm amputated.

Under questioning in court, he said a naturopath he couldn't remember the name of had diagnosed him with "a form of sporadic cancer".

By the end of 2023, Baird had met and hit it off with Davies, 29. "It was early days but they were both smitten with each other," his mother, Helen Baird, told the court. He sent her a picture of the pair at a Pink concert. "I just sent a big love heart and said 'I'm so happy for both of you'."

Jesse Baird and Luke Davies at the Pink concert shortly before they were killed

Early on the morning of 19 February, Lamarre picked up a hire car he had asked his mother to rent and drove to Baird's home. After waiting several hours for Baird's housemates to leave, while disguised as an UberEats driver, he let himself in with a key he had copied.

Lamarre told the court he had been at Baird's home to pick up some belongings, including a A$25,000 (US$16,500, Â£12,600) Cartier ring, and had disguised himself to avoid the embarrassment of being recognised if anyone was home.

He claimed he let himself in when no one answered the door, and the two men were killed in separate struggles. He argued that Baird had been holding a knife and the first shot was fired to disarm him, and the second by accident. Davies shot himself when he grabbed the gun, Lamarre said.

But prosecutors told the jury Baird was shot in the back and then once again through his temple as he lay gravely wounded on the kitchen floor.

The evidence, Hatfield said, pointed to one thing only: "an execution".

Davies was then shot through a bedroom door as he tried to call emergency services. The court heard the panic in his voice as the seconds-long call was replayed before it dropped out.

After that, Lamarre set about trying to hide the evidence of what Hatfield called his "brazen and shocking murders".

One of his first actions was to call a friend. He testified Lamarre confessed he had "killed people" and asked him to take a day off work "to help him clean up his mess".

Later Lamarre pretended the call was a joke, saying, "Did you like my Oscar performance? Got you".

Meanwhile, Lamarre went to a DIY store to purchase mops, bleach and bin bags. After returning to Baird's house to clean up, he left the bodies wrapped in surfboard bags and tarpaulins in the courtyard.

Baird had been set to move out that week, and Lamarre used Baird's phone to reassure his housemates he had hired an Airtasker to move his belongings, adding, "I might be flying to Perth tonight".

Baird's housemates quickly spotted the surfboard bags when they returned home, snapping a photograph tendered in court which showed "red liquid" seeping out.

"I thought it was rusted water from the outdoor furniture," one of the women said.

The surfboard bags were gone the next morning.

Lamarre was captured on CCTV sizing up a surfboard bag in the days before the murders

Lamarre had hired a van, and over the next two days made more trips to the DIY store and a sports shop to buy gumboots, a wheelbarrow, bigger surfboard bags and 10kg weights. He squeezed in a hospital appointment for a minor procedure and paused for a sushi date with a friend, CCTV showing him laughing and eating as the bodies lay in the back of his van.

On 21 February, he picked up another friend in the van for what she thought was lunch.

There were blue tarps in the back and it smelled "lemony", she told the court. She "wasn't allowed to put the windows up or turn the aircon off" as they motored towards Goulburn, 200km southwest of Sydney, stopping only at another DIY store, where he asked her to buy an angle grinder.

His friend told the court she remembers Lamarre playing No Body, No Crime at some point: "It's a very niche song."

Arriving at a farm owned by a Hinge date's family, Lamarre used the angle grinder to cut through a padlock, making his way to a dam where he unsuccessfully tried to sink the bodies.

It was now late at night and he drove his friend, by this time upset and confused, home before returning to retrieve the bodies. He then left them in the van, parked in Sydney overnight, before driving once more to Goulburn, where he dumped the bodies near a monastery.

Later that day he visited another friend, telling her to look up the definition of schizophrenia and asking whether he could use her hose, because "there was a bit of blood he had to scrub off" the hired van, the court heard.

The deaths of Baird and Davies triggered an outpouring of horror in Sydney

By this time police were closing in, and on 23 February Lamarre went to turn himself in at Bondi police station. On the way, he called a police helpline.

"I've been bullied by multiple New South Wales police force members," he told the woman on the other end of the line. "All this stress and trauma and psychological abuse and trauma - did I say trauma already? - has caused me to not sleep, get anxious, it's caused me to have some symptoms of schizophrenia."

"Actually sorry, you know what, can you just... delete that whole thing. We'll just keep it short and sweet."

He wasn't sure when he'd be back to work, he said. Shortly afterwards he was arrested and charged with murder, and the bodies of Baird and Davies were found four days later.

Lamarre's lawyer John Stratton insisted he'd acted in self-defence and had "panicked" realising how the scene would look to outsiders.

The former police officer did plenty of "foolish" and "unusual" things - "He was, it's fair to say, a poorly behaved boyfriend," Stratton said - but that did not make him a murderer.

Ultimately, however, the jury was not convinced and took just over an hour to convict him. He will be sentenced in March and faces life in prison.

#129. DOJ accuses former cyber executive of defrauding ransomware victims by secretly paying hackers

The U.S. Department of Justice has indicted a former executive of a ransomware recovery firm after he allegedly defrauded victims of cyberattacks by claiming the company could get their encrypted files back.

According to federal prosecutors, Juan Ricardo Palacio Escobar, 52, was the chief technology officer at RansomwareHelp, which purported to offer data recovery services to victims of ransomware. The company’s website, which currently reads as “under maintenance,” claimed it could decrypt hacked data by using its proprietary technology, according to an archived copy of the site from earlier this year. RansomwareHelp touted a success rate of “over 99%.”

But the company allegedly had no technical ability to unscramble data and instead “negotiated with and paid a ransom to the ransomware actors to decrypt the victim data,” while charging the ransomware victim more and keeping the difference,” per the Justice Department’s statement.

Palacio Escobar then allegedly used the funds to buy several properties in Florida.

The DOJ said the FBI carried out an undercover operation in June, in part posing as a company that claimed it had been hit by ransomware. The company claimed to the undercover agents that it never negotiated with hackers or paid a ransom. But then Palacio Escobar paid the apparent hackers some $6,000 in cryptocurrency, without the knowledge that FBI agents were also posing as the ransomware hackers.

404 Media first reported the indictment last week against Palacio Escobar. He faces three charges of wire fraud, and could receive 20 years in prison on each count.

Palacio Escobar is the latest, albeit rare, example of cybersecurity professionals going rogue. Earlier this year, two ransomware negotiators working for cybersecurity companies were jailed on hacking and extortion charges, after being caught moonlighting with the ransomware gangs that they were ostensibly investigating. A third ransomware negotiator was also convicted of helping hackers deploy ransomware against several companies.

Last month, independent cybersecurity journalist Brian Krebs reported that FBI agents arrested a Canadian cybersecurity executive in Philadelphia on allegations that he was involved with the theft of FBI personnel records by the hacking and extortion gang ShinyHunters. The hacking group told TechCrunch that they took credit for the theft of FBI data in September, which they said they would not publicly release, a departure from its earlier extortion-based cyberattacks.

The federal charges against the Canadian cybersecurity executive relate to conspiracy to commit extortion, though much of the court docket — and his alleged links to the hacking group — remains under seal.
