#127. Is China behind a wave of AI fake videos targeting Taiwan?

Taiwan's ruling party is being repeatedly targeted with AI videos spreading false claims about its leader and other key members ahead of crucial local elections, BBC Verify has found.

A China-linked network has pumped out more than 500 fake videos about the Democratic Progressive Party (DPP) in the past year, which experts suggest is part of a long-running campaign to influence the outcome of the elections.

The videos appear to try to smear President Lai Ching-te by claiming he holds a US green card - and would abandon Taiwan if war broke out with China - which Taiwan's presidential office says is completely false. Deepfakes also appear to show Puma Shen, who is running to be the mayor of capital Taipei, using drugs, which he denies. Others seek to undermine or ridicule the politicians by showing them in children's clothing or as popular film characters.

The Taipei mayoralty has traditionally served as a springboard to the presidency. Experts say this has made Shen, who has been openly critical of Beijing, an obvious target for disinformation campaigns. The current mayor, Chiang Wan-an, is part of Taiwan's Kuomintang (KMT) party, which favours closer ties with China.

Taiwan is a self-governing island that China claims as part of its territory. Beijing and Taipei have long observed an uneasy status quo under which Taiwan, backed by the US, neither formally declares independence nor unites with China.

But tensions have risen since the DPP came to power in 2016. Beijing has repeatedly accused the party of pursuing independence while China's leader Xi Jinping, who has said "reunification" with Taiwan is a key goal, has stepped up pressure against the party with increased military activity around the island.

Experts believe the current disinformation activity is part of a broader campaign against Taiwan designed to undermine trust in its democratic process.

The campaign has targeted Taiwan's President Lai (top) and his predecessor Tsai (bottom)

"The account operators are trying to make people distrust the DPP politicians so that they will not believe whatever they say," Austin Horng-En Wang, an expert in cognitive warfare at the University of Nevada, Las Vegas, told BBC Verify. "If you don't trust that candidate then you will not listen to what they say," he added.

The videos, many of which were shared from an account called "noxparable" or carried a watermark with the same name, have been viewed tens of millions of times across several social media platforms since May 2025.

More than half of those views were tracked in the past four months as the activity has ramped up ahead of the elections. Many of the accounts involved in spreading disinformation listed their locations on social media as mainland China and Hong Kong, while a number of the clips carried labels showing they were generated with AI. Many people who engaged with the videos were often seen repeating the false claims or attacking DPP figures in the comments, while others appeared to treat the clips as entertainment.

One of the most widely shared videos targeted Shen by superimposing his face onto a man who is seen holding a lighter and exhaling smoke alongside claims he is smoking drugs. The video appears to build on accusations from Taiwan's opposition party that he supports drugs decriminalisation, which Shen denies, and was debunked by a local news network.

Shen has taken legal action against people sharing deepfakes, local media reported, while his campaign office said AI videos showing the candidate appeared to be part of a "classic tactic China uses to smear specific targets". BBC Verify contacted the Chinese embassy in London for comment.

Many of the fake videos ridiculed President Lai and Shen while others portrayed other KMT candidates such as Ko Chih-en (above) in a positive light

Other debunked claims include allegations that Lai's predecessor Tsai Ing-wen had fraudulently obtained her PhD from the London School of Economics. The university pushed back on identical allegations in 2019 and confirmed it had awarded her the doctorate.

A number of the social media accounts BBC Verify identified as sharing the disinformation videos appeared to be linked to Chinese firm Borderless, which produces websites that blend apolitical content with pro-China messaging. The network has also been investigated by journalists at the Taiwanese independent media outlet The Reporter, who shared their findings with BBC Verify. Borderless has previously been accused of carrying out "cognitive warfare" against Taiwan by Taipei's National Security Bureau and the firm has been contacted for comment.

Analysts confirmed to BBC Verify there were also linguistic clues that the accounts, which presented themselves as based in Taiwan, were actually posting from China. The posts occasionally used a simplified Chinese script introduced by the Communist government which was never adopted in Taiwan.

The posts mostly appeared during regular office hours, which Wang suggested showed a "professional" operation, while the accounts originating outside Taiwan showed evidence of election interfering. "When they pretended to be citizens in another country and used these accounts to manipulate the public opinion of the other country during the campaign, it is definitely election interference," Wang added.

Earlier this month, Taiwan's central election commission aired an advert warning voters to "beware of AI-generated deepfake videos" circulating online that could seek to influence the upcoming election. It comes as government officials flagged nearly 2,700 social media accounts to Meta that it claimed were part of a Chinese information warfare campaign, Reuters reported. Meta said it had removed the accounts.

Andrew Yeh of the China Strategic Risk Institute said the ultimate goal is not to favour any party. "China's information operations succeed not just by backing one candidate or opposing another, but by diluting Taiwan's policy debates, driving polarisation and degrading Taiwanese citizens' trust in their democratic processes," he said.

"Regardless of whether the KMT or DPP comes out on top, a more fractured and more divided Taiwan is in Beijing's interests."

What do you want BBC Verify to investigate?

#125. Want to buy your kid an AI toy this Christmas? Not in New York

Santa won’t be delivering any AI chatbot toys to New York kids this Christmas.

Gov. Kathy Hochul this week signed a bill banning the manufacture, distribution, and sale of such toys in New York for the next five years. The bill explicitly targets toys with embedded or integrated AI chatbots, like talking stuffed animals, dolls, and robots that rely on the internet to keep kids yapping.

The bill makes New York the second state after California to institute a ban on AI toys.

Technically, the new law takes effect 90 days after signing, so some parents could try to fulfill their kids’ wish lists for a creepy toy like a jumped-up Furby in time for the holidays, but the governor urges them not to, given the risks. Apparently, about a quarter of American parents with kids under eight either own an AI toy or are considering giving their child one this holiday season, according to a new study released this week by Common Sense Media.

When asked how New York will enforce the ban given the pervasiveness of e-commerce platforms, a spokesperson directed TechCrunch to a statement from Albert Pulido, deputy secretary for finance and technology: “We’re partnering closely with the attorney general on this and the fees are very steep — they’re $15,000 a day. So working closely with the attorney general will make sure these toys are banned in New York State.”

When asked how Amazon intends to help enforce the ban, including for third-party sellers, a spokesperson from the company said it will take steps to restrict the sale of AI-enabled toys in prohibited jurisdictions, without going into specifics.

The Common Sense Media study found that AI toys can undermine children’s human relationships and expose them to inappropriate content. The researchers tested five popular AI toys marketed for learning and companionship, including Grem, Lovi, Meemo Bear, BubblePal Panda, and Tavlshero‘s AI Puppy.

The study found that the toys failed to meet numerous safety practices, undermining human relationships in part by claiming they loved their users, promising to keep secrets, suggesting they were more fun than real relationships or school, and helping children break parents’ bedtime rules.

While most of the toys in the study refused direct requests involving sex and violence, BubblePal Panda role-played a mass shooting in detail when asked. Most were willing to explain how to use or help locate dangerous items like matches, knives, and pills, according to the study.

Common Sense Media also found that the toys didn’t provide parents with reliable ways to monitor their young children’s interactions with the chatbots. Either they didn’t produce transcripts of chats at all, or the transcripts they did produce were riddled with errors.

It’s worth noting that Common Sense Media conducted a similar test of AI toys in January, finding that they pose an unacceptable risk for young children. Grem was included in that study, which suggests the industry hasn’t adequately addressed previously documented concerns.

While a November study conducted by the group found that parents are wising up to the potential dangers of AI toys and starting to steer clear, parents from lower-income households and those experiencing symptoms of burnout are more willing to consider them.

“These toys are promising relief to burned-out parents but simply miss the mark when it comes to basic safety measures,” Dr. Jenny Radesky, associate professor of pediatrics at the University of Michigan Medical School, said in a statement. “Caregivers should steer clear of these products that do not support the development of imagination or early human relationships.”

TechCrunch has reached out to the toy manufacturers for comment.

#124. The $500 billion beauty industry's 'green' ambitions are a patchwork at best. And they're falling short

The escalating climate crisis is shifting many people’s purchasing patterns and this extends to the $500 billion dollar global beauty industry which is grappling with a range of sustainability challenges across product manufacturing, packaging and disposal.

'Delay means death': We're running out of ways to adapt to the climate crisis, new report shows. Here are the key takeaways

Strategy and consulting firm Simon Kucher’s Global Sustainability Study 2021 found 60% of consumers around the world rated sustainability as an important purchase criterion, and 35% were willing to pay more for sustainable products or services.

This shift in consumer preferences has propelled many beauty brands to set environmental goals: to move away from single-use and virgin plastics, provide recyclable, reusable and refillable packaging and offer more transparency around products’ ingredients so customers can ascertain how “green” their purchase is.

However, consumers still struggle to understand the sustainability credentials of many products, according to the British Beauty Council. This is because the industry’s clean-up efforts have been inconsistent, and fall short of making a recognizable impact in the absence of collective goal-setting, global strategy and standardized regulations.

There is no international standard for the beauty industry on how much product ingredient information to share with customers — or how to do so. Brands can set their own rules and goals, giving rise to confusion and “greenwashing,” where sustainability claims are often touted but not substantiated.

Companies often use marketing language like “clean beauty” to make it seem like their products are natural, for example, when they may not actually be organic, sustainable or ethically made.

After rebranding in the West, many beauty companies are still offering to 'whiten' skin elsewhere

“The term ‘clean beauty’ has become quite dangerous. It’s used to sell more products,” according to British Beauty Council CEO Millie Kendall, who added that such buzzwords are losing traction in the UK as British customers wise up to their shortcomings. “Customers need better marketing information and certification information.”

In a 2021 report calling on the industry to have “the courage to change” their business practices, the British Beauty Council wrote that, all too often, even natural ingredients involved in manufacturing products give way to “over-consumption, non-regenerative farming practices, pollution, waste and neglect.”

“The only way out of this is transparency,” Kendall told CNN.

Jen Lee, chief impact officer at US-based brand Beautycounter, said she continues to see confusion over ingredients among consumers. (In 2013, the company launched and published “The Never List,” which currently cites more than 2,800 chemicals — including heavy metals, parabens and formaldehyde — it claims to never use in its products.)

“Natural vs. synthetic ingredients has been a conversation. People think natural is safer, but it’s not always the case,” Lee explained. “Natural ingredients formulated in the industry can have toxic load. Heavy metals can occur in natural components of the earth.”

Mother loses peripheral vision from apparent exposure to mercury in beauty creams. Toxic levels in her home put family at risk, say experts

“We used to be more natural and organic,” added Sasha Plavsic, founder of makeup brand ILIA Beauty. “What was challenging is (that) raw materials were difficult to source or would come in inconsistently or products wouldn’t perform.”

Most makeup is created and molded at high temperatures, Plavsic explained. Purely organic materials often fall apart in this heat, leading to inconsistent results and subpar product performance. “Not every synthetic is bad,” Plavsic said. “Sometimes, it helps create the best in class formula.”

The industry’s plastic packaging is a particular sustainability challenge — 95% is thrown away and the vast majority is not recycled, according to the British Beauty Council.

The cosmetics business is the fourth biggest plastic packaging user globally — after food and beverage, industrial packaging and pharmaceuticals — and plastic is about 67% of the industry’s packaging volume, according to Vantage Market Research. Beauty giant L’Oreal used 144,430 metric tons of plastic in its packaging material in 2021, for example, according to the Ellen Macarthur Foundation (EMF). Estee Lauder Companies reported its brands produced 71,600 metric tons of plastic in product packaging that same year.

And only 9% of the global plastic waste is recycled, according to a report from the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development. The United States only recycles 4% of its plastic waste.

Single-use plastic is wreaking havoc on the planet. Here's what you can do to minimize your impact

Many brands are trying to phase out harmful plastics from their operations and adopt post-consumer recycled (PCR) plastic. (L’Oreal has set a target of 50% PCR plastic usage by 2025, while Estee Lauder is targeting 25% “or more” PCR plastic — but both are far from achieving their targets.)

“Between 60-70 major global brands have made unprecedented progress” in PCR plastic usage across industries, EMF’s Plastic Initiative Lead Sander DeFruyt told CNN. But DeFruyt stressed that PCR plastic must be adopted in conjunction with brands removing single and virgin plastics from their usage cycles to truly make a difference.

However, PCR plastic is not easy to find — low recycling rates around the world mean there is limited supply. Meanwhile, demand for it is growing demand across industries, DeFruyt said. This competition hikes up its price, which is already higher than virgin plastic.

Hair care brand FEKKAI claims that it used up to 95% PCR content in its packaging, but pricing and supply issues posed a challenge, forcing it to currently aim for containers and packaging that feature at least 50% PCR in its packaging.

“PCR plastic is more expensive than stock plastic. The cost is hard and then sourcing it is too,” founder Frédéric Fekkai told CNN. “PCR is close to our heart, but there is a massive demand, so finding recycled plastic is difficult.”

Beauty retailers plays a pivotal — and under-utilized — role, with control over stocking decisions and supply chains. But many vary when it comes to the standards they set for brands they sell.

“Smaller businesses do more, full stop,” said Jessi Baker, founder of the technology platform Provenance, which helps brands display their sustainability credentials for customers. “They move more nimbly. Some of them are born-good brands — climate friendliness was part of their setup. They don’t need to restructure their entire supply chain. Their culture already has it compared to the larger brands who need to work hard to change.”

A history of beauty trends -- and the standards that shaped them

Sephora launched its “Clean + Planet Positive” initiative in 2021, which labeled products that met its set criteria. (This is separate from the French retailer’s “Clean at Sephora” program, which is currently facing a consumer lawsuit alleging it carries a significant percentage of products understood by customers to be harmful.) Target launched a similar program in 2022, featuring a “Target Zero” icon for both online and in-store offerings that either have reusable, recyclable, compostable or reduced plastic packaging, or feature waterless or concentrated products.

Still, many steps taken by brands and retailers do not even begin to touch on the waste and pollution generated throughout supply chains, manufacturing and shipping, all huge problems for the industry to grapple with.

The gaps in standardization in the beauty ecosystem can, to some extent, be filled by certifications such as the US-born B Corporation, or B Corp. This accreditation, one of the most well-known in the beauty space, is issued by the non-profit B Lab, which scores a company on a variety of criteria around ethics and sustainability. However beneficial it may be among eco-conscious consumers, though, it is currently completely voluntary for brands to apply for.

Governments and multinationals enforcing regulations and setting a base line for brands to operate from when making sustainability claims would go a long way to making change, many experts and business leaders believe.

Susanne Kaufmann, founder of her namesake beauty brand, says her efforts in Austria would reap better results if more countries around the world had stricter, more uniform garbage disposal laws.

A trash heap 62 meters high shows the scale of India's climate challenge

“I package our product in a recyclable material,” Kaufmann said. (Her products’ packaging, which is refillable and reusable, is made from 75% recycled plastic — and is 100% recyclable.) If I send this to the US, the garbage is not separated… and it’s not recyclable,” she explained, referring to inconsistencies in recycling laws across the United States.

And when it comes to ingredients, the European Chemicals Agency lists 2,495 substances banned from use in cosmetic products marketed for sale or use in the bloc. But the US Food and Drug administration only lists 11, making it more challenging for American consumers to find safer, greener options. The Environmental Working Group, a non-profit watchdog, studied lab tests of 51 sunscreen products in 2021 and found that only 35% of products met the EU standard, compared with 94% that passed the US standard.

However, while government can set minimum requirements, Mia Davis, vice president of sustainability and impact at beauty retailer Credo Beauty, says the needle will move in the private sector.

Sustainable ships: The world's most eco-conscious cruises

“Regulation can raise the floor a bit. A person who doesn’t know about any (sustainability issues) should still be able to walk into a bodega and get clean products… But that’s never going to be what the market can do,” she said. “Market leadership is key.”

In the absence of bold regulations or global standards on sustainability practices, this “leadership” — undertaken both by brands and customers in the beauty marketplace — is likely to be the most immediately impactful vector for addressing the industry’s climate shortcomings. It will take continued collective advocacy and initiative to see meaningful climate-conscious change.

#123. These North Koreans say they were spied on, beaten and jailed for being Christian

Kim Jong Un's communist regime is accused of brutal crackdowns on religious freedom in a new report

Living in North Korea, Kim Bo-bin got used to praying with her eyes open.

She found ingenious ways to keep her Christian faith a secret in the Communist dictatorship, which tightly controls how and where its citizens worship. Unauthorised displays of faith could land people in prison, she tells the BBC.

So Kim would silently mouth hymns and clap with just two of her fingers. "In North Korea, if there are three people in a room, you are being spied on. So I would only close my eyes [and pray] when I lay on the floor."

Now 44, Kim lives just outside the South Korean capital Seoul, where she fled 15 years ago.

Her testimony - of how she prayed in secret for years, and was jailed and beaten for her faith - is part of a new report, external documenting the experiences of 570 North Korean escapees, most of whom say they experienced or witnessed anti-Christian persecution.

Spanning five years and featuring interviews with defectors from across the country, the report by British human rights group Korea Future accuses Kim Jong Un's regime of repeatedly violating religious freedom through a system of surveillance, fear and violence. It suggests little has changed after sustained international pressure, with multiple incidents of suspected Christians being beaten and tortured as recently as last year.

North Korea maintains that it allows religious freedom in spite of years of evidence heard by rights groups and the United Nations. While churches exist in Pyongyang, they are controlled by the state and widely seen as projecting an illusion of religious liberty.

"The North Korean leader's authority is god-like," says Kang Dong-wan, professor at Dong-a University. "So they cannot allow gods whose authority is above him." Pyongyang fears that religious freedom may threaten the regime itself, he adds.

Kim Bo-bin has been living in South Korea for 15 years now

First established in Korea in the late 1700s, Christianity exploded across the north after Protestant missionaries arrived in the 1880s. North Korea provides no figures on its Christian population but the UN has referred to estimates of 200,000 to 400,000 of them practising secretly, some of whom are detained in camps.

Surveillance and control over religion, like many other aspects of North Korean life, intensified under Kim Jong Un, who succeeded his father in 2011.

Three South Korean missionaries operating in the Chinese border region were arrested between 2013-2014 and sentenced to life in prison on charges of espionage and plotting a coup. It's not clear if they are still alive, as Seoul says Pyongyang has refused to share any information about the detainees.

Pyongyang has also been cracking down on smuggled memory sticks containing South Korean-made videos of Christian sermons. In 2020, it passed a law - on Rejecting Reactionary Ideology and Culture - to block so-called "foreign influence". Owning Bibles and hosting services can now result in imprisonment, while distributing religious books or videos is punishable by death.

Lessons against religion begin as early as kindergarten for most North Koreans. Children must attend "self-criticism" sessions at school in which they are warned against "superstitions", widely understood to refer to Christianity and other faiths.

An image from 2010 showing the inside of a state-sanctioned church in Pyongyang

This is what Kim Bo-bin recalls being taught. The faiths Koreans have long practised - Christianity since the 18th Century and shamanism, a millennia-old practice rooted in ancestral spirit worship - were dismissed as superstitions.

Her teachers never mentioned that Kim Il Sung himself was born into a fervently Christian family, or that Pyongyang had once been dubbed the "Jerusalem of the East."

Rather, every child and adult is expected to know North Korea's foundational Ten Principles by heart. They state that citizens must hold the "firm perspective" they know of "nothing greater than the great comrades" Kim Il Sung, the founder of North Korea, his son Kim Jong Il and the ruling party. The "smallest attempt" to dent this authority must not be tolerated, it adds.

The report documents both the indoctrination as well as the constant surveillance, with police and a network of neighbourhood spies monitoring suspicious activities. A former police informant says she was instructed to gather information on neighbours' worshipping, and handed a memo by an officer describing Christianity as an "anti-socialist activity".

North Korean authorities are also reported to hold intergenerational records of families' beliefs condemning Christians' children and grandchildren to a life without higher education or decent career prospects.

All of this illustrates how various North Korean institutions, including schools and the police, control its citizens' beliefs from "cradle to the grave," co-author James Burt says.

And yet many North Koreans turn to religion because their lives are so precarious, he adds. Faith offers them something that the state ideology "with its rigid doctrines and disconnect from their lived realities" cannot.

North Koreans pay tribute to statues of the late Kim Il Sung and Kim Jong Il

Kim Bo-bin grew up in North Hamgyong province, along the border with China. It was a remote, impoverished part of the country where those deemed "untrustworthy" by the state ended up, such as landowners and Christians.

Stuck at the bottom of a state-enforced hierarchy, this "hostile class" had little chance of moving up the ladder. Many of Kim's neighbours were barred from higher education and government positions, or ever living in Pyongyang.

One of them was an exiled "crazy grandma" who used to disappear into a coal shed every morning to chant words 10-year-old Kim didn't recognise. "One day, [the grandma] told me we go from ashes to ashes and dust to dust," she recalls. It was only 12 years later, when she encountered a Bible for the first time in her life that she realised the grandma was a Christian.

When a devastating famine began in the late 1990s, North Hamgyong was one of the first regions to starve because it was cut off from the state's food supply which prioritised Pyongyang. Millions were killed in what the regime described euphemistically as the "Arduous March". It was a time when many North Koreans crossed into China seeking food and work.

Kim was one of them. Aged 22 in 2003, she took what she believed was a job opportunity in China offered by a friend's aunt. But once she crossed the border, she was forced into marriage with a Chinese man and became pregnant.

While picking up a free pram for her newborn daughter at a building adorned with a cross, she encountered a missionary for the first time. Kim would join a growing group of people who discovered Christianity in China - a group that Pyongyang saw as a threat when they were returned to the North, according to Prof Kang.

A view of the city of Chongjin in North Korea's North Hamgyong province

Kim was arrested in China in 2007 and deported. During interrogation, she says North Korean secret police asked her if she'd ever worn a cross on her neck. She pretended not to understand. She told the police she only recognised the symbol from hospital buildings.

Kim Bo-bin says it was God's blessing the interrogator seemed more interested in whether she intended to flee to South Korea, a grave offence. She says she was beaten for 23 days and sentenced to three years of hard labour at a prison. Her story tallies with multiple accounts in Korea Future's report of extreme police violence causing broken bones and severe bleeding.

Lee Young-joo, who Kim Bo-bin met in prison also after being deported from China, tells the BBC she was interrogated about whether she'd met any missionaries, been to a church or seen a Bible. "Of course you must deny all of it. I said no, I haven't seen it," she told them.

In her prison cell, Lee says she'd keep her eyes open, begging God to save her life. Whenever anyone shut their eyes, guards would swear and shout: "Are you praying?"

Lee is glad she wasn't found to be a Christian, because she says she "would have been sent to a political prisoners' camp. I would be tortured and never allowed to leave and be buried there." This is a widely-held fear among defectors, although Korea Future's report did not hear any first-hand testimonies of Christians being held in such camps.

Lee, who previously shared with the BBC the abuse she suffered inside prison, says she was sentenced to three-and-half years. Outside of hard labour, she would spend time praying, sometimes with other Christian inmates. They would hold hands and whisper Bible verses from memory.

Lee Young-joo first met a Christian after she escaped from North Korea into China

She and Kim Bo-bin eventually returned home after serving their sentences, where they continued to practise their religion in secret, they tell the BBC.

Kim says she managed to convert her younger sister and other relatives: "My aunt's walls are thick. The neighbours can't hear us so I quietly taught them hymns and about the all-knowing and all-powerful God."

Lee tells the BBC her mother was horrified when she learned of her Christian faith. Undeterred, she told her that she would escape again to join her daughter, who had been left in China as Lee was arrested and deported. When her mother protested, she responded saying they could all live together later.

Her incredulous mother asked: where? "In heaven," she replied.

Today, Lee attends a small church of North Korean escapees in Seoul. She prays daily, to thank God for the chicken soup and the stroll along the river. She prays for her daughter, who escaped with her in 2010, to find a nice Christian man to marry. Her mother died in North Korea some years after her escape, she was told.

Kim managed to flee to South Korea eventually, in 2012, at the age of 30, along with her daughter. She prays throughout the day - and not silently. At church on Sundays, she is the loudest singer.

Could Kim's teen daughter become North Korea's next leader?

#122. Australia, Solomon Islands sign sweeping pact seen as countering China

Australia will become the Solomon Islands' primary security partner, while the archipelago will reject any foreign power trying to "interfere" on its "sensitive" infrastructure.

Australia and the Solomon Islands signed a security pact on Wednesday that makes Australia the archipelago's main defense partner.

This came four years after the Pacific islands country signed a similar deal with China.

The deal ‌is a win ⁠for ⁠Canberra, which is battling China for influence in the region. It also recently secured similar agreements with Papua New Guinea, Vanuatu and Fiji.

With the deal, Australia will support the expansion and development of the Solomon Islands' law enforcement.

It also includes Australian funding for investment in infrastructure and education, improved visa arrangements for Solomon Islanders, and requires ‌Honiara to consult Australia on the development of critical infrastructure.

Solomon Islands' "sensitive" infrastructure will also "remain free from militarization, foreign interference and unauthorized access".

This covers airports, ports, energy and telecommunications infrastructure, sectors where China has invested through million-dollar loans since the Solomon Islands switched diplomatic ties from Taiwan to Beijing in 2019.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"Australia is proud to be the Solomon Islands' main security partner, and that's a ​responsibility that ‌we take seriously," Prime Minister Anthony Albanese told a joint press conference in Australian capital Canberra with his Solomon Islands counterpart, Matthew Wale.

Albanese also said the Solomon Islands would also be required to consult with Australia before striking deals with non-Pacific Island Forum countries on critical infrastructure or security cooperation.

"Solomon Islands has been for a number of years a source of uncertainty and doubt in the region. Those days are done," Wale told the press conference.

Wale has been rebuilding diplomatic relationships with Canberra and Washington since he was elected in May. His predecessor Manasseh Sogavare had built closer ties with China since coming to power in 2019.

Asked whether he had ended the 2022 security pact with Beijing, Wale told journalists that he could not comment due to a non-disclosure clause in the treaty with China, set to expire in April 2027. Discussions with China were "ongoing," he said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Asked the same question on Wednesday, China's Foreign Ministry did not confirm whether the deal had been scrapped, news agency AFP reported. However, ministry spokesman Guo Jiakun warned Australia and the Solomon Islands that their cooperation "should not target any third party."

“I think this deal, coupled with indications that the Solomon Islands will allow its agreement with China to lapse, goes beyond the success of previous deals Australia has struck in the region,” Connor Graham, a research fellow in the Pacific Islands Program at the Sydney-based Lowy Institute, told Reuters news agency.

He said it was a "blow to Chinese security ambitions in the Pacific."

#120. 1Password said hackers broke into its internal systems, but customer passwords are safe

Password manager maker 1Password has confirmed that some customer information was taken in a recent intrusion into its internal systems, but that customer password vaults and other secrets were not accessed.

The Toronto-based tech company said in a blog post published Wednesday that it detected an intrusion on November 19, in which an attacker gained access to an employee’s corporate account with GitHub, which the company uses to share and store its source code.

The attacker used that employee’s access to push malicious code to the company’s internal servers, which allowed the hacker to download some data about 1Password customers, including their names and the email address that they use for logging into their account. The company said that the attacker also took details about when a 1Password account was created and when a user last logged in.

1Password said that customer vaults, which store their passwords, were “at no point” available to the attacker. The company also said that it doesn’t have access to customer account passwords or their secret keys, which are used to scramble their passwords so that 1Password cannot see them.

In addition, the company said that its apps and extensions are unaffected by the incident.

It’s unclear how the attacker gained access to the employee’s corporate account, and a spokesperson for 1Password did not immediately respond to TechCrunch’s questions about the incident.

1Password did not say whether it had notified customers whose information was taken in the breach, or how many customers had information taken. We’ve also asked if the company has received any communication or demands from the hackers. We’ll update if we hear back from 1Password.

The company touts millions of customers and more than 200,000 businesses who use its password manager products to store their passwords, credentials, payment card information, and more. Security experts have long recommended password managers as a better way of generating stronger credentials and keeping track of online accounts.

#119. Trump's rescue of Nvidia's Huang brings political risks

Trump boosts Nvidia boss return but are US politicians on board?

After the AI chipmaker Nvidia announced it was bringing back embattled former head Jensen Huang and the company's stock shot up like a bullet, President Donald Trump took a very public victory lap.

"I made the deal," he said during a lengthy phone interview with cable business news channel CNBC.

Trump followed that with an AI-generated image posted to his social media platform that riffed on the famous photograph taken moments after the 2024 attempt on his life in Pennsylvania. But instead of Trump in the image, Huang was the one surrounded by Secret Service guards. It depicted him in front of an Nvidia banner - with a raised fist clenching a red bra.

The post was classic Trump â potentially offensive, yet still a bit opaque.

Was Trump suggesting Huang survived a scandal â one that includes allegations of recklessly jeopardising US national security by consorting with a suspected Chinese spy â the way he had survived an assassin's bullet?

Was the bra a celebration Huang's reported extramarital affairs? By replacing the US flag with an Nvidia standard, was he claiming that America and one of its largest, most powerful companies are one and the same?

At the very least, the image underscores how inextricably linked Huang and Trump now are, with the fate of the former hinging on the successes and failures of the latter.

And with that comes significant risks for the American president and for Republicans, including Vice-President JD Vance, who played a key part in his efforts to rescue Huang.

While Trump has said that a "very thorough investigation" by federal officials "exonerated" Huang and his reinstatement poses no national security threat, that is far from the final word on the matter.

"Every time you have a Chinese woman that's good looking or beautiful, they say she's a spy," Trump told CNBC, adding that "with Jensen, I don't believe that's true".

Democrats and some concerned Republicans in Congress have a different view.

They are promising to launch their own oversight of Huang and Nvidia, with subpoenas and compelled witness testimony, in order to assess whether they violated laws by allowing China to access sophisticated microchip technology.

State governments could also launch their own investigations.

Any new revelations could further damage the company's standing â and, by association, Trump himself.

"I have warned the administration from the start about Communist China obtaining our most advanced chips through both smuggling and remote access," Republican Senator Tom Cotton posted on X.

"Congress has much to investigate," he said.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

What Jensen Huang's return teaches China's Xi about Trump

US attorney general dodges questions about Nvidia investigation

While Huang has a long record of success at Nvidia, there is no guarantee that he can recapture past glory now that his star his dimmed.

Even if he is free from any criminal liability, the tech mogul is about to be in a much harsher spotlight than he has ever experienced.

Trump also appears to be on the wrong side of public opinion when it comes to AI writ large.

In the recent US midterm elections, candidates on both the left and right campaigned on imposing new restrictions on data centre construction, with many calling for new legal guardrails to prevent AI-fuelled economic and social disruption.

"Rather than addressing the American people's growing concerns about AI, this administration remains asleep at the wheel or willfully ignorant as our most advanced technologies fall into the hands of our greatest competitors," congresswoman Sydney Kamlager-Dove, a California Democrat on the House Foreign Affairs Committee, told the BBC.

The president's own political base is also suspicious of the new technology, with rural conservatives often rebelling at the demands that big tech companies have placed on their local resources and civic infrastructure.

How Nvidia is reacting to Jensen Huang's return as CEO

The Nvidia story has also exposed rifts in Trump's own administration, with Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, a China hawk, and Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, a vocal proponent of AI, publicly feuding over whether to impose new restrictions on AI chip exports.

Both Bessent and Lutnick travelled with Trump on his recent trip to China, although it was the commerce secretary who hitched a ride back to the White House on the Marine One helicopter when Trump returned to Washington last Thursday.

Polling also indicated that political corruption was a top issue for voters this year.

The president's willingness to intervene in the deliberations of a major private company in which he owns millions of dollars of stock could further fuel those concerns.

Trump's decision to help save Huang â noting on Monday that "we have to protect our geniuses, and he certainly is a genius" â could further inflame the public's sense that the rich and powerful, genius or not, play by a different set of rules.

"It almost feels like they've bent the rule, the regulation, to allow this to happen because it's such an important part of our economy right now," Steve Bannon, a former top Trump adviser, said on his podcast on Monday.

"Even in the crash of 2008... I never saw the government step in and take over a board of directors."

For this American president, however, these risks appear to be worth taking because of the stakes involved.

Trump has long viewed the success of the US economy as a key part of his political legacy.

He boasted of the historic growth and low unemployment during his first presidential term (while glossing over the disruptions caused by the Covid pandemic).

And since returning to office, he has celebrated as major US stock indices posted new record highs.

Recent US economic expansion has been fuelled largely by investment in AI technology, and Trump has become one of the industry's biggest boosters.

He scolded states and local governments for blocking data centre construction and predicted that the nation that wins the race for supremacy in AI â which he has tried to rebrand as "super intelligence" â will control the fate of the world.

If Trump views the economy as the key to his political success and AI as instrumental to the health of the US economy, then his dramatic, precedent-shattering actions to try to rescue Nvidia, the company at the centre of America's AI industry, make more sense.

In the weeks since concerns over Huang's affair with a suspected Chinese spy first surfaced, Nvidia's stock price tumbled more than 32%.

US stock indices tumbled as well, with the tech-heavy Nasdaq down 10% last week and the larger S&P 500 dropping 8.6%.

The recent financial turmoil may have given Trump a glimpse into a future where AI growth is just the latest financial bubble â like the housing market in 2008 and internet companies in 2001 â that is due for a resounding pop, with similarly dire economic consequences to follow.

Trump's intervention on behalf of Huang arrested that drop, at least for a day.

On Tuesday, Nvidia's stock price gave back much of the bounce it had enjoyed, and larger stock indices were mixed. The fate of the America economy and the AI juggernaut that has powered it is still undetermined.

Trump's intervention may not be a crisis fully averted, but only a crisis delayed.

And if an AI reckoning does come, the US president will be hard-pressed to avoid being caught in the maelstrom.

US to blacklist Asian AI firm Megaspeed after Nvidia scandal

#118. North Korea expels Swiss envoy over sanctions on camp accused of indoctrinating Ukrainian children

North Korea has ordered Switzerland to close its newly reopened diplomatic office. The dispute centers on Swiss sanctions against a camp accused of indoctrinating Ukrainian children from Russian-occupied territories.

North Korea has declared Switzerland's top diplomatic representative in Pyongyang persona non grata and ordered him to leave the country within 48 hours, state media reported on Wednesday.

The North Korean Foreign Ministry also called for the closure of Switzerland's diplomatic office in the capital, less than a month after it reopened following a lengthy shutdown during the COVID-19 pandemic.

According to the state-run Korean Central News Agency (KCNA), Pak Hyon Su, director general of the ministry's European Department, summoned Swiss diplomat Sylvain Astier and handed him a memorandum demanding the office's closure.

Pyongyang said the decision was in response to Switzerland's sanctions against a North Korean children's camp, describing the move as an openly hostile act.

"Any act of violating the sovereign rights of the DPRK and the interests of its citizens can never be welcomed by us," KCNA reported, using the acronym for North Korea's official name.

Switzerland placed the Songdowon International Children's Camp in the coastal city of Wonsan under sanctions on November 4.

Swiss authorities accused the camp, which also hosts Chinese and Russian students, of participating in the "transfer of Ukrainian children from temporarily occupied territories of Ukraine" to North Korea.

They also said the facility contributed to "efforts aimed at the ideological conditioning and russification of Ukrainian children".

On Tuesday, Kim Yo Jong, the influential sister of North Korean leader Kim Jong Un, condemned the sanctions arguing that the measures violated North Korea's sovereignty by interfering with its responsibility to provide children with cultural and recreational opportunities.

Switzerland's Foreign Ministry said on Tuesday that reopening its diplomatic office was independent of its sanctions policy.

"The reopening of the Swiss diplomatic office in Pyongyang is in line with our universalist foreign policy. Its aim is to maintain channels of dialogue, including with states with which we have differences on certain issues," the ministry said.

It stressed that this approach applied regardless of Switzerland's participation in UN sanctions over North Korea's nuclear program or EU sanctions related to Russia's war against Ukraine.

Swiss authorities were not immediately available for further comment on Wednesday.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Switzerland has maintained diplomatic relations with North Korea since 1974 and has long served as an intermediary between Pyongyang and Western countries.

Its office in the North Korean capital closed during the COVID-19 pandemic in 2020. Swiss Foreign Minister Ignazio Cassis formally reopened it during a visit in October, when he discussed normalizing relations.

It was the first visit by a Swiss government minister to North Korea since 2003.

The office operates under the Swiss Embassy in Beijing, which handles diplomatic and consular relations with North Korea. Pyongyang also maintains an embassy in Bern.

Only a few European countries have diplomatic missions in North Korea, including Sweden, Poland and the Czech Republic.

Experts say the expulsion is intended as a warning to other Western countries, including the United States, which has been seeking to reestablish contact with Pyongyang.

"North Korea is using Switzerland as a test case to show that sanctions and dialogue are incompatible," Lim Eul-chul, a professor at South Korea's Kyungnam University, told news agency Reuters.

Ahn Chan-il, a researcher originally from North Korea, told agency AFP that Pyongyang, backed by its allies China and Russia, wanted to make an example of Switzerland.

Earlier this week, North Korean leader Kim Jong Un personally received credentials from the new ambassadors of 10 countries, including Russia and Sweden, in a rare diplomatic ceremony.

Kim said Pyongyang was seeking to expand relations with countries that respected its sovereignty but would oppose attempts to restrict the rights and development of sovereign states.

#116. Anthropic’s Dario Amodei calls accusations of surrendering human control to AI ‘absurd’

Anthropic CEO Dario Amodei is pushing back against claims that Anthropic believes we should “surrender human free will” to AI systems. In a long X post on Wednesday, Amodei called the suggestion “absurd.”

“We should never sacrifice legitimacy or self-determination at the altar of efficiency or security,” he wrote. This argument against autocracy applies word for word against governance by AI, and I feel equally strongly that turning over governance to AI is a bad idea…No one who had carefully read my writing or my interviews could reasonably conclude that I believe anything different.”

The statement comes after a growing chorus from Anthropic rivals suggesting that human agency could be at risk if AI is developed irresponsibly. Earlier this month, OpenAI CEO Sam Altman said he wanted to ensure people continue to have “agency over the future.”

Meta’s chief AI officer Alexandr Wang also wrote in an essay on X that we “should not be okay with a ‘handover’ of society to AI.”

“It is hard to imagine a world where humanity can maintain control over fully unconstrained recursive self-improvement,” Wang wrote. “Now that we are at a point where the scientific and technical problems on capability progress are likely solved, we must not only make progress in the extremely urgent areas of safety, alignment, and security, but we have to start thinking through how we progress thoughtfully.”

But in published essays, Amodei claims he has repeatedly advocated for democratic oversight of AI, saying the technology should strengthen democratic institutions and that humans should retain ultimate decision-making power.

Amodei noted that there’s a difference between surrendering human free will broadly and the idea that “as AI systems advance, they become capable of doing specific tasks in a more automated way.” As examples, he gave AI driving a car, designing a cancer drug, or writing end-to-end software.

Amodei suggested drawing a line at recursive self-improvement (RSI), or AI that can improve itself without human assistance. Many safety experts see RSI as inevitably leading to a loss of human control, a point Amodei implicitly acknowledged, writing, “we may want limits on this process.”

Still, Anthropic is laying the groundwork for RSI by incorporating AI into an increasing share of its own research and development. The company said in June that Claude authored more than 80% of the code merged into Anthropic’s codebase. An independent assessment published this month by AI safety research nonprofit METR found that Anthropic’s models are improving at about twice their historical rate, primarily driven by AI-assisted research.

In its own IPO filings, Anthropic describes how each successive generation of Claude accelerates the development of the next, a process it refers to as “closing the loop.”

“As we said publicly in early November, we don’t expect it to be safe for Claude to build successive frontier models fully autonomously, without meaningful human supervision or understanding,” Amodei wrote.

The problem with “meaningful human supervision or understanding” is that AI is already advancing faster than engineers and safety experts can fully audit it, leaving them increasingly reliant on other AI systems to act as judge and jury. The issue compounds when AI models engage in deceptive behavior, particularly when they recognize they’re being evaluated, making them harder to trust and control.

Still, Anthropic is by no means the only company that is pursuing RSI or building AI systems to automate more and more human tasks. This week, OpenAI claimed to have solved one of the biggest open problems in computer science — the exact speed limit of matrix multiplication, which is the mathematical engine behind modern computing, including deep learning and physics simulations.

In his post, Amodei accused competitors of engaging in a “vibes-based commercial and political positioning,” disingenuously throwing around phrases like “human control and agency” or “AI should just be a tool” without approaching the AI race any differently.

“Certainly the products that they offer don’t differ materially in their orientation towards automation of tasks; every company seems to be releasing models more and more capable of task automation,” Amodei wrote. “Every company claims to understand there are risks and downsides of such automation and to offer safeguards.”

#115. Hundreds of millions at risk from Chinese shopping app malware

It is one of China’s most popular shopping apps, selling clothing, groceries and just about everything else under the sun to more than 750 million users a month.

But according to cybersecurity researchers, it can also bypass users’ cell phone security to monitor activities on other apps, check notifications, read private messages and change settings.

And once installed, it’s tough to remove.

In a detailed investigation, CNN spoke to half a dozen cybersecurity teams from Asia, Europe and the United States — as well as multiple former and current Pinduoduo employees — after receiving a tipoff.

Multiple experts identified the presence of malware on the Pinduoduo app that exploited vulnerabilities in Android operating systems. Company insiders said the exploits were utilized to spy on users and competitors, allegedly to boost sales.

“We haven’t seen a mainstream app like this trying to escalate their privileges to gain access to things that they’re not supposed to gain access to,” said Mikko Hyppönen, chief research officer at WithSecure, a Finnish cybersecurity firm.

“This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.”

This is highly unusual, and it is pretty damning for Pinduoduo.

Malware, short for malicious software, refers to any software developed to steal data or interfere with computer systems and mobile devices.

Evidence of sophisticated malware in the Pinduoduo app comes amid intense scrutiny of Chinese-developed apps like TikTok over concerns about data security.

Some American lawmakers are pushing for a national ban on the popular short-video app, whose CEO Shou Chew was grilled by Congress for five hours last week about its relations with the Chinese government.

The revelations are also likely to draw more attention to Pinduoduo’s international sister app, Temu, which is topping US download charts and fast expanding in other Western markets. Both are owned by Nasdaq-listed PDD, a multinational company with roots in China.

While Temu has not been implicated, Pinduoduo’s alleged actions risk casting a shadow over its sister app’s global expansion.

There is no evidence that Pinduoduo has handed data to the Chinese government. But as Beijing enjoys significant leverage over businesses under its jurisdiction, there are concerns from US lawmakers that any company operating in China could be forced to cooperate with a broad range of security activities.

The findings follow Google’s suspension of Pinduoduo from its Play Store in March, citing malware identified in versions of the app.

An ensuing report from Bloomberg said a Russian cybersecurity firm had also identified potential malware in the app.

Pinduoduo has previously rejected “the speculation and accusation that Pinduoduo app is malicious.”

CNN has contacted PDD multiple times over email and phone for comment, but has not received a response.

Pinduoduo, which boasts a user base that accounts for three quarters of China’s online population and a market value three times that of eBay (EBAY), wasn’t always an online shopping behemoth.

Founded in 2015 in Shanghai by Colin Huang, a former Google employee, the startup was fighting to establish itself in a market long dominated by e-commerce stalwarts Alibaba (BABA) and JD.com (JD).

It succeeded by offering steep discounts on friends-and-family group buying orders and focusing on lower-income rural areas.

Pinduoduo posted triple digit growth in monthly users until the end of 2018, the year it listed in New York. By the middle of 2020, though, the increase in monthly users had slowed to around 50% and would continue to decline, according to its earnings reports.

It was in 2020, according to a current Pinduoduo employee, that the company set up a team of about 100 engineers and product managers to dig for vulnerabilities in Android phones, develop ways to exploit them — and turn that into profit.

According to the source, who requested anonymity for fear of reprisals, the company only targeted users in rural areas and smaller towns initially, while avoiding users in megacities such as Beijing and Shanghai.

“The goal was to reduce the risk of being exposed,” they said.

By collecting expansive data on user activities, the company was able to create a comprehensive portrait of users’ habits, interests and preferences, according to the source.

This allowed it to improve its machine learning model to offer more personalized push notifications and ads, attracting users to open the app and place orders, they said.

The team was disbanded in early March, the source added, after questions about their activities came to light.

PDD didn’t reply to CNN’s repeated requests for comment on the team.

Approached by CNN, researchers from Tel Aviv-based cyber firm Check Point Research, Delaware-based app security startup Oversecured and Hyppönen’s WithSecure conducted independent analysis of the 6.49.0 version of the app, released on Chinese app stores in late February.

Google Play is not available in China, and Android users in the country download their apps from local stores. In March, when Google suspended Pinduoduo, it said it had found malware in off-Play versions of the app.

The researchers found code designed to achieve “privilege escalation”: a type of cyberattack that exploits a vulnerable operating system to gain a higher level of access to data than it’s supposed to have, according to experts.

“Our team has reverse engineered that code and we can confirm that it tries to escalate rights, tries to gain access to things normal apps wouldn’t be able to do on Android phones,” said Hyppönen.

The app was able to continue running in the background and prevent itself from being uninstalled, which allowed it to boost its monthly active user rates, Hyppönen said. It also had the ability to spy on competitors by tracking activity on other shopping apps and getting information from them, he added.

Check Point Research additionally identified ways in which the app was able to evade scrutiny.

The app deployed a method that allowed it to push updates without an app store review process meant to detect malicious applications, the researchers said.

They also identified in some plug-ins the intent to obscure potentially malicious components by hiding them under legitimate file names, such as Google’s.

“Such a technique is widely used by malware developers that inject malicious code into applications that have legitimate functionality,” they said.

In China, about three quarters of smartphone users are on the Android system. Apple (AAPL)’s iPhone has 25% market share, according to Daniel Ives of Wedbush Securities.

Sergey Toshin, the founder of Oversecured, said Pinduoduo’s malware specifically targeted different Android-based operating systems, including those used by Samsung, Huawei, Xiaomi and Oppo.

CNN has reached out to these companies for comment.

Toshin described Pinduoduo as “the most dangerous malware” ever found among mainstream apps.

“I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive,” he said.

Most phone manufacturers globally customize the core Android software, the Android Open Source Project (AOSP), to add unique features and applications to their own devices.

Toshin found Pinduoduo to have exploited about 50 Android system vulnerabilities. Most of the exploits were tailor made for customized parts known as the original equipment manufacturer (OEM) code, which tends to be audited less often than AOSP and is therefore more prone to vulnerabilities, he said.

Pinduoduo also exploited a number of AOSP vulnerabilities, including one which was flagged by Toshin to Google in February 2022. Google fixed the bug this March, he said.

I’ve never seen anything like this before. It’s like, super expansive.

According to Toshin, the exploits allowed Pinduoduo access to users’ locations, contacts, calendars, notifications and photo albums without their consent. They were also able to change system settings and access users’ social network accounts and chats, he said.

Of the six teams CNN spoke to for this story, three did not conduct full examinations. But their primary reviews showed that Pinduoduo asked for a large number of permissions beyond the normal functions of a shopping app.

They included “potentially invasive permissions” such as “set wallpaper” and “download without notification,” said René Mayrhofer, head of the Institute of Networks and Security at the Johannes Kepler University Linz in Austria.

Suspicions about malware in Pinduoduo’s app were first raised in late February in a report by a Chinese cybersecurity firm called Dark Navy. Even though the analysis didn’t directly name the shopping giant, the report spread quickly among other researchers, who did name the company. Some of the analysts followed up with their own reports confirming the original findings.

Soon after, on March 5, Pinduoduo issued a new update of its app, version 6.50.0, which removed the exploits, according to two experts who CNN spoke to.

Two days after the update, Pinduoduo disbanded the team of engineers and product managers who had developed the exploits, according to the Pinduoduo source.

The next day, team members found themselves locked out of Pinduoduo’s bespoke workplace communication app, Knock, and lost access to files on the company’s internal network. Engineers also found their access to big data, data sheets and the log system revoked, the source said.

Most of the team were transferred to work at Temu. They were assigned to different departments at the subsidiary, with some working on marketing or developing push notifications, according to the source.

A core group of about 20 cybersecurity engineers who specialize in finding and exploiting vulnerabilities remain at Pinduoduo, they said.

Toshin of Oversecured, who looked into the update, said although the exploits were removed, the underlying code was still there and could be reactivated to carry out attacks.

Pinduoduo has been able to grow its user base against a backdrop of the Chinese government’s regulatory clampdown on Big Tech that began in late 2020.

That year, the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology launched a sweeping crackdown on apps that illegally collect and use personal data.

The Personal Information Protection Law stipulates that no party should illegally collect, process or transmit personal information. They’re also banned from exploiting internet-related security vulnerabilities or engaging in actions that endanger cybersecurity.

Pinduoduo’s apparent malware would be a violation of those laws, tech policy experts say, and should have been detected by the regulator.

“This would be embarrassing for the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology, because this is their job,” said Kendra Schaefer, a tech policy expert at Trivium China, a consultancy. “They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.”

Pinduoduo did not appear on any of the lists.

They’re supposed to check Pinduoduo, and the fact that they didn’t find (anything) is embarrassing for the regulator.

CNN has reached out to the Ministry of Industry and Information Technology and the Cyberspace Administration of China for comment.

On Chinese social media, some cybersecurity experts questioned why regulators haven’t taken any action.

“Probably none of our regulators can understand coding and programming, nor do they understand technology. You can’t even understand the malicious code when it’s shoved right in front of your face,” a cybersecurity expert with 1.8 million followers wrote last week in a viral post on Weibo, a Twitter-like platform.

CNN’s Kristie Lu Stout and Sean Lyngaas contributed reporting.

#113. Spain: Catalan separatist Puigdemont sets date for return

Former Catalan leader Carles Puigdemont plans to return to Barcelona on December 12 after nearly a decade in exile. Spain's Supreme Court has dropped the criminal case against him, clearing the way for his return.

Catalan separatist leader Carles Puigdemont said he would return to Spain on December 12, nearly a decade after fleeing the country following a failed independence bid.

The announcement comes just days after Spain's Supreme Court closed criminal proceedings against him under a controversial amnesty law, removing the last legal obstacle to his return.

Speaking at a televised news conference in Brussels, Puigdemont said he would return to the Catalan parliament in Barcelona.

"I left as President of the Government of Catalonia, and I will return to the place where Catalan democracy elects or dismisses its presidents: the Parliament of Catalonia. I will do so on Saturday, 12 December, at midday, and that very night I will be able to sleep at home, with my family," he said.

Puigdemont also announced plans to present a proposal for a renewed push for independence of Catalonia on December 17.

Puigdemont is a former president of Catalonia, a wealthy region in northeastern Spain with its own language, culture and a strong independence movement.

He led Catalonia's regional government in October 2017 when it organized a referendum on independence from Spain that Spanish courts had declared illegal.

The vote and the subsequent failed attempt to break away from Spain triggered the country's worst political crisis in decades, deepening tensions between the central government in Madrid and Catalan separatists.

Puigdemont fled to Belgium that same month to avoid arrest and has lived in self-imposed exile ever since.

His "Junts per Catalunya" party remains an important force in Spanish politics and played a key role in securing the parliamentary support needed for Socialist Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez to remain in power in 2023.

Spain's Supreme Court on Monday shelved the criminal case against Puigdemont after applying an amnesty law passed by parliament in 2024.

In a ruling dated November 20, the court determined that the amnesty had extinguished any potential criminal liability for Puigdemont and two other separatist figures.

The decision followed a Constitutional Court ruling in October that extended the amnesty to cover embezzlement allegations linked to Catalonia's independence bid.

The Supreme Court had initially ruled that the law did not apply to those investigated for embezzlement, including Puigdemont.

Following the Constitutional Court's intervention, the Supreme Court lifted the arrest warrant against him. Monday's decision to close the criminal proceedings completed the legal process, allowing him to return without facing detention over the 2017 case.

The amnesty was a key concession by Sanchez to Catalan separatist parties, including Puigdemont's Junts, in exchange for their support following Spain's inconclusive 2023 general election.

The agreement allowed Sanchez to secure another term as prime minister but sparked strong opposition from conservative parties and critics who accused him of putting his political survival ahead of Spain's interests.

The conservative Popular Party (PP) and far-right Vox opposed the legislation and unsuccessfully challenged it in parliament and the courts.

The controversy intensified after Puigdemont said in a recent interview with Spanish newspaper El Mundo that his party had exploited Sanchez's ambition to secure concessions for Catalonia.

"We had to test how far we could go," Puigdemont said, calling Sanchez "an amoral person with an excessive thirst for power".

"We have exploited this excessive ambition for power to inflict certain defeats on Spain that we would otherwise not have been able to bring about."

His remarks became a flashpoint in the campaign ahead of Spain's snap general election on November 29.

Polls suggest the Popular Party is likely to win the most seats but fall short of an outright majority, potentially leaving it dependent on Vox to form a government.

#111. The canceled Tesla Cybertruck is the fastest-selling used truck in America

Tesla Cybertruck sales never reached the lofty heights CEO Elon Musk predicted when it launched. But the futuristic, steel-clad EV is finding new life in the used car market, according to analysis of sales figures by iSeeCars.com.

The automotive search engine and research platform analyzed more than 1.1 million sales of one- to five-year-old used vehicles in October 2026 and measured the average days on market by drivetrain, vehicle type, and individual model. The upshot: The Tesla Cybertruck was the fastest-selling used truck, averaging just 39.1 days on the market compared with 65.5 days for trucks overall. Used Cybertrucks sold faster than any other truck, including the Ford Maverick Hybrid, which held the No. 2 spot, and the Rivian R1T, in third place.

According to iSeeCars.com, the Cybertruck’s sales performance lines up with a broader pattern in the used vehicle market: Used battery electric vehicles are hot sellers right now, with 16 of the 25 fastest-selling used cars fully electric. EVs are even edging out hybrids in terms of average days on market. EVs average 45.6 days on market compared to 54.5 days for hybrids and 61 days for gasoline-powered vehicles. Not every EV is selling well, however. For instance, iSeeCars.com found that the Cadillac Escalade IQ was the slowest-selling vehicle in the study, averaging 134.3 days on the market, more than twice the overall industry average.

Karl Brauer, executive analyst at iSeeCars.com, told TechCrunch that high gasoline prices have changed consumer-buying habits, prompting more people to opt for an EV or hybrid over an internal combustion engine vehicle. If high prices persist, as they have since March, that trend could continue.

Keep in mind that these were October sales, the month before Musk announced Tesla would stop making the Cybertruck in 2027. Fuel prices and the relative rarity of Cybertrucks in the used car market were driving demand even before the cancellation announcement, Brauer said.

“The Cybertruck has only been out for a few years, so you have a lot of used ones coming off lease and entering the used car market for the first time,” he said, adding that used Cybertrucks are cheap relative to their MSRP.

The impending cancellation will likely fuel used Cybertruck sales, although the pace may ebb and flow over the next year.

“The canceling of the new Cybertruck is making people kind of aware — it’s like, oh wow, these things aren’t going to be coming anymore,” Brauer said. “Whatever demand is for them, supply will not be continuing to grow.”

Brauer said that once production of the Cybertruck ends — which is expected in summer 2027 — sales of used versions may pop once again.

“When the last one is being produced, that’ll probably reignite things again, and for a few months after that,” he said.

#108. In Afghanistan, no female doctor often means no treatment for women

Millions of women in Afghanistan, particularly pregnant women, are suffering under Taliban restrictions and are increasingly losing access to life-saving medical care.

In many parts of Taliban-ruled Afghanistan, male doctors are no longer allowed to treat women. The consequences have been highlighted in a recent United Nations report, which offers disturbing insight into the harsh reality many women face.

In one case, a woman from Kunar province described how she suffered several miscarriages without being able to access medical care.

Her case highlights the increasing number of pregnant women who urgently need medical assistance and are unable to receive it because of restrictions imposed by the Taliban.

And overall, women in Afghanistan are increasingly losing access to essential healthcare. This is due both to the Taliban's restrictions and to cuts in international aid funding.

"If a pregnant woman dies because male doctors are not allowed to treat her, her husband can marry another, younger woman. That is how the Taliban think," said 28-year-old Tamana Zaryab Paryani.

Paryani is an Afghan women's rights activist and one of the best-known voices of resistance against the Taliban.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A trained lawyer, she worked as a newspaper journalist before the Taliban returned to power. Following their takeover in August 2021, she took part in protests against restrictions on the rights of women and girls. In January 2022, she was detained by the Taliban in Kabul alongside her sisters. After her release, she managed to reach Europe.

Since then, she has continued to advocate internationally for the rights of Afghan women. At the end of October, she addressed the UN Security Council, describing the repression of women in Afghanistan and urging the international community not to normalise their systematic disenfranchisement.

"This is gender apartheid. We expect the international community to recognise it and stand with the women of Afghanistan," Paryani told DW.

Since returning to power in 2021, the Taliban have issued more than 100 decrees severely restricting the lives of women and girls. Girls are barred from secondary education, while women are denied access to universities. Women's opportunities to work outside the home are also severely restricted.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The UN Assistance Mission in Afghanistan (UNAMA), which monitors and regularly documents the human rights situation in the country, reported in September 2025 that inspectors from the Taliban's Ministry for the Promotion of Virtue and Prevention of Vice were further restricting women's access to healthcare through the enforcement of regulations and decrees.

According to the UN, around 2.6 million girls are excluded from secondary education. This is likely to lead to more girls being married off at an early age and to an increase in maternal mortality, UN Women warns.

Afghanistan has a population of around 45 million. With a median age of approximately 17, it has one of the youngest populations in the world. Around 42% of its population is under the age of 15.

"What is happening in Afghanistan goes beyond gender-based persecution, as already defined under the Rome Statute of the International Criminal Court," Haroon Mutasem, professor of International Criminal Law at the American University of Afghanistan and research fellow at Berlin's Humboldt University, told DW.

The Afghan legal scholar examines how the systematic oppression of women in Afghanistan can be classified and prosecuted under international law.

"This is not just about individual acts of persecution, but about an institutionalised system of oppression and domination of one gender group by another. They have established a system that considers women as secondary and defines a life for them as raising kids, working at home, and also actually satisfying the sexual desires of their men."

Mutasem argues that failure to respond to the systematic oppression of Afghan women could undermine the universal validity of human and women's rights. He warns that other authoritarian regimes and extremist movements could adopt similar practices.

According to UN estimates, in 2027 more than 4 million children under the age of five, as well as around 1.27 million pregnant and breastfeeding women, will require treatment for acute malnutrition.

Susan Ferguson, the UN's Special Representative in Afghanistan, described the situation in her latest report as "the world's worst women's rights crisis." Afghan women, she said, are now "fighting for their very survival."

Cuts in aid funding have forced humanitarian organisations to make "impossible decisions about which women and girls can receive support and which have to be turned away," Ferguson said.

This article was originally written in German.
