#107. Jensen Huang broke Nvidia’s code of conduct, and his employees may not forget it

When Jensen Huang gave his first all-hands address since returning as CEO, there was an odd emphasis on Nvidia’s code of conduct. He admitted to violating the code, then emphasized that no one was exempt from it. But that “does not mean, the moment you violate a code of conduct, we walk you out the door,” Huang said, according to Wired. “Facts, circumstances, a lot of assessments” still matter. In other words, the code applies to everyone, but breaking it doesn’t automatically cost anyone their job, including, apparently, the CEO.

It’s an awkward stance for a company whose CEO has admitted to breaking its rules. A central part of modern human resources, a code of conduct can seem like a quasi-legal rulebook that lays out unacceptable behavior. But experts say most codes are better understood as a reflection of corporate culture. When an infraction goes unpunished, the main risk isn’t legal but cultural — that employees will feel embarrassed or even angry about the company they work for. That’s the risk Huang now faces at Nvidia.

Matt Plass, chief market strategy officer at LRN, an ethics and compliance training outfit, recently surveyed more than 2,000 workers across different industries for a study on workplace codes of conduct. As Plass sees it, the biggest risk is that employees will become ashamed of their workplace.

“People coming into the workforce now, ethics is incredibly important to them,” he told TechCrunch. “They’re really mindful of the ethical standards of their company. They’re really embarrassed and angry when senior leadership lets them down.”

The consequences of violating the rules aren’t always tangible, but they’re definitely real. Plass gave one example of a company that banned corporate travel to cut costs — only to have its CEO turn up in a VIP box at the Olympics.

“For over a decade afterwards, ‘the Olympics’ was used within that company as an example of the worst thing an employee could do. People would say, ‘Yeah, I shouldn’t have done that, but it’s not like I went to the Olympics.’”

Plass says companies that remove an executive typically do so after an investigation, then try to win back employees. “In most organizations they make a change at the top based on what’s happened,” he said, “and then they will have a hearts-and-minds initiative, to go out to their employee base and their customers and their investors and everybody else, and say, we get it. We’re sorry. We’ve changed. That’s essentially what they do.”

Of course, that playbook is harder to follow at a company like Nvidia, where Huang left without a clear succession plan, and there are serious doubts about whether the company could continue to thrive without him. In cases like that, Plass acknowledges there’s no easy answer.

“I would draw a distinction there between the kind of companies where the CEO is a kind of godhead figure … where the person and the company are almost impossible to separate,” he said. “There it’s much, much harder to deal with.”

In that context, Huang’s stutter-step over the code of conduct is easier to understand. Judging by the employee campaign for his reinstatement, Huang’s return hasn’t created a morale problem so far. But as the coming months put more pressure on Huang and his colleagues, sidestepping the code could end up costing Nvidia something harder to measure — its employees’ pride in the company.

#103. Finland's Prime Minister Sanna Marin concedes election

Finland’s left-wing Prime Minister Sanna Marin conceded defeat on Sunday in the Nordic country’s parliamentary election as the opposition right-wing National Coalition Party (NCP) claimed victory in a tightly fought contest.

The pro-business NCP was expected to win 48 of the 200 seats in parliament, narrowly ahead of the nationalist Finns Party with 46 seats and Marin’s Social Democrats on 43 seats, justice ministry election data showed with all ballots counted.

“We got the biggest mandate,” NCP leader Petteri Orpo said in a speech to followers, vowing to “fix Finland” and its economy.

He will get the first chance at forming a coalition to obtain majority in parliament as Marin’s era as prime minister was expected to end.

Popular abroad, at home Marin faces battle to keep her job

“We have gained support, we have gained more seats (in parliament). That’s an excellent achievement, even if we did not finish first today,” the prime minister said in a speech to party members.

Marin, 37, the world’s youngest prime minister when she took office in 2019, is considered by fans around the globe as a millennial role model for progressive new leaders, but at home she has faced criticism for her partying and her government’s public spending.

Finnish Prime Minister Sanna Marin takes drug test following partying backlash

While she remains very popular among many Finns, particularly young moderates, she antagonized some conservatives with lavish spending on pensions and education they see as not frugal enough.

The NCP has led in polls for almost two years although its lead had melted away in recent months. It has promised to curb spending and stop the rise of public debt, which has reached just over 70% of GDP since Marin took office in 2019.

Orpo accused Marin of eroding Finland’s economic resilience at a time when Europe’s energy crisis, driven by Russia’s war in Ukraine, has hit the country hard and the cost of living has increased.

Orpo has said he will negotiate with all groups to obtain a majority in parliament, while Marin has said her Social Democrats may govern with the NCP but will not go into government with the Finns Party.

Marin called the Finns Party “openly racist” during a debate in January – an accusation the nationalist group rejected.

The Finns Party’s main goal is to reduce what leader Riikka Purra has called “harmful” immigration from developing countries outside the European Union. It also calls for austerity policies to curb deficit spending, a stance it shares with the NCP.

Most notable of Marin’s foreign policy actions has been her push, along with President Sauli Niinisto, for the country to make a watershed policy U-turn by seeking NATO membership in the wake of Russia’s invasion of Ukraine.

That process is now almost complete, with Helsinki expected to join within days after all the Western defense alliance’s 30 members approved the accession.

#100. FCC approves SpaceX plan to put AI data centers in space

SpaceX was given conditional approval by the Federal Communications Commission on Wednesday to deploy and operate a constellation of up to 1 million satellites for its orbital data center business, a regulatory victory for Elon Musk and his bid to move AI data centers into space.

The FCC authorization was granted despite objections from Amazon, Viasat, and the American Astronomical Society, which flagged concerns over orbital debris and congestion, radiofrequency interference, and environmental consequences. Amazon, which is building a competitor to SpaceX’s Starlink service, argued in its petition that the application lacked basic details and described it as a “lofty ambition rather than a real plan.” Despite that lack of detail, Amazon also said the proposal “risks real harm.”

The FCC dismissed some of the key concerns raised by opponents, including potential threats to scientific research, because the agency determined they fell outside its regulatory authority.

FCC Chair Brendan Carr said in a statement that the action “opens a bold new frontier for the computing infrastructure needed to power America’s leadership and growth.”

Musk thanked Carr in a post on X, writing “Much appreciate. This will be a great asset to America and the world.”

This initial regulatory approval doesn’t mean orbital data centers are imminent.

SpaceX faces a number of technical and economic challenges, including how to dramatically reduce the cost of manufacturing and launching satellites, cool processors in space, and even how to secure financing for chips that are destined for orbit.

The company must also clear other regulatory hurdles. The FCC has not yet approved SpaceX’s plans to handle orbital debris for satellites that would operate above 600 kilometers, for instance. SpaceX requested a waiver of existing FCC rules, which requires satellites ending missions below 2,000 kilometers reenter Earth’s atmosphere within five years of completing those missions. The company is interested in moving some of these retired satellites into other orbits around Earth or even around the sun, according to the FCC authorization document.

SpaceX has said it plans to deploy the satellites, known as Starmind, at altitudes ranging from 500 to 1,002 kilometers. The FCC’s approval gives SpaceX the authority to use radiofrequency communications to maintain contact with and control its orbital data centers from Earth if their primary communications systems fail. The Starmind satellites would primarily communicate using lasers to connect with other satellites, including SpaceX’s Starlink.

The FCC has given SpaceX time to sort out these issues. Under the approval, SpaceX has seven years to get its first operational satellite into orbit and 14 years to deploy the entire constellation.

#97. Britain's pound is beating every other major currency this year

The British pound crashed to a record low last fall as investors rebelled against budget plans by former Prime Minister Liz Truss. Now, it’s enjoying a comeback.

Sterling hit its highest level against the US dollar in 10 months on Tuesday, topping $1.25 for the first time since June 2022. The pound, which has advanced about 3.3% versus the greenback since the start of 2023, is the best-performing currency among developed economies this year.

The UK currency has been boosted by indications the country’s economy is holding up better than expected. Activity is now thought to have expanded 0.1% in the final three months of last year, up from a previous estimate of no growth at all. Gross domestic product growth in January has been estimated at 0.3% after dropping 0.5% in December.

This resilience is bolstering expectations the Bank of England will maintain aggressive interest rate hikes despite concerns about the health of the global banking sector. Rising rates can boost the domestic currency because they help attract foreign investors searching for higher returns.

Inflation in the United Kingdom also jumped to an annual rate of 10.4% in February, underscoring the need for the Bank of England to maintain its tough approach.

The pound plunged close to $1.03 in September 2022 after the Truss government unveiled plans to boost borrowing while slashing taxes, unleashing panic in financial markets that fueled fears of a recession in the United Kingdom.

The International Monetary Fund predicted in January that the UK economy would contract by 0.6% this year, while all other advanced economies would grow, if only slightly.

“There was a lot of pessimism being priced into the pound,” said Francesco Pesole, a currency strategist at ING.

But the sharp pullback in energy prices and China’s reopening have provided some relief about the economic outlook since the start of the year.

“There was a big re-rating of growth expectations around Europe, and that impacted the UK,” Pesole said.

The euro has also been lifted by these dynamics, rising 2.3% against the US dollar in 2023. The pound’s rally has been sharper in large part because its 2022 declines were more severe, according to Pesole.

Both currencies have been aided by the greenback’s sharp drop from highs reached last September as recession fears have percolated in the United States.

A lack of clarity around the Federal Reserve’s next steps has also restrained the dollar in recent weeks. Investor speculation has increased that the Fed could pause or stop rate hikes due to concerns about the economy following the failure of Silicon Valley Bank last month.

Jordan Rochester, a currency strategist at Nomura, said he thinks the pound could rise to $1.30 this year and “potentially higher.” But he still sees risks given the uncertainty surrounding the Bank of England’s plans and how rate rises will feed back through the country’s economy. And Pesole cautioned that currency fluctuations are often overdone when markets are choppy, as they are now.

“In a volatile market environment, moves are exacerbated,” he said.

#94. Why Indian nationals are still ending up in Russia's war on Ukraine

Whether through deception or desperation, Indians continue to be drawn into Russia's war in Ukraine. What can New Delhi do to protect its citizens from ending up fighting in Ukraine?

Gurpreet Singh Randhawa, a 40-year-old from Jalandhar in India's northern Punjab state, said he traveled to Russia for work after responding to a Facebook advertisement for a driving job at a garment factory.

But instead of finding work as a driver, he was sent to fight in Ukraine for the Russian armed forces.

After escaping from his unit, he surrendered to Ukrainian forces in November. He is now being held as a prisoner of war.

In an interview with Ukrainian YouTuber Volodymyr Zolkin, Randhawa described how, after arriving in Russia, he was taken to a military facility, made to sign a contract and sent for training. He said they confiscated his passport and phone. His account has not been independently verified.

Randhawa's story is a familiar one in India.

He is the second Indian national known to have escaped the Russian military. Sahil Majothi, a 23-year-old from Gujarat, surrendered to Ukrainian forces in October 2025. He had joined the Russian military while serving a prison sentence in Russia, reportedly believing that military service would help him secure his release.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The cases underline how little has changed since Prime Minister Narendra Modi raised the issue with Russian President Vladimir Putin during his visit to Moscow in July 2024.

At the time, Putin assured him that Indians who were misled into joining the Russian army would be discharged. But the recruitment of Indians has continued.

In an October filing before the Supreme Court, India's Ministry of External Affairs (MEA) said approximately 267 Indians have joined the Russian armed forces since the start of Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

Of these, 139 were discharged and 51 were killed. The whereabouts of the 77 others were unknown. Russian authorities have confirmed that seven of them are missing.

The problem extends beyond Indians who travel to Russia seeking civilian employment, only to find themselves exposed to recruitment networks and the dangers of a war zone.

The MEA has also highlighted cases of Indians knowingly enlisting in the Russian military for the supposed financial benefits on offer, believing they could later secure release through government intervention.

Some had also approached Indian authorities to renounce their citizenship and obtain Russian passports. "This is a dangerous path and is strongly discouraged by the government," the ministry said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Former diplomat Anil Trigunayat said the growing movement of Indian workers to Russia presents both economic opportunities and new challenges for their protection.

"Inadequate oversight of recruitment networks could leave vulnerable workers exposed to exploitation," Trigunayat told DW.

He said Indian workers were often burdened by substantial recruitment fees and continued deductions from their salaries by intermediaries.

"Some of them had paid between 300,000 to 500,000 Indian rupees [$5,400 /€4,900] to Indian recruitment agents, and even while working in Russia, more than 30% of their agreed monthly salary was taken away by local and foreign agents," Trigunayat said.

The former diplomat added that some Indians who had overstayed their visas in Russia were particularly vulnerable. Military service was allegedly offered to earn substantial wages and regularize their immigration status.

Mohammad Sufiyan, from Hyderabad in the southern state of Telangana, said he was lured to Russia under false pretences and forced into active combat in Ukraine.

He had traveled abroad in search of employment and a better livelihood, but ended up in a war zone. He has since returned home to India.

"Many people are duped. Some decide it is better to seek citizenship than come back to India. It's a tough life, and people will go to any lengths to get some sort of employment," Sufiyan told DW.

Sufiyan's experience points to the economic desperation that makes such offers attractive, even when they carry the risk of being caught up in a war.

Rajan Kumar, a professor at the Center for Russian and Central Asian Studies at Jawaharlal Nehru University's School of International Studies, said some Indians who traveled to Russia for work could find themselves caught up in the fighting without having joined the military.

"Some of them, they don't join the army, but they end up in the army region. They're caught in the crossfire," Kumar told DW.

He said India was negotiating with Moscow to secure the return of its citizens caught up in such situations. "Russia is very cooperative so far," he said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

India has been pressing Russia to take action, but the issue persists.

The recruitment efforts reflect Russia's need for more manpower for its nearly five-year war in Ukraine , and the limited influence New Delhi can exert over Moscow, despite the close political relationship between the two countries.

Mohsin Raza Khan, a professor at the Jindal School of International Affairs, told DW that Putin's need to sustain the war effort was taking precedence over the concerns of a strategic partner.

He points out that the Russian president was reluctant to impose another mobilization on his own population for fear of domestic unrest.

"In these circumstances, sustaining the war effort appears to take precedence over concerns raised by India. Neither Modi nor New Delhi has sufficient leverage to change that," said Khan.

Khan also said the willingness of some Indians to enlist, and even consider giving up their citizenship, is down to the lack of decent employment opportunities at home.

"For young, educated men without contacts or connections, the chances of respectable employment are so low that the risks of a high casualty war are preferable," he said.

#93. South Africa's Ramaphosa wins court battle over impeachment threat

President Cyril Ramaphosa has faced calls to resign over his handling of the "Farmgate" scandal

South African President Cyril Ramaphosa has won a legal battle to overturn a report that found he may have a case to answer over the theft of large sums of cash from his private farm.

In 2022, an independent panel said Ramaphosa may have committed serious misconduct relating to the theft of $580,000 (Â£443,000), hidden in a sofa, at his Phala Phala game farm in the Limpopo province. He denied any wrongdoing.

Ramaphosa challenged the findings of the report in court after the Constitutional Court ruled that parliament had violated the constitution by blocking moves to impeach him.

The Western Cape high court ruled that the panel's report was unlawful and set it aside "in its entirety".

In its judgment, the court said the panel, appointed by parliament to investigate the allegations against Ramaphosa, had "misconstrued its function, the nature of the inquiry it was to conduct and the recommendation it was required to make".

"It went beyond its lawful remit ... and made several material errors of law. [Therefore] the report of the independent panel ... is declared unlawful and is reviewed and set aside in its entirety," Judge Nolwazi Mabindla-Boqwana said.

The court also declared unlawful the steps taken by parliament in relation to the report.

Political analyst Khanyi Magubane said the court ruling was a victory for Ramaphosa as it gave him "political and constitutional credibility".

She made it clear that the ruling was not a criminal finding on possible improper conduct at the farm but rather an indictment at the independent panel's report.

"[So] he does get significant reprieve ... but there will always be the shadow of the Phala Phala report," Magubane said.

The ruling effectively removes the basis for the parliamentary impeachment process against Ramaphosa, although opposition parties have indicated they intend to challenge the decision.

The scandal, dubbed "Farmgate" by local media, has has dogged Ramaphosa since it emerged in 2022, when former spy chief Arthur Fraser reported the matter to police in a dossier.

Fraser, an ally of former President Jacob Zuma, accused Ramaphosa of concealing the theft from the police and tax authorities.

The break-in took place in 2020, when thieves allegedly stole bundles of US dollar bills hidden inside a sofa at the farm.

Opposition parties, pictured above, have been vocal in their call for the president to account for his role in the saga

Earlier this month, two people were found guilty of the theft.

Ramaphosa confirmed that a robbery had taken place but said the money had been from the legitimate sale of buffalo from his livestock business.

Several investigations cleared him of any wrongdoing, but parliament began an impeachment proceedings in 2022 and appointed an independent panel to investigate the allegations.

The panel made a series of damaging findings, including that there was "substantial doubt about the legitimacy of the source of the currency that was stolen".

It also concluded that Ramaphosa "has a case to answer".

Ramaphosa initially challenged the report in court but withdrew his application after parliament voted against adopting its findings.

He revived the legal challenge again in May this year after the Constitutional Court, the country's highest court, ruled that parliament had acted unconstitutionally by blocking moves to impeach him.

It ordered parliament to refer the report to an impeachment committee.

In his latest challenge, Ramaphosa argued that the panel had "misconceived its mandate, misjudged the information placed before it and misinterpreted the four charges advanced against me".

The Western Cape High Court's ruling could bring the current impeachment process to an end, unless parliament successfully challenges the decision.

If they do, and the appeal is successful, the parliamentary committee would be able to examine the charges against the president and make a recommendation.

If the committee recommends impeachment, parliament would have to vote on whether to remove him from office. This would require the support of at least two-thirds majority in parliament.

Ramaphosa's Africa National Congress (ANC) has enough parliamentary seats to block such a move.

He is the first president to face impeachment proceedings under the rules introduced in 2018.

Legal expert Professor Richard Calland told the BBC he had expected the court ruling, given what he termed as "fault lines" in the independent panel's report.

Calland said the court's findings now meant that the impeachment process was "dead in the water [and] cannot continue".

He said that any political party that wanted to revive it "would have to come up with a whole new basis" for this.

Calland said political parties who want to hold Ramaphosa accountable would likely seek to appeal the ruling but it was unlikely parliament would also do so.

"So, the president's political career is now free of the cloud of impeachment," he said.

#89. Veteran Nigerian Nollywood actor Pete Edochie dies aged 79

Pete Edochie was one of Africa's most celebrated film stars

Veteran Nigerian actor Pete Edochie, one of Africa's most celebrated film stars, has died at the age of 79, his family has announced.

Known for his deep voice, commanding screen presence and memorable use of African proverbs, Edochie became a household name across Africa, often portraying powerful traditional rulers, wealthy businessmen and family patriarchs.

In a statement, his family said Edochie died in the early hours of Wednesday in the south-eastern Nigerian city of Enugu following a prolonged battle with prostate cancer.

Tributes are pouring in from fans and fellow actors, remembering him as a Nollywood legend who helped shape Nigeria's film industry.

Edochie began his career as a broadcaster in 1967 before turning to acting. He rose to prominence in 1987 after starring in a television adaptation of Chinua Achebe's acclaimed novel Things Fall Apart.

His portrayal of Okonkwo, the proud but troubled Igbo warrior in Chinua Achebe's acclaimed novel Things Fall Apart, earned him international recognition helping establish him as one of Nigeria's most respected actors.

The television series depicted the struggles of an African community as British colonial rule and Christianity began to transform its traditional way of life.

He went on to become one of the most recognisable faces of Nollywood, Nigeria's film industry, which has since grown into one of the world's largest film industries.

Over a career spanning several decades, he appeared in numerous films, including Igodo, Rituals, Billionaire's Club and the 2018 romantic comedy Lionheart.

His contribution to the arts was recognised in 2003 when he received one of Nigeria's national honours, the Member of the Order of the Niger (MON).

He also received several other accolades during his career, including the Industry Merit Award at the Africa Magic Viewers' Choice Awards.

In recent years, his distinctive facial expressions, witty remarks and memorable scenes from his films found a new generation of admirers through viral social media memes.

"As we mourn this painful loss, we pray that Almighty God grants his gentle soul eternal rest and gives the entire family the fortitude to bear this irreparable loss," a statement shared on Instagram by his son Leo said.

Enugu State Governor Peter Mbah said Edochie's death was a huge loss to Nigeria, praising the actor for introducing international audiences to Igbo traditions and inspiring younger performers.

"Pete Edochie was more than an accomplished actor, he was an institution," Mbah said.

The Actors Guild of Nigeria (AGN) president, Abubakar Yakubu, described Edochie as a "father figure" in the entertainment industry, saying his influence would be felt for generations to come.

Edochie had spoken publicly about his deteriorating health during a visit to Ghana in October, saying he was struggling to walk and experiencing swelling in parts of his body.

He is survived by his wife, Josephine, and their six children, including fellow Nollywood actors Yul and Linc Edochie.

#87. Two children among six dead after plane crash off Mississippi coast

Six people, including two children, have been found dead after a plane crashed off the coast of Mississippi on Tuesday, the US Coast Guard says.

The Cessna 210 was on its way from New Orleans to Fort Myers, Florida, according to the flight tracking website FlightAware. Air traffic control lost touch with it at about 15:45 (21:45 GMT) and it later went down in the Gulf of Mexico between Pascagoula and Horn Island, officials said.

Divers found the bodies of four adults and two boys in the fuselage, said Coast Guard spokesperson Juan Ros.

Rescuers spent hours searching for the group and initially only found three life jackets, a life raft and the plane's cabin floating on the water, he said.

On Wednesday, the Jackson County Coroner's Office identified the passengers as Brent Stokes, 50, Michael Knox, 43, Arlyn Knox, 43, Hunter Stokes,16, Charles Holden Knox, 12 and Truett Knox, 10, according the BBC's US media partner CBS News.

The Coast Guard had previously said the young boys were 10 and eight years old.

Cmdr Trevor Blount, the Coast Guard rescue mission co-ordinator, said: "While this is not the outcome any of us had hoped for, we are grateful for the strong interagency co-ordination that enabled us to swiftly locate those aboard the aircraft and provide closure to their families."

The Federal Aviation Administration said the plane was descending at 10,000ft (3,048m) per minute when contact was lost, according to a CBS affiliate in Biloxi, Mississippi.

The National Transportation Safety Board said it is investigating the Cessna 210L crash.

#85. French minister under fire for Playboy magazine cover

French government minister Marlene Schiappa has come under fire from members of her own party after appearing on the front cover of Playboy magazine.

Schiappa, who has been a government minister since 2017, appeared on the cover of the magazine to accompany a 12-page interview she did on women’s and LGBT rights. Schiappa, who is the current Minister for the Social Economy and French Associations, was photographed for the cover wearing a white dress.

Schiappa has been a long-time advocate for women’s rights and was appointed as the country’s first ever Gender Equality Minister in 2017. In this role, she successfully spearheaded a new sexual harassment law which allows for on the spot fines to be issued to men who catcall, harass or follow women on the street.

Her appearance has drawn criticism from political colleagues including French Prime Minister Elisabeth Borne.

Borne pulled up Schiappa over the cover, telling her it “wasn’t appropriate, especially during this period,” CNN affiliate BFMTV reported Saturday, citing a source close to the prime minister.

France is currently in the throes of a political and social crisis triggered by French President Emmanuel Macron’s move to push ahead with controversial pension reforms despite widespread public opposition.

“We are in the middle of a social crisis, there is the issue of policing, there are people between life and death, and I have the impression of being behind a smoke screen,” Sandrine Rousseau, Green Party politician and fellow women’s rights activist, told BFMTV Friday.

France wracked by more pension protests amid rising violence on the streets

French politician, Jean Luc Mélenchon who came third in the 2022 presidential elections criticized both Schiappa’s appearance and the decision by French President Emmanuel Macron to give an interview to children’s magazine, Pif Gadget, this week.

“In a country where the President expresses himself in Pif and his minister in Playboy, the problem would be the opposition. France is going off the rails,” Mélenchon tweeted Saturday.

Schiappa responded to her critics in a tweet on Saturday, saying: “Defending the right of women to have control of their bodies, that’s everywhere and all the time. In France, women are free. With all due respect to the detractors and hypocrites.”

French Interior Minister, Gérald Darmanin came to Schiappa’s defense during an interview with French news channel CNews on Sunday calling her a “woman of character.”

“I wanted to say that Marlene Schiappa is a courageous female politician who has her character and who has her style which is not mine, but I respect,” he remarked.

#84. Ice hockey prospect Max Calce follows Leon Draisaitl's path, leaving Mannheim for Canada

The player widely seen as Germany's biggest ice hockey talent to come along in years is leaving the country's top professional league for Canada – changing tracks in his bid to make it to the NHL.

Max Calce made the surprise announcement that he would be moving to the Brantford Bulldogs of the Ontario Hockey League (OHL) in a statement issued on the website of his German Ice Hockey League (DEL) team, Adler Mannheim late on Tuesday.

"I have long had three goals," he said. "To play for the Adler, in the Canadian Hockey League (CHL), and in the NHL. Today is a difficult and emotional day for me, as I say goodbye to my home."

Having started the season in Mannheim, many thought the 17-year-old would finish the season in the DEL.

The announcement came after Calce, who had been a regular in Mannheim's lineup this season, had been left off the team sheet for Tuesday night's game in Straubing.

This comes less than a month after he became the youngest-ever player to score a goal for the German men's national team – against Slovakia in Germany's final game of the annual Deutschland Cup invitational.

"It was only a matter of time; he's an incredible player," said former NHL player Tobias Rieder, who frequently played on a line with Calce during the tournament.

"The most impressive thing is that, at 17, he plays a very mature style of hockey—he almost always makes the right decision."

Calce is also the youngest goal scorer in DEL history, having found the net for Mannheim in September 2025 at just 16 years and 100 days old.

In his statement, the centerman thanked Mannheim, which has produced a number of NHL players over the years, including Leon Draisaitl, Moritz Seider and Tim Stützle.

"The trust, support, and experiences I gained through my coaches, teammates, and everyone within the organization have played a major role in my development—both as a player and as a person."

This season, his second in the DEL, Calce has six goals and two assists in Mannheim's first 19 games.

"We believe in preparing young players to take opportunities at the highest level of the sport," Dallas Eakins, Mannheim's head coach and sporting director said.

"As much as we would have loved to continue to have him play for us, we respect and welcome his ambition."

On the surface, the notion of moving from a men's professional league to a junior league may seem like a step backwards, but Calce's move to the OHL will give Calce increased exposure to NHL scouts. The OHL is one of the top three junior leagues in Canada and according to figures released by the CHL last season, they produce more NHL players than any other developmental system.

In doing so, Calce is now following the path of Edmonton Oilers superstar Draisaitl, who left Mannheim's youth program at the age of 16 to pursue his NHL dream, joining the Western Hockey League's Prince Albert Raiders.

This, of course is not the only path to the NHL; Moritz Seider of the Detroit Red Wings and the Ottawa Senators' Tim Stützle were both first-round draft choices plucked directly from Mannheim. Calce is hoping to be taken in the first round of next summer's NHL Entry Draft.

Born to Russian parents in Mannheim, Calce changed his birth surname, Penkin, to that of his mother and stepfather last summer.

#82. Thomas Markle charged in US for possessing child sex abuse images

Thomas Markle, the father of Meghan, Duchess of Sussex has been charged by a federal grand jury in Los Angeles with possessing child sex abuse images.

Markle, who remains in custody in Florida, allegedly possessed materials that include at least one image involving a prepubescent minor and a minor under 12 years old, court documents filed in California show.

The 82-year-old has denied possessing images of child sex abuse.

Last week, a judge in Florida declared Prince Harry's father-in-law mentally competent to stand trial.

The FBI arrested Markle on 6 November at an elderly care home in Florida on a single federal charge of possessing child sexual abuse material. He now faces two counts.

US attorney Bill Essayli said Markle faces a maximum of 20 years in prison on each count if convicted.

His arrest came after a search of a storage unit he had allegedly rented in Los Angeles for almost two decades uncovered the abuse material.

Markle was initially released on conditional bail but was then ordered to stay in jail pending trial after allegedly removing a location monitoring device.

Watch: Thomas Markle's charges over child sexual abuse material

In the indictment, filed by the US Attorney's Office in the Central District of California, Markle is accused of knowingly possessing 35mm film negatives on 30 October and 4 November that contained child sexual abuse materials.

The US Attorney's Office said that during both instances Markle allegedly knew the images contained such materials.

Inside the storage units, federal agents found two images that depict an adult man - whom a witness identified as Markle - with an underage child, the US attorney's office said.

They also alleged that a third image appears to depict a child in a room believed to be part of a motel chain in the Philippines. Markle moved to the Philippines last year after he had a series of strokes, and he lived in Cebu until May.

"The affidavit also describes additional photographs that appear to show Markle with children in various stages of undress," prosecutors said.

Judge finds Thomas Markle mentally fit to stand trial on child abuse charges

#81. EU plans €50 billion sovereign tech equity fund to boost quantum technology

The European Commission proposed a tech fund with a target size of €50 billion to boost quantum computing efforts. The bloc aims to keep up with the US and China in the global race.

The European Commission proposed a €50 billion ($54.73 billion) equity financing fund on Wednesday to boost artificial intelligence and quantum computing in Europe.

The draft "Quantum Act" aims to help Europe compete in a race against the United States and China.

"Quantum technologies will shape the future ‌of industry, security and innovation, and they will have a ‌profound ​impact on our societies. Europe needs to accelerate now," EU tech chief Henna Virkkunen said in a statement.

"We want to build a fund and our target scale is now €50 billion," she said, calling it a "sufficient starting point."

Virkkunen introduced the officially named "Sovereign Tech Equity Fund" while presenting new rules to support Europe's quantum technology sector.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The new rules will push public authorities to favor European companies when giving public contracts and funding.

The European Union (EU) also wants to reduce its dependence on third countries amid concerns that US companies could use so-called "kill switches” to disable their products in Europe.

Quantum computing technology is still at the early stages of development, with both the US and China competing to achieve dominance in the field.

While classical computers process data using bits — which are either 0 or 1 — quantum computers use qubits (quantum bits), which can exist in a blend of both states at once.

By using qubits, these machines could eventually solve complex problems much faster than today's supercomputers.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The EU expects the quantum computing to lead to breakthroughs in several areas, including drug development, earlier medical diagnoses or super-efficient batteries and even AI development.

Although quantum technology offers significant potential, it also poses cybersecurity risks, as it could undermine encryption and other safeguards for digital data at a time when governments are battling AI-driven cyberattacks.

Around one-third of the world's quantum companies ⁠are ​based in the European Union, according to ​the Commission. Last year, public funding for quantum technologies reached €8.6 billion in the bloc, compared with €5.3 billion in the United States and €13.6 billion in China.

However, European quantum start-ups have attracted €1.1 billion in private funding so far, compared with €7 billion in the United States.

The EU launched its "quantum Europe" strategy in July 2025, aiming to be a global leader in quantum technologies by 2030.

The bloc is already a global leader in quantum research but it falls short when it comes to transforming this into viable commercial opportunities.

The draft legislation must be negotiated with EU member states and the European Parliament in the upcoming months before it can be law.

#80. The four astronauts NASA picked for the first crewed moon mission in 50 years

Sign up for CNN’s Wonder Theory science newsletter. Explore the universe with news on fascinating discoveries, scientific advancements and more.

Astronauts who will helm the first crewed moon mission in five decades were revealed on Monday, queuing up the quartet to begin training for the historic Artemis II lunar flyby that is set to take off in November 2024.

The astronauts are NASA’s Reid Wiseman, Victor Glover and Christina Koch, and Jeremy Hansen of the Canadian Space Agency.

Wiseman is a 47-year-old decorated naval aviator and test pilot who was first selected to be a NASA astronaut in 2009. A native of Baltimore, Maryland, he’s completed one prior spaceflight, a 165-day trip to the International Space Station that had launched aboard a Russian Soyuz rocket in 2014. Most recently, Wiseman served as chief of the astronaut office before stepping down in November 2022, making him eligible for a flight assignment.

Wiseman will serve as commander of the Artemis II mission.

Hansen, 47, is a fighter pilot who was selected by the Canadian Space Agency for astronaut training in 2009. From London, Ontario, Hansen is one of only four active Canadian astronauts, and he recently became the first Canadian to be put in charge of training for a new class of NASA astronauts.

He will be the first Canadian ever to travel to deep space.

Glover is a 46-year-old naval aviator who returned to Earth from his first spaceflight in 2021 after piloting the second crewed flight of SpaceX’s Crew Dragon spacecraft and spending nearly six months aboard the International Space Station.

“It’s so much more than the four names that have been announced,” Glover said during the Monday announcement at NASA Johnson Space Center in Houston. “We need to celebrate this moment in human history. … It is the next step in the journey that will get humanity to Mars.”

Glover, born in Pomona, California, served in several military squadrons in the United States and Japan in the 2000s, and he completed test pilot training with the US Air Force. When he was selected for the NASA astronaut corps in 2013, he was working in the US Senate as a legislative fellow. All told, Glover logged 3,000 flight hours in more than 40 aircraft, over 400 carrier arrested landings and 24 combat missions.

Glover’s first mission to space was as part of the SpaceX Crew-1 team, which launched to the International Space Station in November 2020 for a six-month stay on the orbiting laboratory.

Koch, 44, is a veteran of six spacewalks — including the first all-female spacewalk in 2019. She holds the record for the longest single spaceflight by a woman, with a total of 328 days in space. Koch is also an an electrical engineer who helped develop scientific instruments for multiple NASA mission. Koch, a native of Grand Rapids, Michigan, also spent a year at the South Pole, an arduous stay that could well prepare her for the intensity of a moon mission.

The Artemis II mission will build on Artemis I, an uncrewed test mission that sent NASA’s Orion capsule on a 1.4 million-mile voyage to lap the moon that concluded in December. The space agency deemed that mission a success and is still working to review all the data collected.

If all goes to plan, Artemis II will take off around November 2024. The crew members, strapped inside the Orion spacecraft, will launch atop a NASA-developed Space Launch System rocket from NASA’s Kennedy Space Center in Florida.

The journey is expected to last about 10 days and will send the crew out beyond the moon, potentially further than any human has traveled in history, though the exact distance is yet to be determined.

The “exact distance beyond the Moon will depend on the day of liftoff and the relative distance of the Moon from the Earth at the time of the mission,” NASA spokesperson Kathryn Hambleton said via email.

After circling the moon, the spacecraft will return to Earth for a splashdown landing in the Pacific Ocean.

Artemis II is expected to pave the way for the Artemis III mission later this decade, which NASA has vowed will put the first woman and person of color on the lunar surface. It will also mark the first time humans have touched down on the moon since the Apollo program ended in 1972.

The Artemis III mission is expected to take off later this decade. But much of the technology the mission will require, including spacesuits for walking on the moon and a lunar lander to ferry the astronauts to the moon’s surface, is still in development.

NASA is targeting a 2025 launch date for Artemis III, though the space agency’s inspector general has already said delays will likely push the mission to 2026 or later.

The space agency has been seeking to return people to the moon for more than a decade. The Artemis program was designed to pave the way to establishing a permanent lunar outpost, allowing astronauts to live and work deeper into space long term as NASA and its partners map a path to sending the first humans to Mars.

Vanessa Wyche, the director of NASA’s Johnson Space Center, declined to provide details to CNN about the selection process. But she emphasized the diversity of the Artemis II crew, which includes men and women rather than only a staff of White male test pilots as has been the case for historic missions of the past.

“I can tell you, they still all have the right stuff,” Wyche said. “We have requirements different than we did (when we) just had test pilots” on inaugural missions.

Koch said in an interview with CNN’s Ed Lavandera that the group found out they were selected a few weeks ago.

“We were all sent to a meeting that was on our calendars under a different pretext that didn’t sound as lofty as the one it was going to be,” Koch said. “And accidentally two of us were very late to that meeting.”

She said the offer rendered her “speechless.”

“It truly is an honor,” she added. “It’s an honor — not to get myself in the space — but because it’s amazing to be a part of this team that’s going back to the moon and on to Mars.”

An interview with the four astronauts will air on “CNN This Morning” on Tuesday, which starts at 6 am ET.

CNN’s Kristin Fisher contributed to this story.

#78. Canadian astronaut welcomes baby girl from space station

A Canadian astronaut aboard the International Space Station is celebrating the birth of his third child from hundreds of miles above Earth.

Joshua Kutryk and his wife, Heather Kutryk, welcomed their daughter as the astronaut takes part in a six-month space mission that began in October, the Canadian Space Agency announced on Tuesday.

"Even with the distance between us, we feel incredibly connected as we welcome our new little star into our lives," said the couple in a statement shared by the agency.

Joshua Kutryk, currently on his first space mission, is just the fourth astronaut in history to miss the birth of a child while aboard the International Space Station.

The couple have two older children, aged five and seven.

"We are very proud of our growing family and are looking forward to getting our little crew of five all reunited."

In a statement, the couple said that Joshua's spaceflight was a "childhood dream" come true.

"We hope to show our children that big dreams are worth pursuing, that hard work and determination matter, and that with the support of family and a strong community, extraordinary things are possible," they said.

Prior to becoming an astronaut, Kutryk, from Fort Saskatchewan, Alberta, was a fighter pilot and a colonel in the Royal Canadian Air Force. He was recruited by the Canadian Space Agency in 2017.

Heather Kutryk said her family had been preparing for years prior to her husband's mission, calling it "a long time coming"

Kutryk was the only Canadian selected to be part of NASA's SpaceX Crew-13 mission, where four astronauts are conducting scientific experiments and testing new technologies in space.

The crew also includes two US astronauts, Luke Delaney and Jessica Watkins, and one Russian astronaut, Sergey Teteryatnikov.

Before their take off on 1 October, Heather Kutryk told Canada's public broadcaster CBC News that her family had been preparing for years before the mission, saying it was "a long time coming".

Heather added that her pregnancy was "not exactly planned" but that it was "a great thing".

"I'm not that stressed out. I feel like we have a really good community. I feel well supported by both CSA and Nasa," she said.

"It's six months, I feel like we'll be fine."

In a separate interview with BBC's US news partner CBS News, Joshua Kutryk said he hopes his new child will look at her dad's mission as "making some sacrifice, taking some risk, but doing so in the name of public service and the name, in my case, for Canada."

#75. German firms eye Africa as global trade shocks hit traditional markets

German businesses are optimistic about Africa, but Berlin's cautious approach to risk is holding them back from cashing in on the opportunities that their competitors from countries like the UAE and Turkey are seizing.

"Africa is a land of opportunity." "The German-African relationship is a win-win." "The continent is the market of the future."

That's what many business leaders and politicians have to say about Africa at the sixth edition of the German-African Business Summit in Accra, Ghana. While these descriptions aren't new, there's a palpable sense of urgency and seriousness this time.

German firms are increasingly viewing Africa as an attractive destination as they scramble to find new markets amid wars in Ukraine and Iran, high energy prices, punishing US tariffs and slowing growth in traditional markets like China.

"For a long time, many German companies had a secure source of revenue from the major export markets in North America and China, but the geopolitical ruptures of our time have undermined this certainty," German President Frank-Walter Steinmeier said during the event.

"One could also say that it is time for new horizons, time to take a closer look at economic opportunities still waiting for us."

Africa is home to some of the fastest growing economies in the world, like Ethiopia and Rwanda. Even as European and other advanced economies contend with declining working-age populations, Africa is expected to add hundreds of millions of workers in the next few decades, emerging as a key source of future labor.

Germany has already signed a migration pact with Kenya and is working on one with Ghana as it seeks to attract skilled workers to help ease acute labor shortages in certain sectors at home.

Even as Africa remains one of the poorest regions in the world, economic growth in parts of the continent has created a larger and more affluent consumer class over the past three decades. This group is aspirational and a growing source of demand for many of the goods that German firms want to sell, from cars to cosmetics.

The continent, with its mineral wealth and renewable energy potential, is central to European Union efforts to diversify its sources of critical raw materials and reduce reliance on China-dominated supply chains.

It is also playing an increasingly important role in strengthening the EU's energy security after the bloc phased out most energy imports from Russia.

German firms have committed to invest billions of euros in green hydrogen projects in countries such as Mauritania and Namibia.

"We don't have the capital to develop them [natural resources]. So foreign investors are coming to help us develop those resources. Whilst we are getting jobs and industrialization is taking place, they are also making profits," said Amin Amidu Sulemani, energy and infrastructure commissioner at the Economic Community of West African States (ECOWAS).

"It's not a relationship where we send raw materials outside of Africa every time, when we can actually process them here," he told DW.

Despite Africa's growing economic appeal, many obstacles to investment remain, including corruption and governance concerns, unreliable energy supplies, foreign exchange risk and poor infrastructure.

African leaders say they are working to address these challenges and improve the ease of doing business.

Yet while German companies have largely remained on the sidelines, competitors from countries such as the UAE, Turkey and India have made significant inroads across the continent.

German firms enjoy a strong reputation in Africa because of their engineering expertise, vocational training programs and long-term industrial investments, which are more likely to create stable jobs than investments in extractive industries.

However, they have struggled to translate that goodwill into a larger commercial presence. Africa, for instance, accounts for less than 2% of the total foreign investment made by German firms over the past several decades.

Business leaders complain that German firms lack adequate financing and risk protection for projects in Africa. They also criticize excessive bureaucracy and the absence of a coherent German strategy, with engagement fragmented across ministries and agencies.

Industry bodies say German institutions and firms often perceive risks too broadly, treating diverse African markets as if they pose the same level of risk.

"German companies can be a bit more bold and maybe also innovative in the way they approach the continent," Khadi Camara, deputy CEO of the German-African Business Association, told DW. "We see that in the companies that are successful, they don't do business as usual. Every country has its nuances that companies keep in mind and say, okay, this model works here, that model works there."

The 2026 World Investment Report by UN trade body UNCTAD showed that investors from the Gulf and Asia are becoming important sources of investment in Africa, especially in energy, logistics, real estate and infrastructure.

The UAE has been a major investor in greenfield megaprojects in recent years, including the $34 billion (€31 billion) Infinity Power renewable energy project in Mauritania, the $24 billion Ras El Hikma real estate development project in Egypt and the $6 billion H2 Global Energy hydrogen project in Tunisia.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Business leaders are calling for better financing and guarantees, and more risk-sharing and fewer bureaucratic hurdles.

Meanwhile, others, including the German president, are urging German businesses to take on more risk and seize opportunities in Africa. Otherwise, they risk being edged out by foreign rivals.

"Be curious, take risks and invest in our common future," Steinmeier said.

#71. Huawei found guilty of stealing US trade secrets but acquitted of racketeering

Defence lawyer Brian Heberlig makes closing arguments at the Huawei Technologies trial at Brooklyn federal court on November 17.

Huawei has been found guilty of conspiracy to steal trade secrets and bank fraud, but not guilty of the more serious overarching racketeering charge in a landmark case in the US.

In a two-month trial, prosecutors accused the Chinese technology and telecommunications giant of lying, cheating and stealing to gain any competitive advantage.

Huawei's defence team argued that prosecutors had decided Huawei was a criminal organisation and built its case from there, but the firm's success was due to its own innovation.

Huawei said, external the racketeering acquittal "confirms that these allegations lack factual basis", adding that it would "take all necessary legal actions to defend our rights and interests".

Jurors in a Brooklyn court found Huawei guilty of 10 out of 12 counts, which included conspiracy to violate US sanctions.

However, the jury declined to convict Huawei of the racketeering conspiracy charge, which required members to find the firm acted like a criminal enterprise.

Federal prosecutors first announced the charges against Huawei in 2019, after the dramatic arrest in late 2018 of senior executive Meng Wanzhou in Canada.

Meng, also known as Cathy Meng, was held for three years in Canada before she was released to return to China through a deal with US prosecutors.

During closing arguments last week, assistant US attorney Meredith Arfa told jurors Huawei spent 20 years doing whatever it took to expand its business, including lying to banks about its business dealings in Iran and stealing technology secrets from rival firms to ensure it had a competitive edge.

"For decades, Huawei and three of its subsidiaries conducted their business in the United States on the basis of lies and theft," US Attorney General Todd Blanche said in statement, external.

"Thanks to the work of the FBI and our federal prosecutors, those who engaged in these criminal activities will be held accountable."

The Chinese government hit out at what it called the US "suppression" of Chinese companies and said it supports Chinese firms protecting their interests.

Defence attorney Brian Heberlig said it was by dint of hard work that Huawei's business had expanded with operations in 170 countries and more than 200,000 staff.

Following the verdict, Heberlig told the BBC that Huawei was gratified that the jury had rejected the racketeering charge.

"The RICO [Racketeer Influenced and Corrupt Organizations Act] count that alleged that Huawei was a corrupt organisation, with a corporate culture and policy of committing crime. That was, in our view, a significant overreach, and the jury soundly rejected it," he said.

"Of course, we have mixed emotions about the rest of the verdict ... [we] thought that Huawei was deserving of acquittals across the board."

Sentencing is planned for September 2027. Heberlig said there will be "significant advocacy" to achieve the lowest sentence possible. Following that, he said he fully expects Huawei to appeal.

"If I know Huawei, that fight will continue, and the company's not going to rest until it's pursued every last argument it can through appeal, and hopefully, ultimately toward vindication," he said.

#70. Beijing promised to 'fight back' over Taiwan leader's US visit. But this time it has more to lose

An anticipated meeting between Taiwan’s President Tsai Ing-wen and US House Speaker Kevin McCarthy in California this week has sparked concerns of a repeat of the pressure campaign China launched last year when then-Speaker Nancy Pelosi visited Taipei.

At that time, Beijing encircled the island democracy with unprecedented military drills – firing multiple missiles into its surrounding waters and sending dozens of warplanes speeding across a sensitive median line dividing the Taiwan Strait.

It also cut off contact with the United States over a number of issues from military matters to combating climate change, in retaliation for what it viewed as a violation of its sovereignty.

This time, Beijing has already threatened to “resolutely fight back” if a Tsai-McCarthy meeting goes ahead.

It also slammed Washington for allowing Tsai to stopover in the US while en route to and from official visits in Central America, warning it could lead to “serious” confrontation between the two powers.

A defiant Tsai staked out her own ground, pledging as she took off on her 10-day trip not to let “external pressure” stop Taiwan from connecting with the world and like-minded democracies.

But the optics of the meeting, taking place in California and not Taiwan, and its timing – at a particularly thorny moment in China’s foreign relations and ahead of a presidential election in Taiwan that could reset the tone of its relationship with Beijing – may see Beijing tread more carefully this time, or at least not escalate further, analysts say.

“This puts the burden on China not to overreact, because any overreaction is only going to push China further away from the world,” said Yun Sun, director of the China Program at the Stimson Center think tank in Washington.

That doesn’t mean, however, that Beijing won’t be closely watching Tsai’s movements as it calibrates its response – and decides how much military might to flex over her meeting with an American lawmaker on American soil.

The opacity of China’s system – and the potential for competing interests within its vast bureaucracy – also make it difficult to accurately predict its response.

“Every time Taiwan does anything that China doesn’t like, the Chinese react with their own military coercion,” Sun said. But in the current situation, “they have to consider the consequences of overreaction,” she added.

The expected meeting, which McCarthy’s office announced earlier this week would take place on Wednesday, also comes at a precarious moment in US-China relations.

Washington and Beijing are struggling to stabilize their communication amid flaring tensions over issues from a downed suspected Chinese surveillance balloon to semiconductor supply chains – raising the stakes of potential damage to that relationship if Beijing lashes out as it did when Tsai met Pelosi.

Taiwan is still feeling the fallout of that response last August, with Chinese military forces now regularly making incursions over what had previously been an informal but largely respected border of control between Beijing and Taipei in the Taiwan Strait. Taiwan’s official Central News Agency also reported Monday that Tsai would meet with McCarthy, citing Tsai’s presidential office.

But a meeting between Tsai and the leader of the Republican majority in the US House of Representatives, who ranks second in line to the Presidency, would mark another symbolic moment for Taiwan and the US, which only maintain unofficial ties.

For Tsai, who is entering the final year of her two-term presidency, “it’s clearly a capstone event,” according to Wen-Ti Sung, a political scientist at the Australian National University’s Taiwan Studies Program. “She has this image as the Taiwanese president who has taken US-Taiwan relations to new heights, and who … has been able to give Taiwan almost unprecedented international visibility,” he said.

That increased visibility – and enhanced cooperation with the US – has followed China’s mounting pressure on the island, which sits fewer than 110 miles (177 kilometers) from the mainland coast.

China’s Communist Party claims the self-governing island democracy as its own despite never having controlled it, and has vowed to take the island, by force if necessary.

China thinks it's diplomatically isolating Taiwan. It isn't

The party has undertaken a sweeping expansion of its military capabilities over the past decade under leader Xi Jinping – and ramped up its pervasive economic, diplomatic and military pressure on Taiwan.

That’s driven concerns, among some in Washington, that Beijing is preparing for an invasion, though China’s official language still suggests that scenario is not its preferred option for achieving the claimed goal of “reunification.”

It is those pressures – and how to support Taiwan against unilateral actions by Beijing – that are likely to be on the table when Tsai, McCarthy and a bipartisan group of US lawmakers sit down on Wednesday.

Congress has been a pillar of increasing American support for Taiwan in recent years. Lawmakers regularly visit the island and drive bipartisan legislation enhancing support and cooperation.

While the US switched its diplomatic relations to Beijing decades ago, it maintains unofficial ties with Taiwan and is bound by law to provide the democratic island with the means to defend itself.

Under Washington’s longstanding “One China” policy, the US acknowledges China’s position that Taiwan is part of China, but has never officially recognized Beijing’s claim to the island of 23 million.

Though McCarthy does not have Pelosi’s decades-long record of advocacy regarding China, the California Republican is now a leading voice pushing for closer scrutiny of Beijing, and meeting Tsai could help him to burnish that image.

Last month, McCarthy told reporters that meeting Tsai in the US would not impact whether he travels to Taiwan in the future – something he had earlier said he wanted to do.

A meeting in California, on US soil, is widely seen as less likely to provoke Beijing than a McCarthy visit to Taiwan.

Pelosi’s trip – the first from a lawmaker of that rank to the island in 25 years – generated a fever pitch of nationalist and anti-US rhetoric in mainland China.

This time, so far, domestic conversation in China’s heavily controlled media sphere has been significantly muted.

But the stakes remain high – including for Beijing itself – over how it responds, analysts say.

As Taiwan prepares for a presidential election in January, a fierce response could push voters away from Taiwan’s main opposition party Kuomintang (KMT), widely seen as more friendly toward Beijing.

It could also jar with another high profile trip happening now: a tour of mainland China from former Taiwan president and senior KMT member Ma Ying-jeou, the first visit from a current or former Taiwan leader since the end of the Chinese Civil War in 1949.

Ma’s tour is a “once in a half a century opportunity to send a conciliatory message between the two sides, Beijing shouldn’t want to tank that,” said Sung, the political scientist.

What you need to know about China-Taiwan tensions

China is also acutely aware that its actions toward Taiwan are under a significantly brighter global spotlight following the invasion of Ukraine by Russian President Vladimir Putin, a close diplomatic partner of Xi. Putin’s rhetoric over Ukraine has echoes of how Xi speaks of Taiwan.

Beijing has recently sought to position itself as an agent of peace in that conflict – especially as it aims to repair frayed ties with Europe.

This week, as Tsai is expected to meet with McCarthy, French President Emmanuel Macron and European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen will head to China – an important opportunity that Xi may not want to overshadow with military posturing.

An aggressive response also risks stoking confrontation with the US, not yet six months after Xi and US President Joe Biden called to enhance communication during a face-to-face meeting in Bali.

“(A less overtly aggressive response) would imply that Beijing does not wish to escalate tension with the US to a level that can risk getting out of hand,” said Steve Tsang, director of the SOAS China Institute in London.

“A reset in US-China ties is not on the agenda, but an easing of tension is not beyond the realm of possibility.”

#67. US plans new tariffs on EU, China and 12 others

The Trump administration is planning new import duties of up to 20% to counter excessive manufacturing abroad. The measures could put further pressure on US consumers already struggling with high living costs.

The United States on Wednesday proposed new tariffs of up to 20% on imports from 14 economies, including the European Union and China.

The Office of the US Trade Representative (USTR) unveiled the proposed duties after completing an eight-month investigation into alleged unfair trade practices involving 16 trading partners.

Washington accuses the targeted economies of promoting excessive industrial production, allowing cheaper goods to undercut American manufacturers.

The proposed tariffs largely stick to rates set under existing trade agreements, US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer told CNBC on Wednesday. However, he added that some rates could be "topped up" by a few percentage points.

The duties will be subject to a public comment period and a hearing before taking effect, possibly in January at the earliest.

China would face an additional tariff of 20%, the joint-highest rate among the targeted economies.

The proposed duties would respect the 15% tariff cap agreed with the European Union, Japan, South Korea and Taiwan, as well as the 19% cap agreed with Malaysia, a senior US administration official said.

Bangladesh, Cambodia, India, Indonesia, Singapore, Switzerland, Thailand and Vietnam would also be affected.

Mexico and Norway are currently excluded from the proposed measures. Greer said talks with Mexico were continuing as Washington's investigation into its trade practices remained ongoing.

In a separate announcement, the USTR also proposed higher tariffs on Brazilian raw and refined sugar and a new 10% duty on aircraft, which would affect Brazilian manufacturer Embraer.

The senior US official said the proposed duties would not exceed tariff limits negotiated under existing trade agreements.

However, the new tariffs on China would be added to duties of around 25% imposed during Trump's first term, bringing the combined rate for some Chinese goods to 45%.

Around $30 billion worth of Chinese imports that Washington is considering for tariff reductions would be exempt from the new measures.

The proposed duties would not be added to existing sector-specific tariffs on products such as automobiles, steel and aluminum.

Nor would they be combined with tariffs of between 10% and 12.5% imposed in July against 60 trading partners over alleged insufficient action against forced labor. Where both measures apply, the higher rate would prevail.

The European Union said on Wednesday it would examine the proposed US tariffs but rejected Washington's allegations of excessive industrial production.

"We strongly reject the premise that Europe is a source of structural overcapacity," the EU said, adding that it expected Washington to honor the trade agreement reached with Brussels last year.

Beijing also rejected Washington's allegations of excessive industrial production and reiterated its opposition to trade wars, but stopped short of threatening retaliation.

"The so-called 'overcapacity' is a false issue, and China opposes using it as an excuse for political manipulation," Chinese Foreign Ministry spokesperson Guo Jiakun said.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Washington has accused Beijing of flooding global markets with cheap exports, ranging from steel to electric vehicles and solar products.

Trump met Chinese President Xi Jinping last week in Shenzhen and is due to host him at the G20 summit in Miami in December.

An existing trade truce between Washington and Beijing is set to expire on January 10. Access to rare earth minerals and Chinese purchases of US agricultural products could feature in further negotiations.

The measures are intended in part to replace revenues lost after the US Supreme Court struck down Trump's sweeping global tariffs in February.

The court ruled that Trump lacked the authority to impose the duties by declaring an economic emergency and bypassing Congress.

The new measures instead rely on Section 301 of the Trade Act of 1974, which allows Washington to investigate and retaliate against unfair or discriminatory foreign trade practices.

Trump has repeatedly promised that tariffs will bring manufacturing jobs back to the US and reduce the federal deficit.

The proposed duties could put further pressure on American businesses, which may pass additional costs on to consumers.

US consumer inflation reached 3.7% year-on-year in September, close to a three-year high. Earlier tariffs, the effects of Trump's war on Iran and a boom in artificial intelligence investment have contributed to rising prices.

High living costs were a key issue in November's midterm elections, which saw Trump's Republican Party lose control of both chambers of Congress.

A Reuters/Ipsos poll conducted after the elections found that 62% of respondents supported efforts to repeal Trump's tariffs.

#64. Suspected Chinese spy balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing

The Chinese spy balloon that transited the US earlier this year was able to capture imagery and collect some signals intelligence from US military sites, a source familiar with the matter tells CNN.

The balloon was able to transmit information back to Beijing in real time, the source said, and the US government still does not know for sure whether the Chinese government could wipe the balloon’s data as it received it. That raises questions about whether there is intelligence the balloon was able to gather that the US still doesn’t know about.

Still, the intelligence community has not been overly concerned about the information the balloon was able to gather, the person said, because it is not much more sophisticated than what Chinese satellites are able to glean as they orbit over similar locations.

A US intelligence official told CNN on Monday that “although analysis of the wreckage of the High Altitude Balloon remains ongoing, thus far, its flight over the United States does not appear to have provided critical new insights to the People’s Republic of China.”

The US also knew what the balloon’s path would be and was able to protect sensitive sites and censor some signals before the balloon was able to pick them up, officials have said.

As CNN has reported, the US intelligence community last year developed a method of tracking what it says is a fleet of these Chinese balloons operating across the globe, controlled by the Chinese military.

The FBI is still examining the balloon, but so far officials have been able to glean additional information about how the device worked, including the algorithms used for the balloon’s software and how it is powered and designed.

CNN has reached out to the National Security Council at the White House and the Pentagon for comment. NBC was first to report on the new intelligence.

The balloon first crossed into US airspace over Alaska in late January before passing through Canada and down into Montana, where it hovered for a few days, leading the US to believe it was trying to surveil sensitive military sites, like Malmstrom Air Force Base in Montana. It was eventually shot down by the US off the East Coast on February 4, and the incident further escalated tensions between Washington and Beijing, including the postponement of a diplomatic visit by US Secretary of State Antony Blinken to China.

A senior State Department official said in February that, as the balloon floated across the US, it “was capable of conducting signals intelligence collection operations.”

Gen. Glen VanHerck, the commander of US Northern Command and NORAD, said at the time that the US “did not assess” that the balloon “presented a significant collection hazard beyond what already exists in actionable technical means from the Chinese.”

The surveillance program, which includes a number of similar balloons, is in part run out of the small Chinese province of Hainan, officials told CNN. The US does not know the precise size of the fleet of Chinese surveillance balloons, but sources tell CNN that the program has conducted at least two dozen missions over at least five continents in recent years.

Roughly half a dozen of those flights have been within US airspace, although not necessarily over US territory, according to one official familiar with the intelligence.

China has maintained that the balloon was actually just a weather balloon thrown off course, and the US has been assessing the possibility that it was not deliberately maneuvered into the continental US by the Chinese government, CNN has reported.

Still, China did maintain some ability to maneuver the balloon, officials believe. And once the balloon was over Montana, China appeared to take advantage of its position to loiter over sensitive sites and try to collect intelligence.

This story has been updated with additional reporting.

CNN’s Alex Marquardt contributed to this report.
