#63. Colombian police storm supermarket to free 76 held hostage for 17 hours

Relatives of those being held hostage spent anxious hours as the situation unfolded

Colombian security forces have freed around 76 shoppers and employees who had been held hostage for about 17 hours at a supermarket in the city of Cartagena.

Gunmen had stormed into the building at 08:00 local time (13:00 GMT) on Tuesday after what Colombian President Abelardo de la Espriella described as a failed attempt at robbing an armoured car carrying money.

They held those inside at gunpoint while trying to negotiate their getaway.

With the situation becoming ever more intense as the day progressed, de la Espriella gave the green light for special forces and police to launch a daring operation to free the hostages, killing one gunman.

Residents living near the supermarket reported hearing explosions as the security forces moved into the building.

Police later confirmed that officers had used stun grenades to overpower the hostage-takers. Four have been arrested.

Around 20 people were still being held hostage when the security forces moved in

De la Espriella described it as "an extraordinary joint operation", which he said had been carried out "flawlessly".

He flew into the northern city to oversee the operation personally. He could be seen flanked by the commanders of the operation with a handgun strapped to his belt, in footage released following its conclusion.

De la Espriella said the gunmen had been given the chance to hand themselves in but had refused.

Earlier in the day, the assailants had freed 10 of the hostages, among them elderly women.

But they continued to hold around 20, even as a police helicopter hovered above and special forces teams prepared to enter the building, located in a popular shopping centre.

Local media report that, as well as asking for safe passage out of the supermarket, the gunmen also demanded nearly $2m (Â£1.5m) and armoured vehicles.

De la Espriella said they had no political affiliations but had taken the hostages once their plan to rob a cash-laden armoured car had been foiled.

"It's not that a terrorist group carried out a kidnapping - no, these are criminals who were caught off guard, had no way out and then kidnapped a group of people," he said.

Johan Correa, one of the rescued hostages, described to local media how the gunmen, who he said had been disguised, had entered the supermarket, where he worked.

"We had just started our shift when some guys dressed as police officers came in and started robbing the place," he said. "They were robbers, but we thought they were police."

He added that he and other hostages had been locked in a cold storage room, face down with their hands tied.

"We were there for more than four hours, lying on the floor with our hands tied, and it was a really tough experience."

Another employee, Dayro Rafael VelÃ¡zquez Castrillo, told Caracol radio that the assailants wore masks and had been armed with guns and knives.

VelÃ¡zquez said the gunmen repeatedly threatened the hostages, telling them that they had rigged the exits with explosives.

Relatives and bystanders congregated outside the entrance to the supermarket

He said that "thank God" they had been rescued, describing moments of tension as they heard explosions and a helicopter flying above.

Photos taken during the rescue operation show armed soldiers inside the building as people kneel with their hands behind their heads.

It is unclear from the photos whether those kneeling are hostages or the hostage-takers.

Local media report that the authorities feared that some of the gunmen may have tried to pass themselves off as hostages as the rescue mission unfolded.

The identities of the four arrested have not yet been released.

The hostages are being reunited with their families after being checked over by medical teams, according to the mayor of Cartagena.

Many anxious relatives had spent hours waiting for news outside the perimeter place around the supermarket by security forces.

A woman called Sandra told Caracol that her 76-year-old mother was one of those being held hostage.

Sandra's mother - among a group of elderly people released after six hours - was reunited with her daughters and grandchildren.

Footage recorded by local media showed bystanders cheering as some of those freed were escorted to ambulances waiting nearby.

But with around 20 people still inside the building, tension outside rose and police said they had to stop some of the relatives from breaching the perimeter.

At one point, flights into the city were halted, with planes diverted to Barranquilla.

Special forces, including units specialised in dealing with kidnappings, were deployed to Cartagena, a popular destination among foreign and Colombian tourists.

Cartagena's Mayor Dumek Turbay called for calm but the granddaughter of one of the hostages told Colombian newspaper El Tiempo that "this shows we're not safe anywhere".

De la Espriella was elected earlier this year on a promise to crack down on Colombia's criminal groups.

He had travelled to the central city of Villavicencio to inspect the damage caused by a car bomb that detonated outside the regional government's offices on Tuesday.

While de la Espriella blamed the leader of a dissident rebel group for that attack, he was adamant that the hostage takers were "simple bandits" without political affiliations.

#57. Protesters call on Israel to free Palestinian student held without charge

Mahdi Tunsi appears by videolink in front of his attorney Nasser Odeh during his hearing at the Jerusalem District Court on Tuesday

Israeli students, activists and academics are calling for the release of a Palestinian undergraduate who was taken from his university dormitory by Israeli police earlier this month and has since been held without charge.

Mahdi Tunsi, a 26-year-old sociology and criminology student at the Hebrew University of Jerusalem, appeared in an Israeli court in the city on Tuesday - a hearing that was held behind closed doors.

Israeli police say he was arrested "concerning ties to an act of terrorism".

Authorities have requested what is known as "administrative detention", which allows the Israeli state to detain people indefinitely without charge on the basis of secret evidence that they cannot see.

"We definitely think this is a political arrest," said Dr Yael Berda, one of Tunsi's university professors.

Tunsi, a Palestinian resident of occupied East Jerusalem who works with well-known Jewish-Palestinian campaign groups, was arrested a month after Israel held an election that saw Arab-led parties win a record 19 seats in parliament, the Knesset, and Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu's right-wing coalition lose its majority.

Since then, Berda said she had seen an intensifying campaign against Palestinian rights.

Knesset Member Dr Ofer Cassif was one of the those gathered outside the court

Outside the Jerusalem District Court on Tuesday, demonstrators waved placards with a black-and-white image of Tunsi, while crying out "shame" in unison.

"There's no due process - [administrative detention is] a sugar-coated term for being kidnapped and held as a hostage," said Dr Ofer Cassif, a Member of the Knesset representing the Arab-led Joint List alliance.

"We must all stand firm against such crimes. It's unacceptable."

Since the Hamas-led attack on Israel on 7 October 2023, which triggered the Gaza war, the number of people in administrative detention has more than doubled.

In October, there were more than 3,100 in administrative detention, according to Israel Prison Service figures published by Israeli rights group HaMoked. Most of them are Palestinians from the occupied West Bank.

Israel says that its use of the policy is compliant with international law and a necessary preventative measure to combat terrorism.

Protesters held signs calling for Tunsi to be freed in Arabic and Hebrew

In a statement, Israeli police said they would "not elaborate further regarding the investigation" into Tunsi.

He appeared via video link from prison - his hair and beard shaved off. His friends also said that he was without medication as well as his glasses, despite a judge requesting them to be brought to him in detention.

The Israel Prison Service said it acted in accordance with the court's rulings.

"When the process was complete and all the necessary approvals had been received, the detainee's lawyer was told he could pass the eyeglasses to the detainee immediately, subject to security procedures," it added.

Berda asked to enter the courtroom on Tuesday but was denied entry. According to Berda, not even Tunsi has been told of the evidence against him.

The judge has said more time is needed to consider the state's request for administrative detention. A decision is expected in the coming days.

The judge has said more time is needed to consider the state's request for administrative detention

"If they have evidence, have them bring it or release him," said Berda.

"That's why we're demanding his immediate release or moving it to a criminal track where he could defend himself."

Berda said that while Tunsi could not vote in Israel's general election because he is not an Israeli citizen, he was "engaged" and very supportive of Arab parties' involvement.

"Mahdi is one of the best human beings I know," said Noga Kaplan, who studies with him at the Hebrew University.

"He has a really pure heart and is very passionate about his studies."

Protesters demand the release of Mahdi Tunsi outside the Jerusalem Central Court during his hearing.

After Tunsi's arrest, the Hebrew University issued a statement saying it was notified a few minutes before it took place.

"The university is not a party to the legal proceedings involving the student, and we do not have information that would allow us to comment on the circumstances leading to the arrest or on the ongoing legal proceedings," it said.

It added that it was concerned for Tunsi's well-being and hoped "that the matter will be resolved promptly and that his rights will be fully respected throughout the process".

Tunsi is a resident of Silwan, a neighbourhood in East Jerusalem where, in recent years, Palestinians have been evicted by Israeli authorities, who say their properties were built illegally. Israeli settlers have since moved into some of those properties.

"Political organisation stopped after October 7 because people were getting super scared about the consequences," explained Kaplan.

"Mahdi saw studying as one of the ways to speak about it in class."

"He didn't do a lot of public stuff because what's happening now is his biggest fear."

#55. Ukraine: Poland to set up no-fly zone near Ukraine border

Poland urged NATO allies to be vigilant at their borders after repeated Russian airspace incursions. Allies have also offered different opinions on Kyiv attacking Russian ships in Libya. Follow DW for the latest.

Keep reading for the latest news in Russia's war in Ukraine on Wednesday, November 25:

In an interview with the German MD Meets Podcast, US envoy Jared Kushner warned against a downward spiral in the conflict in Ukraine, which he described as "a proxy war where Europe is funding and arming Ukraine against Russia."

Kushner, US President Donald Trump's son-in-law and chosen intermediary in negotiations with Russia's Vladimir Putin, also warned that "it won't take a lot of miscalculation for this thing to escalate massively."

After several failed rounds of talks involving his fellow envoy, Steve Witkoff, Kushner admitted that the issue of territoriy in eastern Ukraine remained a sticking point.

"What Steve and I have deciphered from having many, many hours with President Putin and from the Russian team, is that you know the territorial issue is right now a little bit of a deadlock," he said.

However, Kushner also appeared to accuse the Europeans of hypocrisy for supporting Ukraine while continuing to spend vast sums of money on Russian energy.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

A German-Ukrainian woman accused of spying for Russia goes on trial in Berlin on Thursday.

She is charged with providing an employee and secret agent at the Russian Embassy in Berlin with sensitive information.

The alleged spy is accused of tipping the man off about high intelligence-value events over a period of years and helping him to secure entry.

The dpa news agency reports that the Russian agent in question was the country's deputy military attache, who has since been expelled from Germany.

The woman had long been known as a pro-Russian networker in the Berlin area, having lived in the country for several decades.

The lawsuit against her from the federal prosecutor's office based in Karslruhe also alleges that she tried to "win over individuals from the Federal Defense Ministry and the weapons industry for her purposes."

She is accused of acting out of personal conviction, rather than, say, fear or blackmail. She was arrested in January this year.

The woman is accused of making contact with the Russian espionage asset as of October 2023 at the latest. Her most pressing task was to seek out information pertaining to Germany defense policy and the weapons industry, particularly in light of and where relevant to Russia's full-scale invasion of Ukraine.

The Ukrainian General Staff announced that Ukrainian forces had attacked a major Russian military air base, damaging five aircraft used to launch missiles and bombs at Ukraine.

According to the General Staff, three MiG-31 jets, which are used to fire hypersonic missiles, and two Su-34 fighter-bombers were knocked out of operation "for some time."

The report stated that the Savasleyka air base, located east of Moscow in the Nizhny Novgorod region, had been hit in the latest long-range attacks aimed at crippling Moscow's war effort.

Russia did not immediately comment on the announcement.

Numerous sources inside Russian foreign policy circles on Wednesday underscored the Kremlin's desire for European diplomats to travel to Moscow and explain their intentions before they be allowed to participate in any Russia-Ukraine peace negotiations.

Russian President Vladimir Putin originally extended the invitation last month. On Wednesday, Foreign Policy Advisor Yury Ushakov said the invitation stands but that no Europeans have so far agreed to embark on the diplomatic sojourn.

Russia's Ambassador to Germany Sergei Nechayev on Wednesday told France's AFP news agency that reaction to Putin's invitation has been "restrained."

Nechayev claimed that Moscow remains, "ready to continue the dialogue and carry on with negotiations."

He also repeated the Kremlin claim that it is Europe that is the impediment to stopping the war, and that Russia — which launched and continues to execute its unprovoked years-long war of aggression in Ukraine — is the only party truly interested in peace.

Nechayev said the Kremlin doesn't see the same "honesty" from the Europeans that it does from US President Donald Trump's team — made up of his son-in-law Jared Kushner and his friend Steve Witkoff — and wants to "know precisely what their intentions are."

Russia has been accused of foot dragging as a way to prolong attacks on Ukraine. On Wednesday, Nechayev continued in that vein, saying: "Our request to Europe is this: Decide what it is you want, determine whom you will appoint as negotiators, and, most importantly, clarify what you aim to achieve."

Poland's Deputy Defense Minister Cezary Tomczyk told reporters that Warsaw will set up a no-fly zone inside its border with Ukraine and suggested other regional allies do the same in the wake of repeated Russian incursions into NATO airspace as part of Moscow's war of aggression.

"An ordinance is already being prepared by the government and should enter into force at the end of December or the beginning of January," said Tomczyk.

"We will also urge our allies — the ⁠entire ​NATO alliance — to establish such a ​principle along the eastern flank," he continued, "Poland was a pioneer ​in this regard, ‌and the Baltic states have since followed our lead."

Poland strictly limits air traffic at its border already.

On Wednesday, Tomczyk said the proposed no-fly zone will cover several dozen kilometers of territory from the border and provide added support for Poland's armed forces.

German Chancellor Friedrich Merz defended his government's expenditures on Ukraine aid during a budget debate in the Bundestag on Wednesday.

Merz tackled criticism from opposition parties on the left and right head on, saying "Russia knows exactly ​what it wants. And Russia wants more than Ukraine."

"Russia is escalating in the Baltic region, in Poland, on the Black Sea. Russia ​is escalating ‌in space. And Russia is now escalating massively here, in our own country, too, even at the risk of loss of life," he added.

Merz said, "anyone who doesn't see that, doesn't understand the world around them, or they are pursuing entirely different aims."

The Left Party and Alternative for Germany (AfD) have both called for an end to Ukraine aid and better relations with Moscow.

Among other things, Germany blames Russia for an explosive drone incident at Leipzig airport this summer as well as cyberattacks, sabotage and airspace incursions across the EU and NATO.

Merz said on Wednesday that by supporting Ukraine, Germany is protecting itself.   "We are learning from Ukraine," said Merz, "technologically for civilian use but of course also in defense ⁠technology."

Kyiv and Moscow both assessed damage to energy infrastructure on Wednesday morning as both sides continued to inflict damage on one another amid Russia's over four-and-a-half-year attempted invasion of neighboring Ukraine.

Russia again focused its attacks on Ukraine's energy structure in an attempt to break the will of the country's civilian population as the bitter cold of winter sets in.

Moscow's troops attacked a thermal energy power plant, shutting it down; as well as targeting a gas production facility in Poltawa, where ensuing fires also shut down operations.

Ukraine's acting energy minister said that power outages were reported in five regions as a result of the Russian attacks.

In Russia's Samara region, Ukrainian drones hit at least two oil refineries, killing one person, sparking large fires and and causing energy blackouts.

Kyiv's attacks on Russian ships in and off the coast of Libya over the past couple of days have ruffled feathers among Ukraine's allies, with France and Italy, for instance, coming down on entirely different sides of the issue.

French Foreign Minister Jean-Noel Barrot said Kyiv was justified in its actions because it has an "absolute right to self-defense." Libya on Wednesday announced that two of its soldiers had died from injuries sustained during a weekend drone attack that sank a Russian warship.

Speaking with French broadcaster TF1, Barrot said, "For nearly five years now, [Russian President] Vladimir Putin has not only been bombing Ukraine, but has also been constantly engaging in acts of aggression and harassing Europeans in an attempt to undermine our unity."

Barrot said Russia alone is to blame for the war and its expansion, and then asked, "What would we do if we were being bombarded day and night by an enemy — an invader?"

Italy's Foreign Minister Antonio Tajani on Wednesday criticized Kyiv's actions, saying, "It was a mistake to violate Libya's sovereignty" and warned against further similar activities in other regions.

Speaking with Italy's Corriere della Sera newspaper ‌on ⁠Wednesday, Tajani said, "Italy was absolutely unaware of Kyiv's intentions to strike in ​Libyan territorial waters, even inside a port."

Tajani said he had spoken with Ukraine Foreign Minister Andrii Sybihato and conveyed Italy's concern over the matter, adding that Rome would continue its staunch support for Ukraine and its sovereignty while at the same time seeking to keep the conflict from spreading.

As Russia's invasion of Ukraine creeps toward its fifth year, both sides continue to blast each other's infrastructure.

Overnight attacks targeted gas, oil and thermal energy facilities on both sides of the conflict's front lines.

While Moscow chided former UK Prime Minister Boris Johnson's calls for a contingent of Western soldiers to be stationed in Ukraine, France's foreign minister defended Kyiv's recent attacks on Russian maritime vessels in far away Libya — something Italy's foreign minister criticized.

In Berlin, Chancellor Friedrich Merz defended German support for Ukraine as opposition parties with sympathies for Moscow complain it's too expensive.

Merz called out those wanting to curtail support as either "failing to grasp" the fact that "Russia wants more than Ukraine" or "pursuing entirely different aims with regard to our country's freedom, openness and liberalism."

Lastly, Poland's defense minister on Wednesday expressed astonishment over the fact that the US has not yet condemned or even reacted to repeated Russian drone and missile incursions into Polish airspace in recent days.

#53. Five Russian military jets hit in airfield strike, says Ukraine

A Russian MiG-31 military jet (file photo)

Ukraine has said it damaged five Russian military aircraft in an attack on Russia's Savasleyka airfield, 300km (185 miles) east of Moscow.

Three MiG-31 and two Su-34s aircraft were hit, Kyiv's General Staff said, adding that the extent of the damage was still being assessed but that "there may be even better news to come". It did not elaborate on what weapons it had used to hit the airfield.

The airfield at Savasleyka, in Russia's Nizhny Novgorod region, has been a target for Ukraine for several years. It hosts aircraft used by Russia to launch hypersonic missiles and glide bombs.

The take-off of MiG-31s in particular frequently triggers nationwide alerts across Ukraine, as the Kinzhal missiles they carry have been known to hit large infrastructure and military targets and are difficult to shoot down.

Overnight, Kyiv also said it hit two oil refineries in Russia's Samara region some 800km (500 miles) from the front line â the latest in a series of long-range strikes which have dented Moscow's oil processing capacity.

"Did you repair them? We'll hit them again," the defence ministry in Kyiv said.

Samara region governor Vyacheslav Fedorishchev said the night-time attacks had been "a true ordeal", with more than 145 drones shot down. One person was killed and several towns experienced power outages, Fedorishchev said.

Kyiv also said it had hit a radar system as well as fuel and logistics depots across Ukrainian territories currently occupied by Russia.

"Without fuel, military vehicles cannot move; without ammunition, artillery falls silent," the defence ministry said, adding that every successful hit meant Moscow could carry out fewer strikes.

Kyiv has developed a thriving weapons industry and Ukrainian strikes on Russian targets well beyond the front line have become commonplace in the years since Moscow launched its full-scale invasion.

On Tuesday, President Vladimir Putin called for a tightening of security measures across the country's energy facilities.

But he also argued that Russia had managed to stabilise its domestic fuel market without suffering "critical damage" and added that Moscow would "inevitably" retaliate against Kyiv.

Ukraine continues to come under daily attack. Ten people were killed across several regions between 24 and 25 November, while thousands are without power after Russian strikes on energy infrastructure.

#50. Israeli strike kills senior Hamas security official and family in Gaza City

The strike caused extensive damage to the Abu al-Ouf family's apartment

An Israeli air strike in the Gaza Strip has killed a senior Hamas security official and three members of his family, according to local doctors and the Hamas-run interior ministry.

Mohammed Abu al-Ouf, 47, the head of Hamas's internal security service in northern Gaza, his wife and their two teenage daughters died when their apartment in Shati refugee camp in Gaza City was bombed at dawn, the ministry said.

The Israeli military said Abu al-Ouf was a "terrorist" and that he was eliminated "to remove the threat he posed".

On Tuesday, the overall commander of Hamas's internal security service was among 10 Palestinians reportedly killed in Israeli strikes.

Israeli air strike kills Hamas internal security chief in Gaza

Israeli attacks have turned Gaza into 'toxic wasteland', Oxfam says

Wednesday's strike in Shati caused extensive damage to the Abu al-Ouf family's apartment.

Photos showed the exterior wall and several interior walls were destroyed, with rubble and broken furniture left scattered across the floor.

An interior ministry statement said that Abu al-Ouf was killed along with his wife Noura, 41, and their daughters Yusra, 15, and Jana, 13.

It condemned the attack as a "heinous crime" and accused Israel of targeting security personnel in Gaza "in an effort to spread chaos within Palestinian society".

Hamas's internal security service is responsible for enforcing discipline, protecting the group's infrastructure and rooting out informants.

Israel's military said in a statement that Abu al-Ouf was a "significant figure" who had served as "a key source of expertise" in efforts to restrict Israeli counter-terrorism operations in Gaza.

"His elimination is a blow to the apparatus's leadership capabilities and follows yesterday's elimination of his commander," it added.

On Tuesday, an Israeli drone strike in western Gaza City killed Naeem Abu Nahel, the head of Hamas's internal security service.

He had only been in the position since September, when his predecessor was captured in a raid in Gaza City by Israeli special forces.

Near daily Israeli strikes continue in Gaza despite a ceasefire agreed in October last year.

At least 1,679 people have been killed in Gaza over the past 13 months, according to the territory's Hamas-run health ministry, whose figures are considered reliable by the UN.

Israel says six of its soldiers have been killed in Gaza during the same period.

The Gaza war was triggered by the Hamas-led attack on southern Israel on 7 October 2023, when about 1,200 people were killed and 251 others were taken hostage.

Since then, more than 74,740 people have been killed in Israeli attacks in Gaza, the health ministry says.

#48. Nigeria's upcoming election marred by rising AI disinformation

As Nigeria inches closer to the 2027 polls, the use of artificial intelligence in the electoral process has prompted questions about integrity and institutional readiness. Can Nigerians trust their democratic processes?

Nigeria has just over two months to go until its general election, with incumbent Bola Tinubu running on a ticket focused on combating raging insecurity and resolving the nation's economic crisis. He faces off against former Vice President Atiku Abubakar and senior opposition figure Peter Obi.

The country's electoral body, the Independent National Electoral Commission (INEC), recently released its preliminary voter's register, which reportedly is riddled with images believed to have been generated using artificial intelligence (AI).

Among the telltale signs of AI usage were exaggerated facial features in images and inconsistencies on the electoral registers, such as duplicates of names and even identities of people who since the previous election had died.

The development raises concerns about the body's overall preparedness ahead of the country's upcoming 2027 general election. Instead of making the upcoming vote more transparent by allowing access to its procedures, INEC has come under fire amid broader controversies about the expanding influence of AI in elections across the continent.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Joash Amupitan, the chairman of the electoral commission, said the body is now investigating the development, focusing on discrepancies potentially created by AI use.

To this end, he announced the establishment of a dedicated unit to monitor AI-related threats, detect deepfakes and strengthen the integrity of the electoral process, warning that the credibility of the upcoming election could otherwise be under attack.

However, even prior to the proliferation of AI across Africa and the globe, this would not be the first time that Nigeria has found itself in the unfortunate position of having to deal with the circulation of fabricated images, doctored audio and fake videos online. In the run-up to the 2023 election, deepfakes falsely linking candidates to terror-related activities filled social media platforms.

These are often designed to promote certain candidates or to discredit opponents in a bid to influence public perception, which now with the use of AI could create disinformation campaigns of new proportions.

Lois Ugbede, the assistant editor at the factchecking platform DUBAWA, told DW that "based on our monitoring so far, AI-driven disinformation is serious and is becoming commonplace."

"This could influence political discourse and voter behavior ahead of 2027 because we are already seeing parties using inflated crowds to show that they have a large followership or support," she added.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

At the same time, rather than influencing the vote in favor of any particular candidate, "the deeper danger is that people may begin to doubt authentic information simply because they know convincing AI-generated fakes are possible," said Akintunde Babatunde, executive director of the Abuja-based Centre for Journalism, Innovation and Development.

"We often focus on the risk of people believing an AI-generated falsehood, but there is another problem where genuine evidence can be dismissed as AI-generated," he noted, citing instances of a lawmaker who recently made inflammatory remarks during a campaign rally — but then claimed the clips were manipulated.

Chibuike Mgbeahuruike, an election consultant at the Centre for Democracy and Development, told DW that apart from the spread of synthetic content online, AI intrusion into the electoral commission's portal could also "undermine its institutional credibility, especially with the low level of confidence that we are already witnessing."

He added, however, that AI itself is not inherently a threat to electoral integrity. The real threat lies in "the absence of effective accountability for its deployment," as innovations in tech increasingly appear to infiltrate governments and their democratic processes.

"Voters must have confidence … that the technology is also transparent, accountable and independently scrutinized, and ultimately subject to human and institutional oversight," said Mgbeahuruike.

Mallam Jibrin Baba Ndace, the director-general of state-owned broadcaster Voice of Nigeria, agrees, urging journalists at a recent conference also to not fall into the trap of believing reports, which later could turn out to be false due to AI manipulation.

He stressed the importance of protection measures and due diligence in news media, with the growing inability of public institutions like INEC to provide impartial information due to the use and influence of AI and other election-related tech.

Beyond AI, Nigeria's electoral processes have undergone various technological shifts over the years in hopes of improving transparency while increasing efficiency.

Electoral authorities first deployed electronic assistance in 2011, when Direct Data Capture Machines were introduced; the devices collect voters' biometrics by scanning fingerprints or capturing facial recognition data with webcams to stop voters from submitting multiple ballots.

Ten years later, a system called the Bimodal Voter Accreditation System (BVAS) was introduced as the new standard, designed to combine fingerprints with facial recognition. However, during the 2023 general election, this voting process was marred by system failures, as poor cell signals delayed these voter accreditation procedures.

However, with INEC tallying more than 103 million Nigerians registered to participate in the coming poll, disinformation, fake news and technological challenges amount to only a few of several concerns pertaining to Nigeria's democracy.

Voter apathy has become a particular concern, especially among the country's youth. The 2023 election recorded the lowest voter turnout since the country's return to democratic rule in 1999, with merely 27% of registered voters going to the polls.

#45. Russia's own figures show it has lost nearly 500,000 people in Ukraine war

Russia does not provide numbers of its fallen soldiers

At least 490,334 Russian soldiers have died since the start of the war in Ukraine in 2022, according to Russian government statistics analysed by the BBC.

The leaked Rosstat data reveal how the full-scale invasion of Ukraine has fundamentally changed mortality patterns in Russia, and show the high cost paid in human life in a war that Vladimir Putin had assumed would last only days but is now almost five years old.

BBC Russian has had access to more than 11 million death records from 2021 to September 2026, after a hack by Belarusian group Cyber Partisans BY and Ukraine's Silent Crow.

Russia's statistics agency admitted to a cyber-attack, but denied any protected information had been leaked.

Kremlin spokesman Dmitry Peskov said on Wednesday that only Russia's defence ministry could officially report data on losses: "I suggest no other data be taken into account." The defence ministry has not released any official casualty figures for several years.

Together with independent Russian outlet Mediazona, BBC Russian has compared the Rosstat data with its own list of confirmed military deaths, pulled from open sources since the start of the invasion, and it has found matches for 90% of the records.

Military experts estimate that the BBC's list of 290,012 deaths, gleaned from official reports, social media, new memorials and graves, may account for 45-55% of the true death toll, which would range from 527,295 to 644,471.

The records show that 65% of 18 to 24 year-olds in Russia who lost their lives in 2025 died in the war. For those aged 25-34 the number was 59%. Only among 45 to 54 year-olds does cardiovascular disease claim more Russian lives than the conflict.

The data also reveal the extent of losses in specific areas of Ukraine. Some 20,000 Russian soldiers died fighting for control of 10 Ukrainian villages whose combined pre-war population was 12,000.

During the fight for one small village called Andriivka, south of the town of Bakhmut in Donetsk region, 1,122 Russians lost their lives. Before the war 74 people lived there.

Russia does not give public figures for losses sustained since the war began in February 2022. And two years ago Rosstat stopped publishing data of deaths from external causes, which was used to gauge the extent of military losses.

For example, only 70% of deaths we have established of soldiers from the occupied regions of Donetsk and Luhansk in eastern Ukraine who fought in the Russian army were accounted for in the Rosstat files.

Overall, the Rosstat dataset confirms the trends that we have observed with our own count.

Ukrainian losses were last put at about 50,000 by President Volodymyr Zelensky in July 2026, but another list based on open sources by the anonymous UA Losses website puts the figure at 115,952.

The Rosstat dataset contains no names but does include ages, places of residence, places of death and also circumstances of death.

We have filtered the records first by location, leaving only Ukraine as well as Russian border regions that saw incursions by the Ukrainian army.

Next, we identified 425,174 entries explicitly indicating a military-related cause of death, such as explosions or shrapnel during combat operations.

As this count could include civilians, we filtered out women as well as men outside the 18-65 age group. And if someone lived and died in the four Ukrainian regions occupied by Russia, we excluded them as a possible civilian â unless he had a record of military service attached to his entry.

Another 14,093 entries were identified by looking at employment, and 51,067 were recorded in the database as deceased on the basis of a court ruling. This often happens when a soldier goes missing in a combat zone for more than six months and their body has not been recovered.

Again, we filtered out civilians by only counting deaths in the war zones in Ukraine and Russia.

According to BBC Russian's analysis of the leaked files, in the first year of the war the average age of those killed was 34. By 2025, as prisoners and contract soldiers entered the ranks, it had risen to 40 (38 according to our own list).

Some of Russia's poorest regions in Eastern Siberia account for a disproportionately large share of the losses. Men from Tyva, Buryatia and Zabaykalsky Krai are 20-25 times more likely to die in the war than residents of the biggest cities of Moscow and St Petersburg.

In most regions, the distribution of deaths based on Rosstat data differs from that in the BBC's list by just a few tenths of a percentage point.

At least 5,017 foreigners are among those who died fighting for Russia, according to our analysis of the Rosstat files, including 1,031 Uzbeks and 720 Tajiks. We have left out Ukrainians from this count to avoid including civilians.

This is a conservative estimate as dual nationals are listed as Russians in the database, and there is no reference to North Koreans who died repelling the Ukrainian incursion into Russia in 2024.

The BBC identified 2,304 North Korean soldiers who died there by analysing satellite imagery and official photographs of a newly built memorial in Pyongyang.

The leaked Russian statistics may not be complete but taken in conjunction with BBC Russian's own work they are significant.

In the context of a war that has lasted four years and 10 months, these are high numbers for Russia, when compared with the Soviet war in Afghanistan that ended in 1989.

In that 10-year war, an estimated 15,000 Red Army troops died, and an estimated one million civilians were killed.

#44. EU must set out a new timeline for cage-free farming, court rules

European General Court ruled that the European Commission has failed to set out a timeline after pledging to end caged farming by the end of 2023.

A European Union (EU) court told the bloc on Wednesday to draw a new timeline for plans to ban cages for farm animals.

Judges at the European General Court ruled that the EU has failed to follow through on a pledge to ban the practice.

In 2021, the European Commission set out the goal to pass a legislation by the end of 2023 to stop farmers from cooping hens, rabbits, sows and other animals in cages. The move came after 1,4 million people signed a petition.

But it failed to follow up on the pledge. This resulted in the campaigners requesting a new timeline and eventually led to a court case when that was also not happening.

The Luxembourg-based General Court, the European Union's second-highest, on Wednesday ruled in favor of the animal rights campaigners.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

"The Commission is under an obligation — which is not limited in time — to set out a timeline for all the measures which it intends to take in response to such an initiative," the court said.

According to the "European citizens' initiative" system, the Commission has to receive the petitions that gather over one million signatures across at least seven bloc countries. However, it is not obligated to meet the demands.

The court noted that if the Commission decides to take action, it has to keep the public and the European Parliament posted on a timeline in accordance with the principle of "good administration."

The Commission said it was analyzing the ruling before making a decision. It can still appeal the ruling.

It has previously stated an intention to put forward a proposal for the phased elimination of cage systems for chickens by the end of this year, as well as a separate proposal to transition from farrowing crates to pen-based systems for pigs by 2027.

According to the Albert Schweitzer Foundation, no timetable has been set for calves, rabbits, ducks, geese and quail.

In the EU, about 143 million egg-laying chickens are kept in cages while the majority of female pigs spend at least part of their lives caged.

The use of cage systems in agricultural production accredited as "organic" is already prohibited in the EU.

#40. North Korea expels Swiss diplomat in sanctions row

Switzerland is one of the few Western nations to maintain dialogue with Pyongyang

North Korea has ordered Switzerland's diplomatic representative to leave the country within 48 hours and called for the office to close amid a row over sanctions.

It said the move was in response to Switzerland's decision to place sanctions on the Songdowon international children's camp. State media described this as "a blatant hostile act".

That camp is at the centre of claims that it was involved in transferring children from Russian-occupied areas of Ukraine.

North Korea's foreign ministry said "any act of violating the sovereign rights" of the country and the interests of its citizens "can never be welcomed".

Switzerland is one of the few Western nations to maintain dialogue with Pyongyang.

Swiss authorities said relations between the two countries would be maintained through the North Korean embassy in Bern, and the Swiss embassy in Beijing.

The diplomatic rebuke comes weeks after Swiss foreign minister Ignazio Cassis made a rare visit to North Korea, where he officially reopened the office and discussed normalising relations.

Diplomatic links with North Korea were first established in 1974 and Switzerland opened a co-operation office in Pyongyang in 1997.

A Swiss delegation, on left, including Foreign Minister Ignazio Cassis, met with North Korean officials in Pyongyang last month

Switzerland's federal department of foreign affairs said the country "distinguishes between its sanctions policy and its commitment to maintaining dialogue with all countries".

It added: "The reopening of the office in Pyongyang did not in any way affect Switzerland's implementation of UN sanctions related to North Korea's nuclear programme, nor its implementation of EU sanctions targeting North Korea in the context of Russia's war against Ukraine."

Thousands of North Korean soldiers have been deployed to help support Russia's war with Ukraine.

In August, Ukraine's President Volodymyr Zelensky accused Moscow of using North Korean missiles.

#37. Russian authorities detain suspect over St. Petersburg cafe blast

Our live coverage for the day has ended. Follow the latest Ukraine news here or read through the updates below.

The preventive detention hearing for Daria Trepova, who was detained in connection with the explosion at a cafe in St. Petersburg, Russia, will take place on Tuesday at Basmanny District Court in Moscow, according to Russian state news agency Vesti.

Russian authorities detained Trepova, 26, claiming she was involved in the blast that killed well-known military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky at a café in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

The US Ambassador to the United Nations Linda Thomas-Greenfield on Monday described Russia assuming the United Nations Security Council (UNSC) presidency this month as “an April Fool’s joke.”

“But the truth of the matter is, it’s a rotating seat. We expect that they will behave professionally. But we also expect that they will use their seat to spread disinformation and to promote their own agenda as it relates to Ukraine, and we will stand ready to call them out at every single moment that they attempt to do that,” Greenfield added.

Greenfield said she is not surprised if the Russians asked the foreign minister to come to UNSC headquarters in New York but also said council does work beyond Ukraine.

“We haven’t decided yet on what our attendance levels will be, but we intend to carry out the business of the Security Council during this month. The Security Council does more than Ukraine. We work on many issues, and we again expect that Russia will carry their presidency in a professional way, but when they don’t, we will stand ready to call them out,” Greenfield said.

Vasily Nebenzya, Russian ambassador to the United Nations, dismissed concerns that Russia could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the ongoing war in Ukraine.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded and pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Newly published video shows the moments running up to an explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladen Tartovsky in a St Petersburg café on Sunday.

Tatarsky died when a blast tore through the cafe where he was appearing as a guest of a pro-war group called Cyber Front Z. Prior Russian media reports suggested that Tatarsky may have been killed by a device hidden in a figurine presented to him by a woman before the blast.

The 25-second video shows Tartovsky putting a statuette given to him at the event into a box after passing the microphone to another man.

He places the statuette back into the box and then covers it with what appears to be paper wrapping. As he presses down the paper, there is an explosion.

In the last two seconds of the video, Tartovsky is pixillated, but the rest of the scene is not.

More to know: The well-known Russian blogger was killed in what appeared to be an audacious attack on a high-profile pro-Kremlin figure, officials said.

Authorities said they were treating the case as suspected murder.

At least 32 people were injured in the blast, with 10 people in serious condition, state media Ria Novosti reported, citing the Russian Ministry of Health.

Russian ambassador to the United Nations Vasily Nebenzya on Monday scoffed at concerns that his country could fairly be the president of the Security Council for April during the war in Ukraine.

Nebenzya pointed out that the US was president of the council in 2003 — the year Iraq was invaded.

He said there were no complaints in February 2022 when Russia was last president of the council, while Russia invaded.

The ambassador said as long as world order is maintained, there will not be any change in UN procedures that might lead to a change in Russia’s status.

Russia took over presidency on Saturday of the UN’s top security body, which rotates every month.

Ukraine has received the first tranche of $2.7 billion from a new International Monetary Fund (IMF) program, Ukrainian Finance Minister Sergii Marchenko said in a tweet Monday.

The IMF, which regularly makes emergency loans to countries in crisis, on Friday approved a new four-year extended arrangement under the Extended Fund Facility (EFF) of around $15.6 billion as part of a $115 billion total support package for Ukraine.

The program aims to “anchor policies that sustain fiscal, external, price and financial stability and support economic recovery, while enhancing governance and strengthening institutions to promote long-term growth in the context of post-war reconstruction and Ukraine’s path to EU accession,” the IMF said in a statement.

The program also helps Ukraine to carry out “more ambitious structural reforms,” it said.

The Extended Fund Facility (EFF) loan is the first major conventional financing program approved by the IMF for a country involved in a large-scale war, Reuters reported.

The risks to the arrangement are “exceptionally high,” said Gita Gopinath, first deputy managing director of the global lender.

“The success of the program depends on the size, composition, and timing of external financing on concessional terms to help close fiscal and external financing gaps and restore debt sustainability on a forward-looking basis under the baseline and downside scenarios,” she added.

German Chancellor Olaf Scholz on Monday assured Moldova of Berlin’s support on its path to accession to the European Union as Moldovan and American officials allege Russia of trying to weaken the government in Chisinau.

“Moldova is part of our European family. In the summer, we granted it candidate status. And I very much welcome how resolutely Moldova has tackled the necessary reforms that are indispensable for EU accession,” Scholz told a news conference in Bucharest, Romania alongside Romanian President Klaus Iohannis and Moldovan President Maia Sandu.

“Moldova can be sure of our support on this path. I assured the President [Sandu] of this once again today. Moldova does not stand alone, but receives massive international support,” the German chancellor continued.

Scholz expressed “great concern” about reports of alleged Russian attempts to destabilize Moldova and said Germany would do its “utmost” to support Moldova in arming itself against “attempts of destabilization by Russia.”

In February, Moldova’s President Sandu accused Russia of planning to use “saboteurs who have undergone military training and are disguised as civilians” to destabilize the country — claims which were rejected by Russia’s foreign ministry as “unfounded.”

According to White House officials, the US believes that Russia is working to weaken the Moldovan government, as it continues to seek closer ties with the European Union.

“The sovereignty and territorial integrity of any state is inviolable. This requirement of the Helsinki Final Act and other agreements under international law was also signed by Russia. And it is still valid. Therefore, we do our utmost to support Moldova in arming itself against attempts of destabilization by Russia,” Scholz said.

Speaking at the same event, Sandu said that “it is very important, and I am happy that Moldova is a dialogue partner with Romania and Germany. The projects we are involved in together are very useful for our people and will strongly lead us to the accession (to the European Union).”

CNN’s Radina Gigova, Anna Chernova and Natasha Bertrand contributed reporting to this post.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said Monday that the alliance has not seen any changes in Russia’s nuclear posture since Russian President Vladimir Putin announced late last month Moscow’s plans to station tactical nuclear weapons in neighboring Belarus.

“We monitor very closely what Russia does. But so far, we haven’t seen any changes in their nuclear posture that requires any change in our nuclear posture,” the NATO chief said, while answering questions from reporters on the topic at a news conference in Brussels.

Stoltenberg described Putin’s announcement as a “pattern of dangerous, reckless nuclear rhetoric.”

“President Putin tries to use nuclear weapons as a way to prevent us from supporting Ukraine… intimidation, coercion to stop NATO Allies and partners for supporting Ukraine in their right to defend their own country,” he said.

“We will not be intimidated. We will continue to support Ukraine,” Stoltenberg continued.

NATO’s chief Jens Stoltenberg said on Monday that Western allies have delivered more than $70 billion in military aid to Ukraine over the last year since the Russian invasion started.

“There are no signs that President Putin is preparing for peace. He is preparing for more war,” Stoltenberg warned, speaking at a news conference in Brussels.

“We do not know when this war will end. But when it does, we will need to put in place arrangements so that Ukraine can deter future aggression. And history does not repeat itself. We cannot allow Russia to continue to chip away at European security,” he said.

Stoltenberg added that allies have delivered 65 billion euros (around $70.7 billion) in military aid, and said he welcomed the recent arrival of modern battle tanks and other armored vehicles in Ukraine.

“This can make a real difference on the front lines and allow the Ukrainian forces to liberate more territory,” he said.

The NATO head affirmed that the alliance’s support is for the “long haul” and said, “we will discuss how we can step up our support, including by continuing to strengthen Ukraine’s armed forces and supporting the transition from Soviet-era to NATO equipment and doctrine.”

Stoltenberg also said allies must address other threats and challenges, citing instability, terrorism, and the growing influence of Iran, Russia, and China. He urged allies to invest more in defense to tackle all these wide range of issues.

Norway and Denmark will cooperate to donate 8,000 artillery rounds to Ukraine, the Norwegian government announced in a statement on Monday.

“Norway donates the shells, while Denmark donates the associated fuzes, propellant bags and primer cartridges,” the statement said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky reacted to the announcement, saying in a tweet that he was grateful for the donation.

“Grateful to the Danish and Norwegian governments led by @Statsmin and @jonasgahrstore for the joint initiative to transfer an additional batch of artillery shells to Ukraine. Timely military assistance from partners is the key to bringing our joint victory closer!” Zelensky said.

The rounds will be “standard NATO 155 mm artillery ammunition and will supplement the Danish donation of 19 Caesar self-propelled howitzers,” Norway’s statement added.

Norway’s artillery shells will be delivered from the country’s armed forces’ own stock, according to the statement.

Norwegian defense minister Bjørn Arild Gram said “Ukraine has a significant need for artillery ammunition. Norway will contribute where we can. It is important for both Europe’s and Norway’s security that Ukraine succeeds in standing up to Russia’s attack.”

“The Ukrainians still have a great and urgent need for ammunition for their fight for freedom against Russia. Denmark, together with other EU and NATO countries, is in the process of accommodating with a number of different military contributions. Our cooperation with Norway is an important example of this,” the Danish acting defense minister, Troels Lund Poulsen, said in the statement.

The Ukrainian military has reported little change to the front lines but heavy fire from Russian forces at various parts of the front line in the Donetsk region.

The General Staff said that more than 45 enemy attacks had been repelled over the past day, with the focus on Lyman, Bakhmut, Avdiivka and Mariinka.

It said there were 17 air strikes and more than 20 strikes by multiple launch rocket systems.

“In the Bakhmut direction, the enemy is trying to take full control over the city of Bakhmut and continues to storm it,” the General Staff said.

West of the city, Ukrainian defense forces repelled about 20 enemy attacks, according to the General Staff.

Oleksandr Syrskyi, commander of Ukrainian Land Forces, posted on Telegram that in Bakhmut he had met with “soldiers and commanders who destroyed the myth of invincibility of the Wagner fighters and Russian paratroopers.”

“The enemy is weakening and trying to cover up its failures with new fakes about the capture of Bakhmut,” Syrskyi said.

Syrskyi referenced the possibility of a future counter-offensive, saying that “the forward movement of our entire army depends on what every soldier is doing right now on their section of the front.”

Some of the most intense fighting is to the northwest and west of the city of Donetsk – as the Russians continue months-long efforts to dislodge Ukrainian defenses in Avdiivka and Mariinka. The General Staff said that some 20 assaults had been repelled.

“There are also some operational successes of our military in the Donetsk area. Our military have taken some positions. They have taken a more strategic and favourable position,” said Oleksii Dmytrashkivskyi, spokesperson for the military in that zone.

He also noted that Russian special forces had been introduced in the area.

“The enemy is trying to attack our positions between Avdiivka and Marinka. These attempts do not stop. It should be noted that the enemy has suffered quite significant losses,” Dmytrashkivskyi said.

He also claimed that the Russians “don’t have the same amount of equipment and, as they themselves say, they are on a ‘diet’ of ammunition for assaults.”

Social media video showed both sides engaged in heavy fire in the Avdiivka area, which the Russians have surrounded on three sides.

Denis Pushilin, the leader of the self-declared Donetsk People’s Republic, claimed that Russian forces were still advancing in the Avdiivka direction.

“It is still premature to speak of encirclement, but the situation is being created, if not critical, then extremely difficult for the enemy… After clearing Kamianka, our units are moving further. There are difficulties both with the terrain, which must also be overcome, and there are also similarities with weather conditions. But these difficulties are mutual — both for the enemy and for us,” Pushilin said.

The war in Ukraine has cost an estimated $2.6 billion of damage to heritage and cultural sites in the country, the UN cultural body UNESCO said in a recently published report.

The report, which covers one year of war in Ukraine between February 24, 2022, and February 24, 2023, was a joint assessment conducted by the Ukrainian government, the World Bank, the European Commission, and the United Nations.

UNESCO was in charge of the part of the report dedicated to culture and tourism.

“As of February 24, 2023, the total damage cost from identified assets is estimated at $2.6 billion,” the report said in its assessment of damage related to culture and tourism.

Some $1.7 billion of that cost was distributed to “historic cities, buildings, and sites imbued with recognized cultural/social values,” $650 million to tourism facilities, $143 million to “movable cultural properties and collections, repositories of culture,” and $150 million to “buildings/workshops/ateliers dedicated to cultural and creative industries,” according to UNESCO.

The most impacted region in Ukraine was Kharkivska, which has suffered 30% of the total damage to its cultural assets, the report added.

UNESCO said that losses are estimated at around $15.2 billion, which includes revenue losses from tourism, art, sports, entertainment, recreation, cultural education, and creative and cultural industries.

“The war has significantly impacted the diversity and richness of culture and cultural heritage in Ukraine, causing damage to cultural infrastructure and assets, reducing livelihoods for cultural creators, bearers and practitioners, limiting access to culture, and impeding the exercise of cultural rights,” the report said.

UNESCO’s head Audrey Azoulay is currently in Ukraine, the organization told CNN on Monday.

NATO Secretary General Jens Stoltenberg said it will be historic when the military alliance raises the Finnish flag for the first time at its headquarters on Tuesday.

“This is a historic week. Tomorrow we will welcome Finland as the 31st member of NATO, making Finland safer and our alliance stronger. We will raise the Finnish flag for the first time here at NATO Headquarters,” Stoltenberg said, speaking in Brussels Monday.

“It will be a good day for Finland’s security, for Nordic security, and for NATO as a whole,” Stoltenberg added.

Finland is set to become a member of the alliance on Tuesday, with Finnish President Sauli Niinistö traveling to Brussels to attend an accession ceremony at NATO headquarters.

Stoltenberg also spoke about Sweden’s bid to join NATO, saying Finland’s accession is “in itself something we should celebrate” but is also good for Sweden.

“It makes Sweden more integrated into NATO and makes Sweden even more safer,” Stoltenberg said, adding “at the same time we celebrate and enjoy that Finland is now full-fledged member, we should continue work to finalize the Swedish accession process.”

CNN’s Laurent Kent contributed to this report.

At least 501 children have been killed in Ukraine since February 2022, when Russia launched its full-scale invasion, said Catherine Russell, executive director of the United Nations Children’s Fund (UNICEF).

“Another tragic milestone for Ukraine’s children and families,” Russell said in a tweet on Monday.

Russell warned the real figure is “likely far higher” than the numbers verified by the UN agency.

Additionally, almost 1,000 Ukrainian children have been injured, “leaving them with wounds and scars - both visible and invisible - that could last for life,” she added.

The head of the United Nations nuclear agency, International Atomic Energy Agency, will travel to the Russian enclave of Kaliningrad in the Baltics Wednesday to discuss the safety of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia nuclear power plant, a spokesperson for the agency told CNN.

“IAEA Director General Rafael Mariano Grossi will visit Kaliningrad on Wednesday as part of his ongoing consultations aimed at ensuring the protection of Ukraine’s Zaporizhzhia Nuclear Power Plant during the military conflict,” the spokesperson said Monday.

The plant has been occupied by Russian forces since March of last year and is now run by the Russian atomic agency, Rosatom.

The situation at the nuclear plant has not improved, according to the IAEA chief, who visited the facility last week and cited increased military activity in the area.

Grossi during the visit said he was trying to formulate “realistic, viable proposals” that would be acceptable to both sides.

Twelve Ukrainian prisoners have been returned home from captivity – 10 military and two civilians, Ukraine says.

Five of them were seriously wounded, according to the Coordination Headquarters for the Treatment of Prisoners of War.

The soldiers were captured in various parts of the front lines, it added.

On March 24, “Ukraine voluntarily and unconditionally returned to Russia all five severely wounded prisoners who were transportable,” the headquarters said, but added that Russia had released “only five seriously wounded Ukrainians, not all of them, as required by the international conventions signed by Russia.”

The Russian decision showed “a blatant lack of interest in establishing further exchanges for particularly vulnerable categories of prisoners and civilian hostages,” the headquarters said.

Since the beginning of the invasion in February last year, 2,005 Ukrainians have been released from captivity, according to the headquarters.

Wagner boss Yevgeny Prigozhin said Monday that he “gave” the St. Petersburg cafe, where an explosion killed Russian military blogger Vladlen Tatarsky on Sunday, to the Cyber Front Z group, which hosted an event at the cafe.

When asked to confirm whether he was the owner of the cafe, Prigozhin didn’t deny it, in comments published Monday by his holding company Concord on its VK social media page.

“Indeed, I gave the cafe to the patriotic movement Cyber Front Z, and they held various seminars there,” Prigozhin said.

Prigozhin is the founder and sole owner of Concord, according to public records.

In the same post, Prigozhin is also commenting on who may be behind the blast.

“I do not know the details of the incident now, I was only informed that, unfortunately, Vladlen Tatarsky died. I don’t know about the rest of the details,” Prigozhin said.

“Most likely, this tragedy happened at the seminar. As for the death of Darya Dugina, yes, everything is similar,” he said referencing the death of the daughter of ultra-nationalist philosopher Alexander Dugin, who was killed by a car bomb on the outskirts of Moscow last year.

“But I would not blame the Kyiv regime for these actions. I think that there is a group of radicals that is hardly related to the government. That’s what I would call it,” Prigozhin said.

Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has declined to make any comment about the explosion that killed Russian military journalist Vladlen Tatarsky in St. Petersburg on Sunday.

Zelensky said while on a tour of the northern Chernihiv region, “About St. Petersburg. I’m not thinking what is going on in St. Petersburg, or in Moscow. They have to think. Russia have to think about their cities. I’m thinking about our country. And our cities.”

Russia has arrested an anti-war activist named Daria Trepova in connection with the explosion that killed Tatarsky and injured at least 32 others at a cafe on Sunday.

Trepova’s husband Dmitry Rylov told the independent Russian publication The Insider that he is convinced his wife “was really just set up and used.”

Some more context: Kremlin spokesperson Dmitry Peskov has said Ukraine may be behind the explosion. “There is evidence that the Ukrainian special services may be involved in the planning of this terrorist attack,” Peskov said. However, no evidence has yet been presented about who carried out the bombing.

Ukraine has said little about the explosion, beyond blaming in-fighting in Russia.

Two independent organizations in Russia — the Libertarian party and Russian opposition leader Alexey Navalny’s Anti-Corruption Foundation — have denied any association with Trepova.

Wall Street Journal reporter Evan Gershkovich has filed an appeal against his arrest, according to the Russian state news agency TASS.

The agency cited the press service of the Lefortovo court in Moscow, where he was taken into custody last week.

“The court received a complaint from Gershkovich’s defense against the choice of a preventive measure in the form of detention,” TASS reported.

No date for hearing the appeal has been set.

Gershkovich is currently being held in a pre-trial detention center at the notorious Lefortovo prison until May 29. He faces up to 20 years in prison on espionage charges.

It is the first time an American journalist has been detained on accusations by Moscow of spying since the Cold War.

#36. US Army says controlled blast of explosives in Iowa was 'huge success'

Smoke appears after explosion heard in Iowa near Army detonation site

The US military has hailed as a "huge success" the detonation of nearly five tonnes of toxic explosives in Iowa, an operation that sparked widespread fears of health and environmental problems across the region.

Smoke was seen rising into the sky shortly after 09:00 local time (14:00 GMT) following the explosion, as the Army declared the risk "as close to zero as is absolutely humanly possible".

President Donald Trump said reports showed mitigation efforts "far exceeded expectations".

Plans to detonate the lead azide, a highly volatile compound reportedly at risk of exploding on its own, were paused multiple times before Wednesday's detonation following public outcry and legal challenges.

Despite widespread concern from residents of Middletown, Iowa, ahead of the blast, officials have said that lead readings in the area so far have been promising.

"The Army's controlled, mitigated detonation exceeded all expectations and was a HUGE success," Adam Telle, acting secretary of the US Army, wrote on social media.

"We are watching EPA's [Environmental Protection Agency] MASSIVE sensor array throughout the area closely, and there are no readings so far above 0.0," he added.

The explosives had been in storage for around 20 years, and degraded as part of a process known as recrystallisation - this made them more responsive to accidental detonation from friction or other movement.

No level of lead exposure is considered safe, with children and pregnant women particularly vulnerable to harmful effects.

Middletown Mayor Rodney Johnson told the BBC he felt "relieved" and "vindicated" after the detonation.

He watched the blast from his job at a manufacturing plant eight miles (12km) away from the blast - and monitored a camera at his two-bedroom home just one mile from the detonation site.

"We did not feel anything. We did not see anything," Johnson said.

Also eight miles away from Middletown, Sandy Mabeus closed the windows and the garage at her home, brought their outdoor cat inside and hunkered down.

The family watched three separate live streams on iPads and televisions of the explosion as it happened.

"We did not feel or hear a thing here," she told the BBC, adding that she checked the videos to confirm it had actually happened.

"The waiting and wondering has not been a good thing for anybody in the area," she said. "Hopefully they had a very good result and we can just get on with things."

Iowa farmer David Vandenberg lives about 20 miles from the blast site and said he first learned of the detonation around two weeks ago. The Army Corps of Engineers took samples from his yard and pond on Tuesday.

"I was listening this morning, and I did hear a rumble about nine o'clock," he told the BBC on Wednesday, but didn't see or smell anything.

Vandenberg expects officials to return to take more samples but "was happy to see that the blast was relatively contained. "I think the effects will be minimal"

Iowa's incoming Governor-elect Rob Sand said there was "more testing to do" and called for "immediate transparency" to explain why the lead-up was "so chaotic and unclear".

Sand said that any Iowans who have been impacted in any way "must be taken care of fully, right away".

Cameron Hamilton, administrator of the Federal Emergency Management Agency (FEMA), said there would be additional resources and testing in the coming days and weeks.

In recent weeks, the US Army scheduled and rescheduled their detonation ahead of colder winter temperatures.

It also warned locals of "potential exposure to toxic lead dust", which led to some panic. It instituted measures including an optional evacuation zone and a shelter-in-place advisory ahead of the detonation.

The Army faced pushback from residents and politicians alike and there were legal challenges to prevent the detonation from going ahead on environmental and health grounds.

But on Monday, the appeals court overturned a decision to halt the blast. It said that - while these concerns were valid - the risk from an unplanned explosion was greater.

Sheila Flynn and Kwasi Gyamfi Asiedu contributed to this report

#35. US Army detonates unstable lead-based explosives in Iowa

The US Army says a controlled blast is the safest way to deal with explosive chemicals that began crystallizing in storage. US President Donald Trump said "mitigation efforts have far exceeded expectations."

The United States Army detonated nearly 5 tons of aging lead azide at an Iowa ammunition plant on Wednesday, saying the explosive chemical has become too unstable to store.

The controlled blast occurred a little after 9 a.m. local time (1500 GMT) at the Iowa Army Ammunition Plant in Middletown, and will destroy the country's entire stockpile of the chemical. Plumes of smoke were seen on camera in video footage after the controlled blast in Middletown.

The US Army has said residents of a small town and other nearby areas should leave "out of an abundance of caution."

"We don't believe that the public should be in a panic," said acting Secretary of the Army Adam Telle. "Our goal is to keep as much of it as we can here on our Iowa Army Ammunition plant to prevent it from traveling."

"Early reports from Iowa are that the Army's mitigation efforts have far exceeded expectations," US President Donald Trump said on his Truth Social platform.

The material was moved to Iowa for storage from bases around the country in 2009.

But a March inspection found that it had partly crystallized, which makes it sensitive to friction and cold and could set off a spontaneous explosion.

"Inaction is no longer an option," said Major General Eric Shirley, who leads the operation.

Rob Cekada, director of the Bureau of Alcohol, Tobacco, Firearms and Explosives, told reporters: "It's not a matter of if, it's a matter of when, and that would not be a great place for us to be in."

To limit toxic lead dust, the Army has built barriers around the bunkers holding the material and will use foam and water to trap particles.

Reuters reported that Army projections show dust could settle up to 40 miles (64 km) southwest, reaching northern Missouri.

Officials expect the wind to carry the plume away from Burlington, a city of nearly 25,000 people.

While residents within 5 miles (8 kilometers) were encouraged to evacuate, the Federal Emergency Management Agency has also advised people 5 to 20 miles away to stay indoors with windows closed.

The date of the detonation changed three times after public backlash over its impact on the environment and public health. A federal appeals court cleared the plan on Monday after environmental groups sued.

Farmer Jill Holler, who organized a protest, told Reuters: "I'm not opposed if this is the only safe option."

"What I'm saying is that it's been so mishandled that people are confused and don't trust anything they're saying," she added.
