#33. My family were killed in Ukraine. My mum blamed Zelensky's troops - the truth is complicated

Watch: BBC reporter investigates deaths of family members in Russia-Ukraine war

I am sitting on my balcony in Kyiv looking at a photo of a garden in the village I grew up in, in eastern Ukraine. The plot is overgrown with thick grass, and two wooden stakes are planted in the ground. Despite the warm sunshine, I find myself shivering.

The woman who sent me the photo says the stakes mark the graves of my grandparents, killed as the Ukrainian-Russian front line reached our little community. Not far away, in another garden in the village, the body of my father, Serezha, is buried in a greenhouse.

The photo Anastasiya was sent of her grandparents' graves

I cover the Russia-Ukraine war for the BBC World Service, but found myself in the middle of that story in February last year when my mother sent me a devastating text message.

"Granny, grandad and Serezha are gone. Now there is no-one left," it said.

Because not only had I lost three beloved family members, my mother blamed Ukraine for their deaths - the country where I was born and have lived for most of my life.

Mum was born in Siberia. From the moment Moscow launched its full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, she had supported Russia in the conflict.

Anastasiya (bottom left) with her father (top left) and grandparents (right) before the war

Backing Moscow is not that unusual in the Donbas region - the predominantly Russian-speaking part of eastern Ukraine where my village Dachne is located.

After pro-Russian forces captured large parts of the Donbas in 2014, many of those who stayed supported Moscow over Kyiv, a stance cemented by exposure to Russian state TV.

Now the Russians occupy almost 80% of Donetsk - the part of the Donbas where my village is. Over the past nearly five years, since Russia's full-scale invasion, entire towns and cities have been razed to the ground, external.

The remains of Anastasiya's family home are circled in a frame of a video, filmed by Ukraine's 33rd Mechanised Brigade on 27 January 2025

I had already fallen out with my sister Sasha, who had been living in Moscow with her young family when the war broke out. She refused to take sides - I felt hurt and cut her off.

But earlier this year, around the first anniversary of dad's death, Sasha unexpectedly texted me. We started talking over text about our childhood - the way things used to be. I realised I was not angry with her anymore. She too was grieving, missing dad just as much as I was.

Anastasiya's father Serezha - he stayed in Dachne to look after his elderly parents

I told her I wanted to make a documentary about dad and she was very supportive. I knew then that I had an ally on my journey. But we decided not to tell our mum just yet.

I started looking for witnesses to the events in Dachne that took place in January 2025, the month dad is believed to have been killed. It was not easy. It was a village of a few hundred people, but many had left, others had died, and those who remained had limited phone signals.

Russia publicly announced the capture of Dachne on 14 February and it has remained under Moscow's control ever since.

This meant it was impossible for me to travel there in person. So I began trying to contact my family's neighbours.

Anastasiya could not safely travel back to Dachne, so she worked through contacts in village group chats

In a Dachne village messaging chat, I found the number of a man I'll call Misha. I was told he had been with my father on the day he died. Speaking to the BBC could put him in danger with the Russian occupiers, so I did not tell him I worked for the BBC or that I was recording our call.

Misha told me that on 26 January he had looked in on my father and realised he had been injured. Serezha told him he had been "clipped" by a drone, and had asked Misha to bring a first aid kit, he said.

It is impossible to know which side was operating the drone that killed my father, but we do know that Russian drones were constantly targeting the village at that time.

"I asked if the wound was serious. He [Serezha] said it wasn't," said Misha - but then recalling how, when he had returned to check on my father a couple of hours later, he had found him dead.

With the help of two other villagers, Misha said they had buried him in the greenhouse.

"I'm sorry I couldn't save him," Misha told me.

BBC Eye Investigations: Who Killed My Family?

BBC journalist Anastasiya Gribanova investigates who killed her father and grandparents in Ukraine, uncovering painful accounts as she tries to reunite her divided family.

Watch on BBC iPlayer or listen on BBC Sounds now (UK only)

If you are outside of the UK, listen to the Who Killed My Family podcast here

Watch our YouTube documentary here, external

As for my grandparents, my investigations suggested the events surrounding their deaths may have been more complicated.

Their house, on the eastern side of Dachne, had been destroyed. Neighbours told me they had escaped to another home on the same street.

I eventually reached a woman I'll call Lina, who lived nearby and was the last person, as far as I know, who saw my grandparents alive.

She could not recall what this date was, but knew it was in the second half of January.

At this time, she said, "the Russians were fully advancing - the edges of the village were being shelled. Then the Ukrainian soldiers moved from the edge of the village into the centre. They hid in people's yards and used side streets to avoid drones."

She said two soldiers entered her property. They had not been wearing any identifying insignia but had been speaking Ukrainian, she said - a language that Lina described as "Bandera", a derogatory term for Ukrainians.

"You're going to have to leave. Why are you still here?" Lina said they had asked her.

She said one had pointed a machine gun at her as she had stood outside her house, while another had gone to see my grandparents. She told me she had feared for her life and then fled her house for the night.

The following day, she told me she had returned to my grandparents' street to check on them.

She had found the door to the house they had been staying in open, she said, despite the freezing conditions.

With drones buzzing overhead, Lina said she had not dared look inside, but had later heard from neighbours that my grandparents had been found dead. She believed it had been the Ukrainian soldiers who visited her house who had shot them.

Another neighbour, who I will call Maria, described discovering their bodies - my grandmother in bed, covered in a blanket, and my grandfather lying on the floor in a pool of now-dried blood.

Maria also believed Ukrainian soldiers had killed them - because, she said, that she too had felt threatened by Ukrainian troops at the time.

"If they had seen me they would have killed me too."

Her theory was that my grandparents had irritated the troops in some way.

"Your grandparents caught them in a bad mood because your grandfather was always asking to be evacuated. And [the soldiers] were, you know, high. They were taking drugs. So your grandparents got in their way," she told me.

Most of the other people in the village I spoke to also believe Ukrainian soldiers killed them, though their testimony is not based on direct evidence.

The new local mayor, appointed by the Russian occupiers when they finally took control of Dachne in February, said she had been present at my grandparents' burial the following month, and said half of my grandmother's skull was missing.

These accounts of how they might have died in January 2025 were deeply disturbing, though no-one had witnessed their deaths and the information I was given could not be independently verified.

The villagers I spoke to - now living in occupied territory - did say there had been some tensions between themselves and Ukrainian troops at the time, because the soldiers had viewed those who remained in Dachne as supporters of Russia.

The clinical causes of my grandparents' deaths are unclear.

Their bodies were not exhumed for nearly 20 months, when the local authorities sent them to a morgue in the occupied region, where a basic autopsy was carried out by a forensics expert.

He told me that my grandmother had died of an explosives injury to the skull - but could not specify its cause.

As for my grandfather, it said his body had shown no obvious signs of injury.

I compared two satellite images of the house in which their bodies were found - from just before and just after when they are believed to have died - and could detect no significant damage to the building in the second image.

I also spoke to a villager who said he had buried my grandparents.

He said he did not know anything about who killed them.

"I wasn't there. But why would you kill old people?" he asked.

Anastasiya does not know if she will ever be able to give her grandparents (pictured) a dignified burial

I examined military maps from DeepStateMap.Live, an open-source Ukrainian resource, and US think tank Institute for the Study of War. They suggested Ukrainian forces still held the street where my grandparents lived during the week they had died, although the front line had been shifting rapidly.

My colleagues helped me identify the Russian military units that went on to capture Dachne. We found telephone numbers of Russian soldiers. But none of them agreed to speak to me.

A soldier from the 33rd Mechanised Brigade, who gave his name simply as Vlad, said his unit had still been in Dachne at the time my grandparents were believed to have died and that the Russians had been swiftly advancing.

Another Ukrainian soldier I spoke to also confirmed Ukraine's troops had still been in control of some parts of Dachne in late January.

We put our findings on the deaths of my grandparents to the Ukrainian military.

It said it had no verified evidence on how they died, or which implicated its servicemen during the "intense hostilities". It added that investigating allegations of wrongdoing was the role of law enforcement.

We also approached Russia's ministry of defence and Russia's embassy in London for comment - they did not respond.

Sisters Sasha (right) and Anastasiya were reunited at Belgrade Airport

The Donetsk regional prosecutor's office, which is now based outside Russian-controlled territory, has documented alleged Russian war crimes reported by eyewitnesses in villages around Dachne during December 2024 and January 2025 - including the killing of five civilians in a basement in the nearby village of Kostyantynopilske.

However, Ukrainian investigators have no equivalent cases registered in Dachne during that period. They have documented only three deaths there. My father and grandparents are not among them.

Their bodies lie in occupied territory, and I do not know if I will ever be able to say goodbye properly and bury them with dignity.

There is little more I can do for them, except one thing - mend my relationship with my sister and my mother.

Almost three years after I last saw my sister, I fly to Belgrade where she now lives.

Anastasiya (left) and her sister Sasha look through photos of their father

She is waiting for me at the airport, holding lilies, and a plate with a silly nickname on it that she gave me when we were children. We hug, cry and laugh. It feels as if something between us is finally falling back into place

At her flat, we look through family photographs and talk about my investigation and what I was able to find. My grief is still there, but it hurts a little less. I'm no longer carrying it alone.

"Are you ready?" Sasha asks. She rings our mother - who has also moved to Serbia - and puts the phone on speaker.

"Hi, Mum. It's Nastya," I say. First there is silence. Then I hear her crying. "Nastya, my darling, hello," she replies.

#32. €561 Billion and Still Not Enough: Germany's 2027 Budget Exposes a Coalition on the Brink

The budget for 2027 has reached new highs, and pension funds, defense spending and a weak economy are increasing the pressure. At the same time, tensions are mounting within the government.

At first glance, everything seems to be business as usual. Lawmakers in the Bundestag, the lower house of parliament, are holding four days of final debates on how much money the federal government may spend in the coming year, and on what. These follow a familiar pattern: The government pats itself on the back, while the opposition voices criticism. But anyone listening more closely will notice that there are doubts even in the ranks of the coalition parties, the conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), their Bavarian allies, the Christian Social Union (CSU), and the center-left Social Democratic Party (SPD).

"As a country, we are in a difficult situation geopolitically, technologically, and demographically, and next year's budget is an accurate reflection of that," Chancellor Friedrich Merz (CDU) acknowledged on Wednesday in the Bundestag.

"If we don't change course, in a few years we'll find ourselves in a situation where all federal revenue will have to be spent on social programs, interest payments, and defense," he reflected on Wednesday during a four-hour debate focused on government policy as a whole, rather than the individual budgets of the ministries.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

This government has now been in office for about 20 months. Back in early 2025, expectations were enormous. Merz had promised to bring the budget back in line with the debt brake enshrined in the constitution, which stipulates that the government can spend only slightly more than it collects (with exceptions now made for defense and security).

Yet for 2027, spending — at just under €561 billion ($614 billion) — will be higher than ever before, and tax revenues are nowhere near sufficient to cover this, meaning Germany is looking at record borrowing for the year totaling €210 billion.

The largest opposition party, the far-right Alternative for Germany (AfD) calculated that the government will incur €401,000 in new debt every minute next year. In the Bundestag, AfD co-leader Tino Chrupalla called it a "debt orgy" that will paralyze the country. Merz will "go down in history as the chancellor who led Germany to bankruptcy," he exclaimed.

Germany's budget deficit has only ever been higher in the COVID-19 pandemic. The current pressures are obvious: The economy is struggling, the population aging, and Russia's war against Ukraine is about to enter its sixth year.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The defense budget is now by far the highest in the history of the Federal Republic, with €109 billion allocated for 2027, an increase of €27 billion on last year. Added to this is the multi-year, debt-financed special military fund, intended to modernize the armed forces, bringing the total defense budget to €139 billion.

The AfD and the socialist Left Party have sharply criticized this. Left Party parliamentary leader Heidi Reichinnek accused the government of pursuing "militarization and boundless rearmament on the one hand, and brutal social cuts and an orgy of budget cuts on the other."

The AfD's Chrupalla said that the temporary reduction in the electricity tax, pushed through at the last minute, would save the average family a mere €100 a year, while Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy "walks out of the chancellery here in Berlin with a check for €200 million after just one afternoon."

"Whose chancellor are you, Mr. Merz?" he asked.

The opposition Greens did not agree. While they also criticized the budget as "unserious," Green Party Bundestag member Britta Haßelmann described the AfD's stance on Ukraine as shameful. She rejected the idea that money was being wasted on Ukraine, saying that supporting Ukraine bolstered security for Germany and Europe.

The chancellor shares this view. "You can't say it often enough, either to the far right or the far left: Russia knows exactly what it wants. And Russia wants more than just Ukraine," Merz said.

He added that Russia is escalating tensions in the Baltic region, in Poland, in the Black Sea, and in space; and that Moscow is also seeking escalation in Germany, even at the cost of lives. "That is why we must show Russia its limits," he said.

But it is becoming increasingly difficult for the government to justify the high spending on Ukraine. Germany's pension and health care costs are only getting bigger: By far the largest single budget item is that of the Ministry of Labor and Social Affairs, which is rising to over €200 billion, even with cuts to unemployment benefits.

Work is currently underway on a reform of the pension system — something that many see as urgent, as the workforce born in the 1960s, when more than a million children were born in Germany every year, is on brink of retiring. Birth rates nearly halved by the mid-1970s and declined even further afterward. For now, so-called "boomers" still make up one-third of the current workforce — but soon they will be drawing pensions.

Even now, Germany's pensions cannot be financed solely with contributions on wages and salaries. In 2025, just over €420 billion in pensions were paid out, with the government contributing €127 billion of that amount. By 2027, the government aims to save €3 billion on this figure, which is likely to further increase pension contributions.

The coalition parties are stuck in a row over how to reform the pension system. Chancellor Merz insisted in the Bundestag that the coalition was solving the country's fundamental problems, but he warned that in future, tax revenues "will no longer be enough to afford everything we have become all too accustomed to," he said. "All of us here will have to give up some of the ideas we cherish."

The SPD, on the other hand, is counting on increasing federal revenue. Its parliamentary leader Matthias Miersch emphasized in the Bundestag that large fortunes and inheritances must contribute more to "public costs."

That, he said, must also apply to large companies. Currently, heirs to family-owned businesses often pay significantly less in taxes than heirs to real estate or financial assets, because they can be exempted from inheritance tax if they continue to run the business. This is intended to protect them from having to pay taxes in the event of a breakup or sale of the company.

According to Miersch, the government must now think about taxing the wealthy more. "For us, this issue is by no means off the table; on the contrary, we are putting it on the agenda," he said. The government's revenue situation is "an issue we need to discuss."

All in all, the outlook for 2027 is strained — and not just financially. The political differences between the CDU/CSU and the SPD are likely to make working together even more difficult. Conflict is inevitable, especially with several state elections scheduled for the spring.

The chancellor knows this and tried to appear confident that better collaboration was possible. "We must seek compromise in our joint decision-making, represented by these — admittedly quite different — coalition partners," he said.

This article was originally written in German.

#27. Spain: Canary Islands witness 1-day migration record

The arrival of over 2,000 migrants to the islands comes as Spain holds elections this weekend. The president of the Canary Islands said the archipelago is facing "unprecedented migration pressure."

The Spanish Canary Islands witnessed a one-day migration record on Tuesday, with over 2,100 people arriving to the region in over 24 hours.

Most of the migrants landed on the the island of El Hierro, according to Spanish authorities.

"The Canaries can't take it anymore. We're experiencing an unprecedented migration pressure," President of the Canary Islands Fernando Clavijo wrote on X. He requested an urgent meeting with European Commission President Ursula von der Leyen to discuss an immediate EU response.

Around 100 minors arrived on Tuesday, with a baby also born during the trip from Africa to the islands.

The Canaries, a tourist destination whose shortest gap with northwest Africa is around 100 kilometers (around 62 miles), tends to see more migrant arrivals in the fall when the waters are calmer. The waters become rougher in the winter due to storms and swells.

Earlier this month, nearly 1,600 migrants from West Africa were saved and taken to the Canary Islands in a previous record. Over 26,000 migrants came to the Canaries this year so far — with half of that figure in November alone.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

The route from Africa to the Canaries is one of the deadliest in the world for migrants.

Boats taking off for the islands sometimes drift out to open sea, with the migrants onboard facing starvation, dehydration and lack of medical care. Human smugglers frequently organize the trips and abuse the migrants.

The spike in migration to the Canaries comes as Spain will hold a snap election on Sunday.

Migration, along with housing, will be one of the topics at the forefront of voters' minds when they cast their ballots.

Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sanchez, who belongs to the center-left Socialist Party, has faced backlash from the opposition regarding his tolerant attitude towards migration. The Spanish government earlier this year enacted a plan to legalize up to 500,000 undocumented migrants.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In late July, over 80,000 migrants crossed from Morocco into the Spanish North African enclave of Ceuta, triggering a crisis for the Sanchez government. Countries such as Germany and Italy tightened or extended border controls due to the influx of migrants into Ceuta.

The far-right Vox party, which could receive its best-ever result in upcoming election, has called for Sanchez and his ministers to be prosecuted for treason over the Ceuta crisis. The conservative Popular Party could form a coalition government with Vox after the election.

#24. US stocks fall further as fears over AI intensify

Big technology firms connected to AI continued to see share prices fall on Wednesday, with stock in Nvidia and Anthropic dropping sharply in early trading.

The surge in Nvidia shares after boss Jensen Huang was reinstated was temporary, and the general stock sell-off continued, with all three main US stock market indices down. The tech-dominated Nasdaq has shed over 2%.

The declines come amid concerns over the level and sustainability of AI investment which has rattled investors in recent weeks.

Shares in other major tech firms, including Amazon, Tesla and SpaceX, Micrsoft and Meta were also down.

Nvidia's share price was down 8% in early trading on Wednesday, extending Tuesday's slide as the boost from Huang's return eased.

The resignation and subsequent reinstatement of Nvidia boss Jensen Huang after revelations of his extra marital affairs has seen the market value of the world's premier AI chipmaker fluctuate wildly.

Given Nvidia's position as both supplier and financier of many other firms involved in the AI rollout, its rocky fortnight has had a wider impact.

The US government also announced a raft of new tariffs on countries and trading partners on Wednesday as part of President Donald Trump's flagship trade policy.

The value of big tech companies have sheltered the US economy from global economic shocks, including tariffs, but share prices in recent weeks have been volatile in response to the US midterm elections and other events in the AI industry.

Faisal Islam: Fears over the AI boom haven't gone away

Investors have taken fright at Democrat gains in the US midterm elections, with many having campaigned for tougher regulation of AI firms and even a moratorium on the development of new data centres.

Questions also remain about whether the huge sums being invested in AI will eventually pay off.

"It seems that these companies are trying to one-up each other on the amount of money that they're spending but you have to show that it's worth it, and that's where Wall Street is saying I don't know," said Todd Schoenberger, chief investment officer at CrossCheck Management.

Despite concerns, investment in AI is expected to exceed $1 trillion in 2026, according to investment bank Goldman Sachs, which said the technology will remain the "dominant driver of business investment growth" next year.

Shares in Anthropic continued to trade below the $83 they debuted at last week, while Amazon shares fell by more than 5%.

The declines come ahead of the US stock market closing for Thanksgiving on Thursday before opening for a shortened session on Friday.

#22. Peter Magyar meets Volodymyr Zelenskyy: Hungary and Ukraine make a fresh start after the icy relations of the Viktor Orban era

After years of frosty and hostile relations under Hungary's Kremlin-friendly former PM Viktor Orban, his successor, Peter Magyar, and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy seek to reset their countries' ties.

The location and the gesture were carefully chosen: Hungarian Prime Minister Peter Magyar and Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy stood side by side, laid flowers and bowed their heads at the military cemetery in the center of the Ukrainian city of Uzhhorod.

One of those buried there is an ethnic Hungarian who was one of the first soldiers killed in Russia's full-scale war of aggression against Ukraine.

Magyar intentionally chose not to begin his first visit to Ukraine as prime minister at a government building. Instead, his Tuesday afternoon visit began at a place where he could demonstrate his solidarity with Ukraine more clearly than anywhere else.

Uzhhorod, known as Ungvar in Hungarian, is the capital of Ukraine's Transcarpathia region, which is home to around 80,000 members of Ukraine's Hungarian minority. Thousands of ethnic Hungarians are fighting Russian occupying forces on the front lines.

For Hungary, where ethnic Hungarian minorities in neighboring countries traditionally play an important role, the war is therefore closer to home than it is for other European countries.

Magyar described his visit to Ukraine as a "historic day" in a Facebook post. Before the ceremony at the cemetery in Uzhhorod, he said: "I bring with me the solidarity of the Hungarian people. We stand with Ukraine against the Russian aggressor."

Zelenskyy responded on X, saying "We appreciate this gesture of respect toward those who sacrificed their lives for Ukraine's independence."

The meeting between Magyar and Zelenskyy marked a turning point in Hungary's approach to Ukraine.

The two leaders repeatedly shook hands throughout the day. Such scenes would have been unthinkable just a few months ago. The propaganda of Magyar's pro-Russian predecessor, Viktor Orban, portrayed Ukraine as a "mafia state" and "lawless country" on an almost daily basis.

Under Orban, relations between Hungary and Ukraine reached a historic low.

Now the two countries are seeking a political reset, which is important not only for Hungary and Ukraine but for Europe as a whole. After all, Orban's repeated obstruction of support for Ukraine drove the EU to the brink of despair.

During talks in Uzhhorod, the two leaders also agreed to closer economic cooperation.

The talks centered on Hungarian diesel supplies to Ukraine, greater use of Ukrainian gas storage facilities by Hungarian companies, and joint infrastructure projects, including the opening of new border crossings and a direct rail link between Budapest and Kyiv.

Nevertheless, no formal agreements were signed on Tuesday.

At a subsequent news conference, journalists asked questions about sensitive issues between the two countries: minority rights, Ukraine's EU integration and a new EU loan for Ukraine.

Throughout the day, the two men were visibly keen to avoid any discord.

Magyar, for example, described his angry outburst over Ukraine's "Carpathian Eight" initiative in parliament in late September as a "misunderstanding" and suggested that Hungary might, after all, take part.

Zelenskyy's cautious replies to journalists suggest that he knows his country's path to EU membership also runs through Budapest.

One of Magyar's statements on Tuesday was key: "We've finally put a stop to this completely unnecessary and inexplicable political escalation."

Even before taking office in early May 2026, Magyar had promised to reset relations with Ukraine. But the process has not been entirely smooth.

In many respects — but certainly not all — Magyar is distancing himself from his predecessor. He has made no bones of the fact that he sees Russia as the aggressor, but he rejects both arms deliveries to Ukraine and even the transit of weapons across Hungarian territory.

Magyar previously accused Ukraine of treating ethnic Hungarians as second-class citizens — a claim that is not borne out by the facts. He pressed for greater minority rights and for a time blocked the opening of negotiating chapters in Ukraine's EU accession talks.

Magyar also departed from diplomatic convention by choosing the location of his meeting with Zelenskyy in Ukraine: Indeed, it was Magyar who invited Zelenskyy to Transcarpathia.

Magyar's sometimes confrontational tone toward Ukraine can also be explained by the need to avoid appearing too pro-Ukrainian to the Hungarian public.

According to a November 2026 poll conducted by the think tank Globsec, 33% of Hungarians would now support their country providing military aid to Ukraine. Although this is much more than during Orban's time in office, many Hungarians remain skeptical of Ukraine.

That's why Olena Halushka, a Ukrainian political scientist at the International Centre for Ukrainian Victory (ICUV), sees some aspects of Magyar's visit as a concession to public opinion in Hungary.

"Magyar has taken over a deeply polarized country," she told DW's Ukrainian Service. "A visit to Transcarpathia is more of a step for the Hungarian population than for Ukraine."

Ukraine accommodated not only Magyar's choice of location but also Hungary's demands for better protection of minority rights, which were set out in a 10-point plan in June. That's because EU integration is one of the country's top political priorities.

After planned restrictions on the use of minority languages were withdrawn in late 2023, Ukraine's parliament in early October passed a law on minority language rights that in some respects goes even further than European standards.

Hungary subsequently dropped its obstruction of the opening of negotiating clusters for Ukraine's EU accession.

Laszlo Zubanics, chairman of the Hungarian Democratic Federation in Ukraine (UMDSZ, one of two Hungarian political parties in Transcarpathia), told DW that the Magyar-Zelenskyy meeting was "historic."

He particularly welcomed the fact that Ukraine will probably implement practical improvements in the protection of minority rights "not only by the end of 2027, but much sooner, within just a few months."

A Hungarian diplomat who spoke to DW on condition of anonymity also called the meeting "historic," adding that after the Orban government's anti-Ukrainian policies, trust now needs to be rebuilt, and that personal meetings between the two leaders play an important role in that process.

The diplomat went on to say that Hungary not only wants to ensure that the rights of the Hungarian minority are protected, it also has a strong interest in being involved in Ukraine's reconstruction.

Overall, the diplomat concluded, both sides are very keen to expand economic cooperation.

Hungarian political scientist Daniel Hegedus of the Institute for European Politics (IEP) in Berlin told DW that while the meeting was a "symbolic milestone," it will be important to see "how the two political leaders capitalize on the symbolic political capital generated by this meeting."

No observers have suggested that this is the grand finale of reconciliation between Hungary and Ukraine.

Nevertheless, Tuesday's reset sends an important signal to Europe, namely that if the political will is there, a deep gulf that has existed for years can be transformed into a set of diverging interests that can be addressed through negotiations.

Or, as Zelenskyy put it, now there are "all the opportunities to build hopefully constructive relations."

This article was originally published in German.

#20. Israeli judges recommend dropping most serious charges against Netanyahu

Benjamin Netanyahu called for his trial to be ended "immediately"

Judges in Israel have suggested prosecutors drop bribery charges against Benjamin Netanyahu, potentially handing the caretaker prime minister a boost as he fights for his political survival.

The judges told the attorney general that there was little chance of conviction in the first of three cases he is facing, in which he is accused of granting government favours in exchange for positive media coverage.

They also questioned whether his actions amounted to criminal conduct in a second corruption case, and suggested prosecutors seek a plea deal in a third.

Netanyahu has consistently denied the allegations against him and called on Wednesday for the trial to be ended "immediately".

"Ten years of terrible suffering for me, my family, my son," he said in a televised statement.

"Ten years of incitement to hatred that wounds the soul."

Netanyahu was first charged in 2019, but court proceedings have been prolonged in part by the recent Israel-Gaza war.

The case now under serious doubt, known as Case 4,000, concerns an alleged "reciprocal arrangement" between Netanyahu and the controlling shareholder in Israel's biggest telecoms company. Both denied any wrongdoing.

In a highly unusual step, a judging panel at the Jerusalem District Court summoned Israeli Attorney General Gali Baharav-Miara on Wednesday to express their concerns.

"The chances of conviction are low â to an extent that warrants reconsidering the matter," Judge Rivka Friedman-Feldman told her, Israeli media reported.

The judges also cast doubt on whether Netanyahu's actions, which are subject to a second case â concerning another alleged quid-pro-quo with another media baron, known as Case 2,000 â met the threshold for criminality.

While they left open the possibility of a conviction in the third case: Case 1,000, which alleges Netanyahu acted for the benefit of an Australian billionaire after being gifted cigars and champagne by him.

Netanyahu: What are the corruption charges?

However, the judges told the attorney general that, in the public interest, there needed to be a "rethink" in how the trial should move forward, Israeli media report.

Baharav-Miara said she would consider the judges' recommendations.

It has been six years since Netanyahu became Israel's first sitting prime minister to face a criminal trial.

He has always claimed to be the victim of a political witch-hunt and said on Wednesday that the "fabricated cases" against him were now falling apart.

The cases have continued to progress in court while Netanyahu remained in power throughout the war in Gaza, though judges agreed to reduce the number of hearings due to security concerns around the conflict.

The accusations against him have rocked Israeli politics, fuelling already deep divisions. His trial has coincided with controversial, far-reaching judiciary reforms by Netanyahu's government.

For his opponents, Netanyahu's fear of prison has distorted his decision-making as prime minister and made him especially dependent on his far-right coalition partners to remain in power.

This latest development will give a boost to Netanyahu, whose political opponent Gadi Eisenkot is currently attempting to form a coalition government after an inconclusive general election last month.

While Netanyahu's parliamentary bloc won more seats than that of his rival, both fell short of an outright majority. Israel's president has offered Eisenkot the first chance to form a new administration.

Netanyahu remains subject to an arrest warrant from the International Criminal Court for alleged war crimes and crimes against humanity in Gaza, which he also denies.

Israel's new parliament sworn in as leaders compete to form government

Israel's president tasks opposition leader Eisenkot with forming government

#17. Nigeria detains 5 Mexicans, confiscates over $220 million of meth

Nigeria's anti-drug agency said Mexican experts were allegedly recruited by a Nigerian national to run a meth laboratory in the southeastern state of Enugu.

Nigeria's National Drug Law Enforcement Agency (NDLA) said Wednesday it had arrested five Mexicans and confiscated over $220 million (around €200 million) in methamphetamine as part of a significant drug bust.

The agency said the Mexican experts were allegedly recruited by a Nigerian national to run a meth laboratory in the southeastern Nigerian state of Enugu.

"A total of 1,512 kilograms of methamphetamine, finished and in production, with an estimated value of $226.8 million was recovered from the clandestine factory," NDLA spokesperson Femi Babafemi said.

Nigeria first rejected the entry of the Mexicans into the country in August. They later entered Nigeria again in September via neighboring Benin using forged passports, with authorities then putting them under surveillance.

On November 11, the Mexicans were taken into custody at the Enugu lab after their movements were tracked by the Nigerian government. A Nigerian was also arrested in a nearby hotel.

The latest drug bust comes after Nigerian authorities in May also detained 10 people in a anti-meth operation in the southwest, including three Mexicans.

The arrests raise concern that Mexican cartels are getting a foothold in Africa for international drug trafficking. In addition to Nigeria, South Africa is another country where Mexicans have been arrested for being involved in a meth lab.

According to Vanda Felbab-Brown, an expert at the Washington DC-based Brookings Institution, Mexican cartels such as the Sinaloa Cartel and the Cartel de Jalisco Nueva Generation (CJNG) have expanded their operations into other continents such as Africa.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

In a June testimony to the US Senate Caucus on International Narcotics Control, Felbab-Brown said the cartels find "desirable partners" for production in countries such as South Africa and Kenya due to factors such as "limited law enforcement capacities and entrenched criminal and corrupt networks."

According to the UN Office on Drugs and Crime,criminal networks often take advantage of porous borders in the West and Central Africa region to carry out activities such as illicit drug trafficking.

"Many countries in the region have vast territory and borders are located either in very remote or densely populated areas, thus making law enforcement activities particularly difficult to conduct in an efficient manner," the office said. "The porous nature of the borders in West and Central Africa is a major factor of insecurity: the borders are neither demarcated or sufficiently controlled."

#15. Images of asteroid targeted to test Earth's defences reveal stadium-sized crater

The asteroid before (left) and after, showing the crater left behind by a spacecraft's impact

The first images of an asteroid that Nasa deliberately crashed into have revealed a stadium-sized crater on its surface.

The extensive damage was caused when a Nasa spacecraft hit the asteroid Dimorphos in 2022. The asteroid posed no threat, but this was a test of our planetary defences to see if it was possible to knock a space rock off course.

Four years on, a European Space Agency (ESA) spacecraft called Hera is scouring the scene to fully assess the aftermath of the collision.

"The crater is almost as large as the body itself, which is really, really spectacular," said Dr Sabina Raducan, a planetary scientist, who is on the Hera science team.

"It's hard to describe in words all the excitement to actually finally see what happened. And as a crater person, I can also say it's beautiful."

The original 2022 crash test was carried out by a Nasa spacecraft called Dart which hurtled into Dimorphos at 14,000mph (22,000km/h).

It was such a big smash-up that the trail of debris, measuring about 6,200 miles long (10,000km), was spotted by telescopes on Earth and in space.

Dimorphos is about 150m (500ft) wide and was partly chosen as the target because it is one of an asteroid pair - it orbits around a larger space rock called Didymos, which is about 730m (2,400ft) wide.

The crash showed it was possible to change the path of the smaller asteroid.

Before the collision, it took Dimorphos 11 hours and 55 minutes to orbit around Didymos. After the crash, the duration of its orbit had shrunk by about 33 minutes.

Watch: Moment Nasa spacecraft leaves stadium-sized crater in asteroid

A key objective of the Hera mission is to measure the mass of Dimorphos, in order to work out how effective the spacecraft's shove was.

This information would be vital if we ever spotted a space rock that was on a collision course with Earth.

"It would help us to predict what would happen if we had to hit another asteroid," said Prof Simon Green, a planetary scientist who is on Hera's science team.

"We [could] measure its properties and say: 'Well, we need this mass of spacecraft, travelling at this speed, this early, in order to deflect it enough so that it misses the Earth'."

Hera's arrival on the scene is helping scientists answer many more questions too.

Not only did the collision alter the shape of Dimorphos, it also changed the way it moves.

"Before the Dart impact, Dimorphos was more like our own Moon - so it was always showing the same face to Didymos, and it was behaving quite nicely," explained Raducan.

"Now it's very, very chaotic - it's tumbling. Imagine a barrel that you throw down the stairs, and the barrel is rolling and it's moving, and basically that's what Dimorphos does right now around Didymos."

Hera has also taken detailed images of Didymos, revealing its surface in detail.

"The gravity at the surface of Didymos is about 40,000 times less than the gravity on Earth," said Naomi Murdoch, who is on the Hera mission team.

"We can already see some fascinating and complex geology. We can see regions of boulders.

"The smallest details that we can make out in these images are about 3m in size. So that means that all the boulders that you can see with your eyes are at least 3m big, and some as big as 10m. So what we're seeing here is boulders the size of cars and buses sitting on a world with almost no gravity."

The mission has sent back new pictures of the smaller Dimorphos orbiting around Didymos

The Hera mission will spend the next six months studying Dimorphos and Didymos, sending back more images taken from closer up in higher resolution.

"Hera is a sort of Inspector Columbo going back to the crash scene to investigate what is the outcome of this impact," said Patrick Michel, principal investigator of ESA's Hera mission.

The spacecraft is packed with instruments and also has two shoe-boxed sized satellites onboard, which can study the impact site up close, and then later land on the asteroid.

But the first images taken by Hera have also revealed a new challenge. Dimorphos and Didymos are travelling through space surrounded by hundreds of boulders ranging in size from tens of centimetres to metre-size rocks.

This will make it especially challenging to operate the spacecraft and cube satellites through this boulder field.

A long trail of debris from the collision was spotted by the Hubble Space Telescope

This was the last image from Dart before it crashed into Dimorphos in 2022

Asteroids pose a significant threat to our planet.

The biggest, which measures more than a kilometre across, could cause global devastation. But medium-sized asteroids around the same dimensions as Dimorphos could wipe out a city.

"We all know what happened to the dinosaurs, right? And we don't want that to happen to us," said Carolyn Ernst at Johns Hopkins Applied Physics Laboratory, who has worked on both the Dart and Hera missions.

"The good news for us is that there are far fewer potential threats out there in the Solar System than there were millions of years ago. But there are objects out there."

She said the biggest asteroids have been spotted, and none are heading to Earth.

"The smallest stuff isn't too much of a threat because our atmosphere protects us," she added.

"But there's a size range in the middle where we don't know where all of them are, and it could, if impacted, cause problems for humanity. And if we can find out about that and know about that, well, we sure as heck would want to try to stop that."

Artist's impression showing Hera. It has two small satellites on board to study the asteroid up close

Scientists want to gather as much intelligence as they can about space rocks to make us as well equipped as possible to deal with whatever the Universe throws at us.

A new mission is launching in 2027 to track potentially hazardous asteroids - a space telescope called the Near-Earth Object (NEO) Surveyor.

There are also more visits planned to asteroids, including to one called Apophis, which is about 450m (1,480ft) long.

Apophis was discovered in 2004 and it was initially thought it might hit the Earth as early as 13 April 2029.

But now scientists understand its orbit better, and say it won't hit us for at least the next 100 years. Instead of a crash in 2029, it will fly just 32,000km (20,000 miles) above our surface.

This will give scientists the rare chance to study a space rock up-close.

Two spacecraft will head to Apophis, including Nasa's Osiris-Apex (which previously grabbed a sample from another asteroid called Bennu) and Ramses, a joint mission between ESA and the Japanese Space Agency (JAXA).

Nasa's Dart spacecraft 'changed path of asteroid'

#11. US announces new tariffs on host of countries including China

Watch: What we know about Trump's Thanksgiving tariffs

The US has announced a fresh raft of tariffs as President Donald Trump pushes ahead with his signature trade policy.

The new taxes charged on imports to America are set to hit many of the country's biggest trading partners - China, the European Union and 12 other jurisdictions.

The move follows the White House's investigation into the trade practices of other countries in March after the US Supreme Court ruled Trump's previously announced Liberation Day tariffs were illegal.

China is facing the highest rate of up to 40%, followed by Vietnam at up to 35% and then others facing either 20% or 25%.

The new duties are not expected to take effect until next year.

Tariffs are taxes levied on imported goods. The amount is typically calculated as a percentage of the good's value and paid to the government by businesses importing the products from abroad.

Some of these rates will be effectively reduced because of caps on tariffs Trump has agreed to. Individual countries also face different levies.

The proposed tariffs will affect China, the European Union, Singapore, Switzerland, Indonesia, Malaysia, Cambodia, Thailand, Korea, Vietnam, Taiwan, Bangladesh, Japan, and India.

US Trade Representative Jamieson Greer said the latest tariffs issued would "level the playing field for American workers and chart a new, more sustainable course for the world economy".

He claimed that many of America's trading partners had "for decades" adopted industrial strategies which included "targeting manufacturing, wage and labor suppression" as well as "other market distorting practices that tilt the trade balance in their favor while eroding America's manufacturing base".

But economists have warned tariffs can fuel inflation and push prices up for consumers if businesses pass on higher costs, as well as dampen global trade.

Trump has repeatedly threatened or imposed tariffs during his second term, arguing they will boost US manufacturing and create jobs.

Despite a poor result for the Republican party in the midterm elections, the president has continued to press on with his flagship trade policy.

Trump is also seeking to cut the US trade deficit - the gap between the value of goods it buys from other countries and those it sells to them.

Trump's so-called Liberation Day tariffs were struck down by the Supreme Court earlier this year

However, Stephen Brown, chief North America economist for Capital Economics, said the latest tariff announcement was "not as alarming as it looks", due to the taxes "essentially replacing" previously announced tariffs made in the summer.

"The increase in the effective tariff rate looks set to be a touch lower than we've been assuming for our forecasts, although the new tariffs will still provide a marginal boost to prices," he said in a note.

"The key message is that the additional tariff burden is likely to be small, especially as various goods... are also not subject to these new tariffs."

The announcement is likely to increase tensions between the US and China, the world's two biggest economies, who remain locked in a trade dispute and the AI race.

The two countries have also been embroiled in a tit-for-tat tariffs war, which is currently on hold.

Despite last week's meeting between Trump and China's President Xi Jinping, the two sides made no announcement on the trade war truce, which is set to expire on 10 January.

The European Commission rejected the new US tariffs, saying it would investigate the US's reasons for bringing them in. It pointed out that tariffs are limited to the 15% previously agreed.

What tariffs has Trump introduced and why?

#8. Trump pleads not guilty to 34 felony counts

Our live coverage of Trump’s historic indictment has moved.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan wrote his Monday night order that the media’s request to broadcast the arraignment of former President Donald Trump was understandable, but that the news organizations’ interests in providing the broadest possible access to the proceedings must be weighed against “competing interests.”

In rejecting the request to broadcast the arraignment live on television, Merchan still wrote about the historical significance of the proceeding in stark terms.

“That this indictment involves a matter of monumental significance cannot possibly be disputed. Never in the history of the United States has a sitting or past President been indicted on criminal charges. Mr. Trump’s arraignment has generated unparalleled public interest and media attention,” he wrote. “The populace rightly hungers for the most accurate and current information available. To suggest otherwise would be disingenuous.”

News outlets will not be allowed to broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday in a New York state court, a judge said Monday night, but he will allow some photographers to take pictures in the courtroom before the proceedings formally begin.

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan rejected the request by several media organizations, including CNN, for permission to broadcast the historic proceedings. Trump’s arraignment — like most arraignments in the Manhattan courthouse — is a public proceeding, but news cameras are not usually allowed to broadcast from inside the courtroom.

However, the judge is allowing five pool photographers to take still photos at the beginning of the proceedings “until such time as they are directed to vacate the jury box by court personnel.”

Earlier on Monday, Trump’s lawyers urged the judge to reject the media’s request for live cameras in the courtroom. The Manhattan District Attorney’s office told the judge they didn’t have a position.

The media outlets that tried to get cameras into the courtroom argued that “the gravity of this proceeding … and, consequently, the need for the broadest possible public access, cannot be overstated.”

Trump is now in Manhattan ahead of the arraignment. A grand jury indicted the former president last week.

The arraignment is also expected to bring the unsealing of the criminal charges against Trump, which have not yet been seen by his lawyers or the public.

The indictment stems from Manhattan District Attorney Alvin Bragg’s investigation into hush-money payments, made during the 2016 presidential campaign, to women who claimed they had extramarital affairs with Trump, which he denies.

Trump denies all wrongdoing and his lawyers said last week that they’ll fight to get the charges dropped.

Donald Trump is not planning to speak publicly in New York on Tuesday, aides say, if he follows advice from members of his legal team who have urged the former President to refrain from the vitriol he’s been directing toward the prosecutor and judge presiding over the case.

But that restraint does not extend to Florida, aides said, where he is planning to deliver a full-throated defense of his New York case as well as other investigations he is facing during a prime time address from Mar-a-Lago on Tuesday night.

It’s the first test to see whether Trump can navigate two vasty different approaches to his legal case and his political campaign, which are now inextricably linked.

“He’s taking this very seriously and following legal advice,” an adviser to Trump said Monday night. “He will make his case to Americans Tuesday night at Mar-a-Lago.”

Alina Habba, who represents Donald Trump in several civil matters, spent time with the former president in New York today and said, “He’s in good spirits. Honestly, he’s as he normally would be. He’s ready to go in and do what he needs to do tomorrow.”

Habba — appearing on on Fox News’ “Jesse Watters” — was asked about the “game plan” for Trump’s appearance in court in Manhattan Tuesday and said, “It’s all mapped out.”

She added, “Barring any surprises, I think that it should be smooth. We’re trying to coordinate and cooperate with everybody to make sure that there are no problems.”

On whether Trump can get a fair trial in Manhattan, Habba said, “No, no. I think it’s very difficult. I’d like to have faith in this state, but I’ve been practicing for him now for a couple years and gone to court in New York for a few years, and I can tell you, it’s not the same as representing anybody else.”

Former President Donald Trump met with his attorneys Susan Necheles and Joe Tacopina at Trump Tower after arriving in New York City Monday, a source familiar with the meeting told CNN.

It is unclear if the newest addition to Trump’s New York legal team, Todd Blanche, attended the meeting.

Trump is expected to be arraigned Tuesday.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

History was made Thursday when he was indicted by a New York grand jury. It’s the first time that a current or former US president has been criminally charged.

The Manhattan district attorney’s office has been investigating Trump’s alleged role in a hush money payment scheme and cover-up involving adult film star Stormy Daniels that dates to the 2016 presidential campaign.

Trump’s attorney said he plans to file “substantial” legal challenges. The former president has repeatedly denied wrongdoing in the matter and accused Democrats of targeting him politically.

See more photos of the days leading up to Trump’s indictment:

A small group of supporters chanted as former President Donald Trump arrived at his namesake tower on Monday afternoon, with a handful of people waving banners with messages like “Trump won” and “Arrest Biden.”

While Trump couldn’t see his admirers as he entered a side door on 56th Street, his arrival came with more fanfare and security than at any other time in his post presidency.

Now that he is inside the building — where he launched his presidential bid nearly eight years ago — Trump is expected to meet with lawyers and political advisers on the eve of his arraignment, an aide told CNN.

Trump’s mood was described by an adviser as “defiant and focused,” as he watched expansive television coverage while flying back to his native New York City from Florida.

Special counsel prosecutors have secured evidence including daily notes, texts, emails and photographs and are focused on cataloging how Donald Trump handled classified records around the Mar-a-Lago resort and those who may have witnessed the former president with them, according to multiple sources familiar with the investigation.

The new details come amid signs the Justice Department is taking steps that are typically near the end of an investigation.

The recent investigative activity before a federal grand jury in Washington, DC, also includes subpoenaing witnesses in March and April who had previously spoken to investigators, the sources said. While the FBI interviewed many aides and workers at Mar-a-Lago nearly a year ago voluntarily, grand jury appearances are transcribed and under oath — an indication the prosecutors are locking in witness testimony.

The focus of both the mishandling of records and obstruction of justice probes has remained on the actions of the former president, the sources say. That includes prosecutors pursuing evidence of Trump’s intent to keep classified records after he left the White House, plus his knowledge that the records remained in his possession after the Justice Department demanded their return last May.

Witnesses are being questioned about what they saw in Trump’s private residential and work areas within the club, some of the sources said.

Former President Donald Trump has arrived at his midtown Manhattan property Trump Tower.

He will stay at Trump Tower tonight and is expected to depart New York immediately after Tuesday’s arraignment to head back to Florida — where he plans to deliver remarks at his Mar-a-lago home.

President Joe Biden told CNN’s Betsy Klein he isn’t worried about unrest due to former President Donald Trump’s criminal indictment in New York.

“No, I have faith in the New York Police Department,” Biden said on Monday during a tour of a power generation facility in Fridley, Minnesota.

Asked if he had faith in the legal system, Biden replied: “Yes.”

As former President Donald Trump walked down from his plane at La Guardia Airport on the eve of his arraignment, CNN’s Gloria Borger noted the unique situation he’s in.

Trump is “one of the most famous people in the world, and he is a former president, presidential candidate, and now he’s a defendant. We haven’t seen that. It’s hard to kind of process that,” Borger said to CNN’s Dana Bash.

Borger also highlighted how Trump’s day in court is something he’s avoided for decades and that it will be a grievance he uses to rile up his supporters.

“Donald Trump is somebody who’s probably tried to avoid his day in court for a very long time — decades. And now, you know, you have this man who is saying to people who support him, ‘You know I am your retribution.’ And he is going to make his grievance their grievance. And I think we’re going to see that play out over and over again, and we’ll see it play out in court with his attorney,” she said.

CNN’s Audie Cornish noted how Trump ending up in court seemed unavoidable given the “sheer number of investigations against him.”

Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan said he’ll likely issue a decision tonight on whether media outlets can broadcast former President Donald Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

CNN is part of the media coalition that asked the judge for permission to broadcast the hearing.

More on this: Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

The Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request and said that it was deferring to the judge to make a decision on how best to manage his courtroom.

Donald Trump — the first former president in history to face criminal charges — has arrived in New York for an expected arraignment on Tuesday after being indicted last week by a Manhattan grand jury.

The former president will stay at Trump Tower tonight, according to a source familiar with his plans. But even before Trump’s appearance, his presence will be felt in the Manhattan courthouse Tuesday, as all trials and most other court activity is being halted before he is slated to arrive.

Here is what we know about tomorrow’s events:

CNN’s John Miller, Jeremy Herb, Katelyn Polantz, Tierney Sneed, Sydney Kashiwagi, Kristen Holmes, Holmes Lybrand, Hannah Rabinowitz, Paula Reid, Alayna Treene, Gregory Clary and Devan Cole contributed reporting to this post.

Former President Donald Trump has landed in New York’s La Guardia Airport ahead of his expected Tuesday arraignment in a Manhattan courthouse.

The former president touched down shortly after 3:25 p.m. ET.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, CNN has reported, but the indictment remains under seal. He is the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history.

He will spend Monday night at the Trump Tower, according to a source familiar with his plans.

The Secret Service is scheduled to accompany Trump in the early afternoon Tuesday to the district attorney’s office, which is in the same building as the courthouse, where he is expected to be arraigned.

The former president is expected to return to Florida shortly after his court appearance and has scheduled an event that evening to speak publicly.

Lawyers for former President Donald Trump told a New York judge on Monday that they oppose the request by media outlets to broadcast Trump’s arraignment on Tuesday.

“We submit that the media request should be denied because it will create a circus-like atmosphere at the arraignment, raise unique security concerns, and is inconsistent with President Trump’s presumption of innocence,” the Trump team said in a letter to New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchant.

Trump’s attorneys said in their letter to Acting New York Supreme Court Judge Juan Merchan that he is required under court rules to take into account potential security concerns.

“As Your Honor is well-aware, this case presents extraordinary security concerns (including Secret Service-related concerns) and we submit that any video or photography of the proceedings will only heighten these serious concerns,” they wrote.

Among the Trump lawyers signing the new letter was Todd Blanche, who, CNN reported, recently joined the former president’s legal team.

Meanwhile, the Manhattan District Attorney’s office also responded Monday to the news outlets’ request, and said that it was deferring to the judge to decide how best to manage his court room.

However, the district attorney’s office wrote that it would “be a defensible exercise of the Court’s discretion to exclude or restrict videography, photography, and radio coverage of the arraignment in the interest of avoiding potential prejudice to the defendant, maintaining an orderly proceeding, assuring the safety of the participants in the proceeding, or for other reasons within the Court’s broad authority to manage and control these proceedings.”

But the prosecutors also noted that there “does not appear to be a categorical prohibition on cameras during an arraignment” under existing New York statutes and case law.

While the district attorney’s office stopped short of opposing the request, its lawyer pointed out to Merchan that a similar request for audio-video broadcasting was made for the 2021 arraignment in the tax fraud case against Trump Organization and its CFO Allen Weisselberg.

The judge responded to that 2021 request by “allowing a limited number of still photographs to be taken prior to the commencement of proceedings,” the prosecutors noted Monday.

CNN is one of the outlets requesting camera access for Tuesday’s arraignment.

Former President Donald Trump has hired a new attorney to serve as lead counsel in his defense against charges brought by the Manhattan district attorney’s office, multiple sources tell CNN.

Trump has hired Todd Blanche, who was most recently a partner at law firm Cadwalader, Wickersham & Taft. Blanche has previously represented Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort and Igor Fruman, a former associate of Rudy Giuliani who was also a key figure in Trump’s first impeachment trial. Politico was first to report the news of Blanche’s hiring.

Trump attorney’s Joe Tacopina and Susan Necheles will remain on Trump’s legal team.

Blanche’s hiring has been seen by some inside Trump’s orbit as a sidelining of Tacopina, who up until this point has been one of the most forward-facing attorney’s dealing with Trump’s defense. However, a senior adviser to Trump pushed back on that characterization and said the addition of Blanche was intended to add more legal firepower to Trump’s defense.

When pressed by CNN’s Kaitlan Collins on Friday if Tacopina was the right lawyer in New York to take Trump to trial, another lawyer representing the former president, Tim Parlatore, pointed to a potential conflict of interest.

“I know that Joe has certain potential conflict issues, given his prior contacts, with Stormy Daniels. So, who’s the right attorney, to take it to trial, is something that the client will have to decide. Ultimately, the decision of who to stand next to, before a jury, is a decision that only the client can make,” Parlatore said.

Asked by CNN’s Paula Reid if he is still on the case and whether he will be in court tomorrow, Tacopina said: “Yes of course ! And I will be the lead trial lawyer when the bell rings!”

CNN’s Paula Reid contributed reporting to this post.

Senate Homeland Security Chairman Gary Peters said he has not seen any indication that protests in Washington, DC, over former President Donald Trump’s indictment will become dangerous.

“I think you’re always going to be vigilant, so folks will be vigilant. Security and local law enforcement are certainly monitoring it,” he told reporters Monday.

Peters, who is also a member of Senate Democrat leadership, said it is unclear what effect the indictment will have on the Senate. “We don’t know what’s gonna transpire tomorrow, let alone what’s going to happen in the few weeks and months. I think we’d have to take a look at what the indictment is all about.”

Former President Donald Trump has departed Florida and is en route to New York to face criminal charges.

His personalized Boeing 757 took off from the Palm Beach International Airport this afternoon at about 12:59 p.m. ET ahead of his Tuesday appearance in a Manhattan courtroom to be arraigned.

Trump, the first current or former president to face criminal charges in American history, is expected to land at New York’s LaGuardia airport around 3 p.m. ET, according to a source familiar with his plans. He will stay at Trump Tower tonight, the source told CNN.

Trump faces more than 30 counts related to business fraud, according to two sources familiar with the case. A New York grand jury handed down the sealed indictment late last week. The charges have yet to be made public.

The former president is not expected to stay in New York City for very long. Immediately after his Tuesday court appearance, Trump plans to return to Florida, the source said, where he has scheduled an event to speak publicly.

CNN’s Alta D. Spells and Jack Forrest contributed reporting to this post.

New York City Mayor Eric Adams warned GOP Rep. Marjorie Taylor Greene to “be on your best behavior” after the Republican firebrand said she will travel to the city to protest former President Donald Trump’s indictment.

Adams made the statement at a press conference as New York security officials detailed plans ahead of Trump’s arrival in Manhattan for his arraignment scheduled on Tuesday.

“While there may be some rabble rousers thinking about coming to our city tomorrow, our message is clear and simple: Control yourselves. New York City is our home, not a playground for your misplaced anger. We have the safest large city in America because we respect the rule of law in New York City. And although we have no specific threats, people like Marjorie Taylor Greene, who is known to spread misinformation and hate speech — she stated she is coming to town. While you’re in town, be on your best behavior,” Adams said.

“As always, we will not allow violence or vandalism of any kind. And if one is caught participating in any act of violence, they will be arrested and held accountable. No matter who you are,” he added.
